# Fogell — Execution Board

Mission: **a faster and better Jenkins.** Near-100% compatibility is the goal;
`adr/0001` defines what "compatible" is permitted to mean, and `adr/0004`
forbids claiming it without a receipt.

Lineage: front end inspired by **Forge** (same Groovy-parser approach,
`adr/0002`); architecture and evidence discipline inspired by **McLoving**
(`adr/0007`, `adr/0008`). One primary language, F# (`adr/0006`).

## Operating contract

- **HeMan is the engineering bastion**, with the canonical checkout at
  `$HOME/projects/fogell`. Investigation, edits, builds, tests, local
  Qwen-assisted inspection, corpus work and orchestration of the pinned Jenkins
  oracle happen from HeMan.
- **GitHub is the publication, final-review, gating and merge boundary.** It is
  not the normal edit-test loop and it is not an oracle substitute. Its hosted
  gate cannot see the mounted corpus or the pinned Jenkins controller, so a green
  GitHub check never replaces the full pre-publication run on HeMan.
- One coherent commit per ticket. No work on a dirty checkout.
- Every ticket states a falsifiable acceptance criterion. "Done" means the
  criterion was measured, not that code was written.
- Corpus manifest verified before any scoring run (`corpus/README.md`).
- No push, tag, release or publication without owner authorization.
- **`main` IS BRANCH-PROTECTED**, with `gate` as a REQUIRED status check, `strict` on so the check must have run against current `main`, and force-pushes and deletions refused. `enforce_admins` is deliberately OFF so the owner keeps an override. All of that is readable from the protection endpoint and is the part a reader can re-derive. **THE HISTORY HAS A RECEIPT, AND FINDING IT TOOK TWO WRONG VERSIONS OF THIS SENTENCE.** The org audit log holds exactly one `protected_branch.create` for this repository — `main`, at `2026-08-15T14:32:30Z` — with a matching `required_status_check.create` in the same second and NO other `protected_branch` event of any kind. The repository was created `2026-07-30`, sixteen days before, which is inside the retention window, so that absence is evidence rather than a gap. `main` was therefore unprotected for the whole of its history until that moment, and the gate was advisory for every merge before it. THE TWO WRONG VERSIONS ARE KEPT BECAUSE THEY FAILED IN OPPOSITE DIRECTIONS: the first asserted the history with no receipt at all, and the second WITHDREW a true claim after I reported that no audit read could confirm it — I had not queried the audit log, and said it was empty. Overclaiming and overcorrecting are the same defect, which is describing the evidence instead of fetching it. Query: `gh api 'orgs/SuperBadLabs/audit-log?phrase=repo:SuperBadLabs/Fogell+action:protected_branch'`. **PROVEN TO FAIL ON 2026-08-15, AND THE FG-165 FLAKE PAID FOR THE PROOF.** This line first said the protection was UNFALSIFIED and that proving it would need a throwaway PR failing the gate on purpose. It needed no such thing: PR #61 — the very PR carrying this line — had one of its two `gate` jobs fail with `the daemon never started`, and GitHub reported the PR `BLOCKED` while the other job was green. Before today that same shape merged: on PR #58 an identical red job sat beside a green one and the PR read `CLEAN`. So the protection is not merely configured, it has been OBSERVED refusing a merge on a red required check, which is the one thing a required check exists to do. Its converse is still unproven — that it ADMITS every merge it should — and nothing here claims otherwise.
- Imported Jenkinsfiles are untrusted. Parsing is unconditional; **execution is
  allowlisted by executed surface**, on a no-egress network, in a disposable
  workspace.
- **`tier1=1` OF 228 — THE PATH WAS WALKED ONCE ON 2026-08-17 (FG-200) AND IS OPEN.**
  (This bullet's header read "tier1=0 ... OPEN AND UNWALKED" until that day.)
  A tier-1 receipt requires RUNNING a job on both engines, and the corpus scorer only ever
  parses, so no corpus file can reach tier 1 through scoring alone. The route is nonetheless
  BUILT and deliberately kept open: `generate-scorecard.bb` carries an explicit filter
  exemption so that a receipt naming a corpus file counts as tier-1 evidence, with a comment
  recording that an earlier orphan filter closed that path and had to be fixed. FG-090 exists
  to open it. What makes it expensive is the untrusted-input rule above: a corpus file may be
  executed, but only allowlisted by executed surface, no-egress, in a disposable workspace —
  a bar cleared per file, not a prohibition.
  **THIS ENTRY FIRST CLAIMED THE OPPOSITE AND IS CORRECTED IN THE SAME PASS.** It said
  "tier1=0" was a POLICY FLOOR that could not move by engine work while the rules stood, and
  that an outside review calling for corpus-to-receipt coverage had mistaken a decision for a
  gap. Both halves were wrong: the rule allowlists execution rather than forbidding it, and
  the machinery is built to accept exactly the receipts I said could not exist. THE REVIEW WAS
  RIGHT AND MY CORRECTION OF IT WAS NOT. What remains true, and is the only part worth
  keeping, is that the number does not move as a side effect of engine work: someone has to
  allowlist a corpus file and run it. Files that only parse are not execution evidence,
  and the hand-written suite is a different population from the jobs this engine replaces.
- Incomplete pattern matches fail the build (FS0025/FS0026).
- No scalar compatibility percentage is ever published.
- **A REDIRECT'S STATUS FOLLOWS ITS TARGET.** Four states only — DONE, TODO,
  PARTIAL, BLOCKED — with "MOVED to", "SUPERSEDED by" and "Retired by" leading
  the detail rather than hiding in the status cell. A row that points elsewhere
  is DONE only if the row it points AT is: FG-002e supersedes into FG-101 (done,
  so DONE), while FG-038b moves into FG-113 (not built, so TODO). Collapsing the
  vocabulary marked that one DONE and would have read as licensing a
  platform-parity claim for a lane nobody has written.
- **ONE PLACE PER LIVE FACT; EVERYWHERE ELSE POINTS AT IT.** A statement about what
  the engine does TODAY belongs in exactly one location, and the receipt is that location
  wherever one exists — it is regenerated from a run, so it cannot drift from the run.
  Earned on 2026-08-13, when a single retracted claim had to be corrected in FIVE places
  (a source comment, a case header, a queue row, a ticket row, and a second ticket's row)
  and the verifier caught each copy separately over four rounds. The failure was never the
  claim; it was that one fact had five authors and I only ever edited the copies I was
  already looking at. Concretely: a CASE HEADER explains what that case asserts and why it
  is shaped that way — never the status of another ticket, and never an INSTRUCTION for a
  future commit. A "drop these semicolons when FG-187 lands" note went stale in the very
  commit that landed FG-187 and dropped them: an instruction asserts the work is still
  outstanding, so it has a built-in expiry, and the person best placed to forget it is
  whoever does the work. A TICKET ROW carries history,
  so a superseded statement stays and is marked RETRACTED rather than deleted. A QUEUE ROW
  gives ranking only. If a fact is worth stating twice, state it once and cite it.
  SOURCE COMMENTS AND ACCEPTANCE FIELDS ARE COVERED TOO, and they were the two the rule
  had to be extended to reach: a comment listing the findings that motivated a change
  contradicted itself the moment one of them was reattributed to another ticket, and it
  had no business carrying that list — a comment owns the MECHANISM it sits on and cites
  the receipts. The tell is grammatical: a sentence about what the code DOES is durable, a
  sentence about what is STILL BROKEN elsewhere is a status claim wearing a comment's
  clothes.
- **A CHECKER MUST BE PROVEN TO FAIL.** Before trusting a lane scenario, a
  verifier, a monitor or an audit script, run it against a KNOWN-BAD state and
  confirm it says so; three runs if it has timing in it, because one execution
  proves nothing about a race. Earned on 2026-08-02, when four checkers each
  returned a confident wrong answer: lane scenario V had three separate
  false-pass routes, the pre-push verifier read a missing verdict as approval and
  then invented one by matching its own prompt echo, and a PR monitor inherited
  its repository from the working directory and reported a different repo's PR as
  merged. Two were caught by luck. Corollaries: a checker must NAME ITS TARGET
  rather than inherit it from ambient context, and a fixture meant to be unstable
  must have EVERY observable state valid — "alternate good and bad" is a second
  way to pass.
- **Robust beats brittle.** Where the choice is between machinery that is harder
  to hold in the head and a shape that reads more easily but can fail silently,
  take the machinery and pay for it with comments explaining why each part
  exists. Simplification that costs a guarantee is not an improvement.
- **Seal evidence LAST**, after the final review round. A bundle sealed mid-review attests
  to a snapshot that was never merged: FG-101's first seal recorded the pre-review
  implementation, and FG-104's omitted its own deliverable. The checksum makes a stale
  bundle look more trustworthy than no bundle at all.

### Execution cycle and definition of done

The daily work loop starts and ends on HeMan:

1. Sync `main`, confirm a clean checkout, and create the ticket branch on HeMan.
2. Inspect and edit there. Use the local Qwen agent as another pair of eyes when
   useful, while treating its output as review input rather than evidence.
3. Run the narrow tests while iterating, then the ticket's acceptance measurement.
   Differential work is launched from HeMan against Luigi's pinned `jenkins-lab`;
   corpus work uses HeMan's mounted, hash-pinned corpus.
4. Inspect the complete diff and evidence, then run `scripts/build-and-test.sh` on
   HeMan. A run that skips the corpus-dependent scorecard check is not the full
   local gate and does not license publication.
5. Publish the already-proven commit to GitHub and obtain final review of that
   exact head. A finding returns the work to HeMan. For a Codex finding, prove the
   correction there, push it, and request `@codex review`. Copilot cannot be
   re-requested; for a Copilot finding that changes the head, close the unmerged PR
   and open the proved correction as a replacement PR so its open event obtains a
   new Copilot review. Repeat that replacement if Copilot finds another issue. A
   review of an earlier SHA does not carry forward.
6. Run `scripts/review-coverage.py --pr N` from HeMan after the review record
   exists and before merge. It names the current full head and refuses until
   every expected reviewer covered it. The no-argument repository-wide audit
   remains historical evidence and is not a publication guard.
7. Use GitHub for required status checks and merge only the exact head that the
   HeMan gate and final review covered.

A ticket is done only when all of the following are true:

- its falsifiable acceptance criterion was measured in the environment it names;
- the relevant unit, integration, restart, approval, differential and/or corpus
  checks passed on HeMan, including every corpus-dependent check in the full gate;
- every compatibility claim is backed by a sealed receipt from the pinned oracle,
  or is explicitly labelled unproven;
- the final diff was reviewed as one coherent ticket change and the checkout is
  clean after its commit; and
- GitHub's final review and required gate apply to the exact commit being merged.

GitHub proves the published commit passed the checks available there. HeMan proves
the commit was ready to publish. Neither statement is a substitute for the other.

### Batch composition (measured, not assumed)

Derived from 117 review findings across PRs #11–#17, counted rather than recalled:

| what the PR touched | review rounds |
|---|---:|
| leaf concerns — process groups, signals, one step (#11, #12) | **2** |
| the walker, `Fogell.Differential/Fogell.fs` (#13–#17) | **5–14** |

**55% of all review COMMENTS (76 of 138) landed in that one file.** (138 comments carry
117 distinct findings; the two counts are not interchangeable and are kept separate here.) Not because it
is badly written, but because it is where the cross-cutting invariants live, and every
new step rediscovers them one at a time.

Rules that follow from the numbers:

1. **A ticket that adds behaviour to the walker goes in its own PR.** Batching three of
   them multiplies rounds instead of amortising them: #13 carried three tickets and cost
   5 rounds, then spilled 9 unread findings into #14, which cost 14 more.
2. **Leaf tickets batch 3–5.** They did, at 2 rounds each.
3. **Measure Jenkins BEFORE writing the ticket's code.** Every ticket that began with a
   probe (FG-035, FG-045, FG-048b, FG-046) matched on the first or second receipt for the
   behaviour itself; every round beyond that came from invariants, not from Jenkins.
4. **A cross-cutting invariant is not a ticket line, it is a sweep with a test.** Fixing
   instances demonstrably does not stop recurrence — see the counts in Wave 3.5.

> `src`, `tools` and `tests` NAME a receipt, never that the receipt proves the claim.
>
> For a HAND-TYPED number here: treat it as a claim awaiting a generator, and never

> This warning said the scorecard was hand-maintained and that FG-090/091/092 existed

## Execution order (revised after 7 merged PRs; re-anchored after 52)

The waves below are grouped by SUBSYSTEM, which is how the work was originally
conceived. Execution order is now different, because the measured cost of a ticket
turned out to depend on what it TOUCHES rather than which subsystem it belongs to.

    1.  Wave 3.5   invariants        one-time cost, removes a recurring one   DONE
    2.  Wave 3.6   harness capability unblocks 5 tickets incl. the largest one DONE except FG-113
    3.  Wave 4     step coverage      now leaf work, batchable 3–5 per PR
    4.  Wave 9     script/interpreter where the last 21 tickets actually landed
    5.  Waves 5–8  as written

**THIS ORDERING IS NOW A WAVE-LEVEL STATEMENT ONLY, and it has been overtaken.** Steps
1 and 2 are discharged, and step 3 stopped describing the work when `script { }` turned
step coverage into interpreter coverage — a subsystem that had no wave until one was
made for it below. Wave order does not resolve which open P1 goes next, and
that question is now answered by defect class, in the section that follows. Read that
section as the execution cycle's reference point; read this one for how the waves came
to be sequenced.

**Why invariants first.** 117 review findings across PRs #11–#17 cluster into five
cross-cutting classes (54 cancellation, 52 string/quoting, 40 abort-cause, 19
fail-closed, 17 narration). Each new step in the walker rediscovered them
independently — abort-cause was wrong six times, poll coverage five, narration five.
Fixing instances did not stop recurrence; three times the reviewer noticed the
repetition before I did. Wave 3.5 converts that into a one-time cost.

**Why harness second.** Five tickets are blocked on the same limitation — the harness
produces one build, of a plain job, on Linux, with no SCM. One of them is FG-052,
which a fresh count shows is the **largest remaining construct in the corpus** (49
files) and which the board had recorded as 15 and rated P2.

**What this reordering is not.** It does not add scope or reprioritise on taste. Every
number above is counted from the corpus or from review history, and the two
corrections it produced — FG-052's 3x undercount, and `docker`'s 70 raw hits being 13
real directives — are the kind of error that only shows up when you re-measure instead
of trusting the row.

## Live queue — the reference point for the execution cycle

**BOARD ACCOUNTING (derived): rows=209; DONE=130; open=79; open P0–P3=4 / 27 / 36 / 12.**

**WHY THIS SECTION EXISTS.** The derived accounting above is the single live source for
ticket totals and priority distribution. (Was "187 rows … open 84 … 4 / 34 / 36 / 10"
until 2026-08-14, and 189 / 86 / 4-36-36-10 for the length of that same pass — FG-198 is the row it filed against itself, and it is the ONLY row added — the rest of the movement is a recount, not new work. NOTHING REGRESSED: two ids each covered two
different tickets, so a count of unique ids reported 187 while 189 rows existed, and the
two hidden ones were both open P1. The renumber to FG-196/FG-197 did not create work, it
made work that was already on the board countable — which is the argument for deriving
these numbers instead of maintaining them, since the discrepancy was only ever visible as
189 rows against 187 unique ids.) A priority tier with many open items does not
order anything: FG-046b's silently skipped human approval gate and a closure's capture
semantics were both P1, and the practical effect was that whichever ticket the last
review round happened to touch went next. The waves below group by SUBSYSTEM, which is
how the work was conceived and is no longer where it happens — twenty-one tickets had
accreted into **Wave 8 — Release gates** because that table was last in the file.

**A QUEUE ROW GIVES THE RANKING ARGUMENT, NOT THE DIAGNOSIS.** Learned twice, the same way both times: a ticket's row and its queue row each described the defect, the ticket row was corrected, and the queue row kept the disproven version — FG-183's "a placement walk at admission, no interpreter change" and FG-180's two named constructs. Duplicated prose drifts, and the copy nobody edits is the one a reader meets first, at the top of the file. THE LINE, stated precisely enough to apply: a queue row may give the measured SYMPTOM — what each engine did — because that is a fact about a run and does not change when the code does. It must not name the CAUSE or predict the FIX, because both are hypotheses, and a hypothesis in the ranking table outlives its correction in the ticket. **THE TEST, AND THE OVER-GENERALISATION THAT NEARLY REPLACED IT.** The usable test is: **COULD THIS SENTENCE BECOME FALSE IF THE ROWS WERE REORDERED OR RE-RANKED, WITHOUT ANY CODE CHANGING?** If yes, the `#` column or another row already owns the fact. It catches what a deny-list kept missing — `head of the B block`, `Follows FG-195`, `this position is provisional`, `every other open row here was measured on a hand-written case`, `every class-A and class-B ticket is measured through a receipt` — the last two false the moment this branch ranked an unmeasured row, and one of them written into FG-114 by the patch removing a positional claim from it. **A STRICTER RULE WAS WRITTEN HERE ON 2026-08-15 AND IS RETRACTED IN THE SAME PASS: "a row may only make claims about its own ticket".** It is not satisfiable by a RANKING table, whose rows exist to be compared, and it condemned prose that is doing its job — FG-186 + FG-176's "PAIRED because both tickets argue they need the same one distinction" is a cross-row claim, it is the entire ranking argument for that row, and it stays true however the table is ordered. Nine review rounds went into escalating a narrow real defect into an absolute, then finding every row in violation of it. THE LESSON IS THE ONE FG-183 ALREADY CARRIES, arriving from the other direction: an estimate can be confidently too SMALL, and a rule can be confidently too BIG. What survives is the reorder test, which is falsifiable, and the original prohibition on naming a CAUSE or predicting a FIX. If yes it is a positional claim and the `#` column already owns it. That test passes words a deny-list keeps missing and fails phrasings a deny-list keeps flagging — `ranked on its row's argument`, `ranked as the gate`, `its ranking cost` all survive a reorder and are fine; `this position is provisional`, `every ticket above`, `head of the B block`, `Follows FG-195` do not. Every round of this on 2026-08-15 added one more word to a list; the test is what should have been written first. Every row that drifted did one of those two. **THE FIRST VERSION OF THIS PARAGRAPH THEN CLAIMED THE OTHER ROWS ALREADY COMPLIED, AND FOUR DID NOT** — FG-179 named its fix, FG-135 its cause, FG-186 the distinction it needs, FG-177 a schema. Writing a rule and asserting compliance in the same breath, without checking row by row, is the sixth instance of the exact class the rule was written to stop, committed in the act of stopping it. The rows are now swept: each gives a measured symptom and a ranking argument, and where a proposed fix IS the ranking argument — two tickets pairing because they share one — it is attributed to the ticket rather than asserted here. The same reasoning that produced `SSwitch` rather than a third guard: remove the duplication, not the instance. **THE SEVENTH INSTANCE WAS THE RANKING ITSELF, NOT A DIAGNOSIS**, found on 2026-08-14: FG-179's row still read "RANKED HERE because it is the blocker" a cycle after its ticket row was retitled to say it is BLOCKED, behind a ticket that had no queue row at all. So the rule generalises — a queue row's RANKING ARGUMENT goes stale exactly the way a diagnosis does, and it is the harder one to catch, because a reader who checks the ticket finds the diagnosis corrected and has no reason to re-read the ordering. The four rows added in that pass were checked against this paragraph one at a time rather than as a batch, which is the part the sixth instance skipped. **AND THAT WAS STILL NOT ENOUGH — THE VERIFIER FOUND THREE MORE IN TWO ROUNDS**, all of one previously unnamed sub-shape: a predicted SCOPE. FG-180 + FG-141 said the work "needs no interpreter change", FG-193 said an unrun construction "if real is class A", FG-196 said the defect "fires on any step that runs longer than two minutes". None names a cause and none names a fix, so each passed a row-by-row reading against the rule as it was then written, and all three are the same overclaim: a sentence about how BIG the thing is, asserted from a measurement that did not size it. **THE RULE THEREFORE COVERS SCOPE, SEVERITY AND BLAST RADIUS**, and the test is not "did I name the cause" but "could this sentence be wrong without the receipt being wrong". All 18 rows were then swept with a written checker rather than by reading — proven to catch three planted rows and to stay silent on a quoted retraction, since bare apostrophes in `FG-183's` defeat naive quote-pairing and the first version flagged a retraction as a live claim. Wiring that checker into the gate is **FG-198**; until it lands this sweep is a one-off and the rule is still enforced by whoever is looking.

**The P column is left exactly as filed.** Each row's priority carries that row's own
severity argument and re-typing them here would add no evidence. What orders
execution is the CLASS and the queue below.

### Defect class (the ranking rule)

From `adr/0001`, not from taste. The ADR fixes one ordering — a false success is worse
than an explicit rejection — and every class here is a position relative to that line.

| class | what it means | why it ranks there |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | **False success.** Fogell reports success, or a VALUE, that differs from what Jenkins produced | The ADR's worst outcome. A green build that skipped work, or a variable holding the wrong bytes, is the failure mode the whole evidence discipline exists to prevent |
| **B** | **False refusal.** Fogell rejects or fails what Jenkins runs | Conservative and loud. It costs corpus admission and it costs users, but nobody ships on a wrong answer |
| **C** | **Unproven guarantee.** A rule exists and no checker has been shown to fail; or the evidence cannot be produced at all | A guarantee nobody has falsified is a claim. This is where a checker is written, not a fix |
| **D** | **Capability gap.** Honest, absent, named | Costs coverage, misleads nobody |
| **E** | **Hygiene.** Prose, naming, structure | Real, cheap, never ahead of the above |

A ticket's class is a MEASUREMENT, not a label: A and B both require a probe naming what
each engine did. A ticket that has not been probed is not yet classifiable, and saying
so is the point — two tickets have been re-ranked by measuring them, and in OPPOSITE
directions: FG-140 down from P1 once the symptom stopped being silent, FG-181 up from
the P2 its reviewer assigned once the receipt showed a green build carrying a wrong
value. A rule that only ever ranks things up is a rule nobody is applying.

### The duplication axis — added 2026-08-17, and it outranks the tracks

**THE THREE TRACKS BELOW ASK HOW BAD EACH DEFECT IS. NONE OF THEM ASKS WHETHER THIS ENGINE
SHOULD BE THE ONE TO FIX IT.** McLoving — a sibling engine in a repository owned by the same
person — has a large, code-cited platform: mTLS agents, fenced terminal publication, forced RLS
tenancy, a 22-subcommand CLI, a served UI, artifact download, five trigger kinds, a secret
broker, signed releases, a real Win32 executor
([MCLOVING-SOURCE-SURVEY.md](MCLOVING-SOURCE-SURVEY.md), 30 crates). Several Fogell rows look
like they cover the same ground.

**A TABLE NAMING ELEVEN SPECIFIC DUPLICATED TICKETS WAS WRITTEN HERE ON 2026-08-17 AND IS
WITHDRAWN. REVIEW REFUTED SIX OF THE ELEVEN, AND THE SIXTH ONE SETTLED IT: THE METHOD WAS WRONG,
NOT THE ENTRIES.** The table was built by matching McLoving capability names against Fogell
ticket TITLES. It never read the tickets' ACCEPTANCE FIELDS, which is where each one says what
would actually discharge it — and every refutation came from there:

| claimed duplicate | why it is not |
|---|---|
| `FG-113` Windows | it is a DIFFERENTIAL lane — acceptance needs Fogell and Jenkins on one Windows host to license a parity claim |
| `FG-070b` secret isolation | acceptance is same-UID isolation, proving `/proc/<pid>/environ` unreadable; a grant/redeem broker with a TTL is a different threat model |
| `FG-026` effect ledger | acceptance is a FOUR-STATE external-effect checkpoint ledger, not fenced attempt publication |
| `FG-063` agent death | acceptance requires announcing a bounded reconnect window, not process-identity revalidation |
| `FG-053c` triggers | it is Jenkinsfile FRONT-END work — the declarative `triggers` block — not platform trigger ingress |
| `FG-080` packaging | acceptance is REPRODUCIBILITY evidence, byte-identical output; signed releases and an SBOM are not that |

**THE GENERAL RULE, WHICH IS THE ONLY DURABLE THING THIS PASS PRODUCED: A SIBLING ENGINE HAVING A
CAPABILITY DOES NOT SATISFY A TICKET WHOSE ACCEPTANCE IS ENGINE-SPECIFIC.** Most of this board's
platform rows demand evidence ABOUT FOGELL — a differential against Jenkins, a named threat
model discharged, a byte-identical rebuild. No amount of working code in another engine
discharges that.

**WHAT SURVIVES, AND IT IS A QUESTION RATHER THAN AN ANSWER.** The axis is worth having: for any
open platform row, ask whether Fogell should be the engine that builds it, given that a sibling
already has something adjacent. That is a real question and this board could not previously ask
it. What it CANNOT do is answer the question per row from capability names. **Doing that properly
means reading each row's acceptance field against McLoving's code, one row at a time — work this
pass did not do, and whose absence produced a table that was 55% wrong.**

**SO THE FIRST QUESTION ABOUT AN OPEN ROW IS NOT ITS CLASS, IT IS WHICH BUCKET IT SITS IN.**

| bucket | meaning | what a row needs |
|---|---|---|
| **UNIQUE** | only Fogell can do it | a priority — the tracks below apply normally |
| **DUPLICATED** | acceptance demonstrably dischargeable by McLoving's existing work — **established per row by reading its acceptance field, NOT by capability name. No row currently carries this tag** | **a DECISION: build, borrow, or drop.** A priority is the wrong output; ranking it against other Fogell work presumes the answer |
| **DISTINCT** | platform work whose acceptance McLoving's existing work does NOT discharge — `FG-026`'s four-state effect ledger is the worked example, and it is the bucket the first draft of this table had no room for | a priority — it is Fogell's to build |
| **NEITHER** | Fogell-specific, not platform | a priority, as today |

**UNIQUE is the whole justification for Fogell existing separately**, and it is one thing:
the breadth of Groovy it executes at runtime. The generated scorecard owns the current hand-written case-receipt count (a population authored FOR this engine, and NOT the
corpus tier-1 count, which is `tier1=1`); `admitted=199` of 228 corpus files parse, across declarative and scripted forms, parallel branches, conditionals, and 14 modelled steps.
Against that, McLoving's compiler admits ONE job and its step catalogue holds ONE mapping, and
it provably never evaluates Groovy
(`compat/jenkins-worker/src/mcloving/compat/compiler.clj:133` stops at `CompilePhase/CONVERSION`).
Nothing in its source suggests that breadth is close to being matched. FG-195, FG-179,
FG-014/FG-015 and FG-177 are all UNIQUE and all remain ranked as the tracks say.

**A CONSEQUENCE FOR TRACK 2 WAS CLAIMED HERE AND IS WITHDRAWN.** This section argued that
because McLoving holds a corpus receipt (`corpus-052`, certified against Jenkins 2.568.1, 1 of
228) while Fogell was then at "tier1=0", FG-200's question changes from "can this engine get a corpus
receipt" to "why are two engines racing for the same receipt". **Review refuted it and the
refutation is simple: A RECEIPT CERTIFIES THE ENGINE THAT PRODUCED IT.** McLoving's result says
nothing about Fogell's compatibility and does not answer FG-200, whose stated question is the
COST of executing a real corpus file through Fogell. FG-200 stands exactly as written, and
Track 2 stays binding for the reason it already gave. The comparison remains interesting to an
owner deciding where to spend; it is not a fact about Fogell's evidence.

**WHAT THIS SECTION DELIBERATELY DOES NOT DO.**

- **It does not close, drop or re-rank any row.** Every ticket above stays exactly where it is
  with the priority it had. The bucket is a SECOND fact about a row, not a replacement for its
  class, and only the owner can convert a DUPLICATED row into a decision.
- **It does not claim the eleven are equivalent in quality.** McLoving having a crate is not
  McLoving having a working, proven feature; its own `DYNAMIC_PROVISIONER_V1.md` claims no
  certified production provider, and none of its platform work has a differential receipt.
- **NO ROW CARRIES A BUCKET.** The withdrawn table named eleven; nothing replaced it, so all
  open rows are untagged (the derived accounting above owns their count). An earlier draft of this line said "eleven named, 78 untagged" and
  kept both figures after the table that produced them was withdrawn — stale in the same pass
  that withdrew it. Tagging is the remaining work and it is per-row acceptance reading, not
  inference. THAT READING NOW EXISTS AS RECOMMENDATIONS — [DUPLICATION-AXIS-RECOMMENDATIONS.md](DUPLICATION-AXIS-RECOMMENDATIONS.md) reads the 26 platform-adjacent rows selected for that snapshot by acceptance field (2 UNIQUE, 1 provisional DUPLICATED, 17 DISTINCT, 6 NEITHER, each with its survey citation and depth). Still no tag is applied on this board: a recommendation is not a decision, and conversion remains the owner's.
- **The evidence has a stated depth limit.** The survey read ~15 crates at header depth and
  grepped the spine; eight cells are code-cited, and a few rows above lean on cells that were
  confirmed rather than deeply read. **Before dropping a P0 on the strength of "McLoving has
  it", read that crate properly.** Every pass over this comparison found the previous pass
  wrong — runbook, then architecture docs, then code, then the code sweep itself.

### The three tracks, and which one is binding

**THE CLASS SYSTEM RANKS DEFECTS, AND TWO KINDS OF OPEN WORK ARE NOT DEFECTS.** `adr/0001`'s
A-E ordering answers "is the engine correct", which was the right question while correctness
was the thing in doubt. Two things on this board have no class and therefore no rank:

- **"tier1=0" of 228** (so it stood until 2026-08-17; the ledger now derives `tier1=1`)**.** Not a false success, a false refusal, or a capability gap — the
  engine does not do anything wrong here. Nothing owned it, so it appeared in no queue while
  being the number that decides what every other number is worth.
- **FG-198 and FG-199.** Both P1, both about guarantees enforced by whoever is looking rather
  than by a checker. Neither is a thing the engine does; both are things the process does not.

So the queue is split into three tracks. This is a change of FRAME, not of priority: every
correctness row keeps the rank its class argument earned, and Track 1 below is the same
ordered list it was.

| track | what it ranks | ordered by |
|---|---|---|
| **1 — Correctness** | what the engine does that Jenkins does not | defect class, `adr/0001` |
| **2 — Evidence** | what is PROVEN about the engine against real jobs | how much of the corpus a claim can cover |
| **3 — Process** | guarantees currently held by attention rather than by a checker | how quietly the guarantee fails |

**TRACK 2 IS BINDING AS OF 2026-08-15, AND THE ARGUMENT IS A DENOMINATOR.** The hand-written
suite is 187 of 187 proven (one case diverged for four days as FG-201, found and fixed 2026-08-17) and the corpus is `tier1=1` of 228: all but one receipt this engine has
proves a case written for it. That is not a criticism of the suite, which found and fixed
real class-A defects all month — it is that a synthetic population cannot answer "does this
run the jobs it is meant to replace", and no amount of Track 1 work changes the denominator.
An outside review reached the same conclusion independently on 2026-08-15 and ranked
corpus-to-receipt coverage second behind the P1 defects, which is this ordering.

**WHAT THIS DOES NOT SAY.** It does not say Track 1 waits. The P1 false-success rows are
still the first work, for the reason `adr/0001` gives and because a bridge built over a
wrong answer proves the wrong answer. It does not say Track 2 is blocked: the tier-1 path
for a corpus receipt is BUILT and open — see the operating contract — and unwalked. And it
does not rank the tracks against each other row by row, because nobody has measured what a
first corpus receipt costs; **FG-200 exists to find that out, and until it reports, the
claim that Track 2 is binding is an argument from denominators and not a measurement.**

**HOW TO READ THE NEXT ACTION.** Track 3's FG-199 is a local post-publication,
pre-merge guardrail, not work to perform in GitHub. Its `--pr N` checker runs on
HeMan and queries GitHub review metadata only after the candidate is published
and its head and review record exist. The blocking gate runs its offline
known-bad proof without GitHub credentials or network; the live guard itself
runs only at the review-to-merge boundary. The no-argument historical audit is
evidence of the backlog, not a required merge guard. In the implementation queue, the
first active ticket is FG-177; its branch, edit-test loop and evidence stay on
HeMan until its commit is ready to publish.

**FG-015 exact-head index-boundary addendum.** Direct Jenkins 2.568.1
measurement proved an `EIndex` result is an ordinary receiver boundary for an
outer property, safe-property, or method-result write; the selected source map
mutates and the two focused cases add tier-1 receipts. A direct projected-index
l-value is different: simple/compound/inc/dec writes change only the temporary
projection, while another index can reach a referenced source list and persist.
That distinction is now closed by FG-015b: the interpreter decides from runtime
list identity. Replacing a slot in the fresh projected list stays temporary;
selecting a referenced source list through another index or a method result
persists. Direct projected indexes are no longer statically refused, while an
actual spread-property l-value retains `unsupported_spread_assignment`.
Evidence: [spread-index boundary](../evidence/20260821T020910Z-fg-015-spread-index-boundary)
and [FG-015b closure](../evidence/20260821T083100Z-fg-015b-list-index).

**FG-015 method-result write addendum.** Review probes on Jenkins 2.568.1
measured the receiver boundary rather than inferring it: `rows*.child.first()?.name`
mutates the returned non-null map; a null receiver raises a catchable
`NullPointerException`; a scalar raises a catchable `MissingPropertyException`;
and receiver effects precede RHS effects before either fault. Fogell now routes
every `ESafeProp` assignment through the ordinary map writer, with a distinct
typed null fault, instead of evaluating the RHS and reporting success without a
write. Syntactically invalid assignment targets still refuse explicitly before
the RHS; typed integer list-index writes are now the measured FG-015b path.

The same probe proved `rows*.children.first()[0] = value` persistently mutates
the selected source list, a null receiver throws catchably, and the statically
indistinguishable map spelling also mutates. FG-015b now makes that decision at
runtime: a list receiver accepts only an integer index with Groovy's measured
mutation rules, and a map receiver keeps the existing map-key writer. The former
conservative map over-refusal and `unsupported_spread_index_assignment` gate are
retired. Outer
property/safe-property writes on call or index results, free-call arguments,
method arguments, trailing closures, and spread reads used as index keys remain
admitted. Evidence: [method-result write boundary](../evidence/20260821T025710Z-fg-015-call-write-boundary)
and [FG-015b closure](../evidence/20260821T083100Z-fg-015b-list-index).

A migration whose text changed after being applied fails loudly rather than diverging silently |
| FG-021 | P0 | **DONE** | Atomic admission: build + node + attempt + event + outbox in one transaction, idempotency-keyed | Replay returns the original ids and emits no second event/outbox row. **16 concurrent submissions of one key yield exactly one build** — arbitrated by a unique constraint, not check-then-insert. Composite FKs make cross-tenant parent substitution unrepresentable |
| FG-022 | P0 | **DONE** | Attempt fences + lease owner + expiry using `clock_timestamp()`, not `now()` | **16 concurrent publishers → exactly 1 winner, exactly 1 terminal event.** Refused: stale fence, wrong owner, expired lease, double publication. Each offer increments the fence |
| FG-023 | P0 | **DONE** | Controller restore epoch: restore invalidates every active lease; pre-restore agents cannot publish | `ActivateRestore()` bumps the epoch and moves live attempts to `reconciliation_required`; a pre-restore holder's publication is refused |
| FG-024 | P0 | **DONE** | Append-only per-step journal with a selectable fsync policy (`adr/0003`) | Append-only so a torn write can only truncate the tail; a partial final line is recovered-up-to rather than trusted or fatal. Measured **on HeMan** (fsync ~0.7 ms): `EveryStep` **0.859 ms/step** (2 syncs — started *and* finished), `EveryStage` **0.005 ms/step**. Cross-host comparison against the luigi Jenkins figure is invalid; the host-independent claim is **~2 syncs/step vs Jenkins' ~6.9** |
| FG-025 | P0 | **DONE** | **Exactly-once resume**: replay from the last durable step | Proven with a **real SIGKILL** of a separate process mid-stage. Marker file shows `step-0` executed **exactly once** across the crash; the interrupted `step-1` is reported for reconciliation and **never re-run** (re-running is at-least-once, which ADR 0003 rejects); the unreached `step-2` runs. Resuming a finished build is a no-op |

| FG-027 | P1 | **PARTIAL** | Retry semantics: never rewrite an attempt; one child attempt with immutable link; exhausted budget → dead-letter | Domain and Store foundations now replay the exact child or dead-letter decision. Runtime retryability policy and scheduler/controller invocation remain, so parent completion is not claimed |
| FG-027a | P1 | **PARTIAL** | Pure immutable retry-decision foundation, intentionally outside `Fogell.Store` | Signed input `7399fa68f6092029563787c9882ad7f18d266ce7` (tree `935c24f91b9e11c7c68ecd5a51c2eac23d0b5750`) adds a Domain law that creates one queued child below a zero-based attempt limit, returns `BudgetExhausted` at the boundary, and validates an exact prior result before replaying it. The decision binds the parent organization, node, ordinal, ancestry, restore epoch and original limit; a child gets the next ordinal, immutable parent link, initial fence and no lease. That signed input was Domain-only: it had no Store persistence, transaction/concurrency arbitration, dead-letter or outbox record, or scheduler/controller wiring, and terminal-status retry policy remained external. Current Store progress is tracked separately in FG-027b — [→ detail](tickets/FG-027a.md) |

Migration 0011 independently owns that invariant for direct SQL, repairs pre-0011 queued/offered children whose aggregate still carries the parent result across forced tenant RLS, and publishes build-bound compensation for both new and repaired decisions; the FG-224 claimant also requires queued node/build state. Budget exhaustion and replay never reopen aggregates. 16 concurrent callers still converge on one writer and 15 exact replays; cancellation after reopening is accepted and aborts the child before launch. A cancellation completed before a later retry decision may truthfully observe the then-terminal build; eliminating that interval requires runtime retry arbitration before terminal exposure. Scheduler/controller invocation and retryability policy remain outside this slice — [→ detail](tickets/FG-027b.md) |

## Wave 3 — Execution and process lifecycle

| id | pri | status | item | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FG-030 | P0 | **DONE** | Linux executor: fresh normalized workspace per attempt; reject absolute paths, traversal, symlink components | 6 tests: absolute, traversal, mid-path traversal, symlinked component, outside-root, and reuse all refused with a named error |
| FG-031 | P0 | **DONE** | Process group per step; SIGTERM then grace then SIGKILL (`adr/0005` match) | A `trap ... TERM` handler runs and writes its marker before death (graceful, no escalation). A step that ignores TERM is escalated to SIGKILL with `Escalated = true` |
| FG-032 | P0 | **DONE** | **Reap the step's process group at step end**, with an opt-out (`adr/0005` beat) | A real backgrounded daemon (pid recorded to a file) is dead after **success** and after **timeout**; the opt-out keeps it alive. Leaks are counted and reported in the diagnostic, never ignored |
| FG-033 | P0 | **DONE** | **Dead-process detection in seconds**, diagnostic naming the cause (`adr/0005` beat) | An externally SIGKILLed step is detected in **under 10 s** (Jenkins: ~10 min, then `exit code -1`). A signal exit (128+N) is reported as `Signalled`, **not** an ordinary exit code, and the diagnostic names the signal and warns the effect may be incomplete. **CLAIM CORRECTED** after a Codex review on PR #11: the engine cannot *prove* the termination came from outside. `setsid --wait` propagates the child's status verbatim, so the wait-status bit distinguishing "killed by signal 9" from "exited 137 deliberately" is already gone before .NET reports `ExitCode`. The diagnostic now says "most likely", names both possibilities, and reports the raw code so whoever wrote the script can settle it. The signal range was also widened from 128+36 to 128+64 — Linux has 64 signals and the higher ones were being reported as ordinary exits | Our own timeout is still `Aborted`, never mis-attributed |
| FG-034 | P1 | **DONE** | `timeout` and cancellation semantics identical to Jenkins' interrupt contract | `timeout-seconds` receipt PROVEN tier-1: `timeout(time: 3, unit: 'SECONDS')` around `sleep 60` aborts on both engines with an identical workspace (`started.txt` present, `finished.txt` absent). Jenkins' **default unit is MINUTES** — measured, and honoured. Interruption is polled while the step runs and takes the trappable SIGTERM → grace → SIGKILL path; a step interrupted from outside reports `Cancelled`, never `TimedOut`, so the cause is not misattributed |
| FG-035 | P1 | **DONE** | `retry(N)` = N total attempts, no backoff (`adr/0005` match) | Two receipts PROVEN tier-1. `retry-exhausts`: the body appends a line per attempt, and `attempts.txt` has **exactly 3 lines with the same hash on both engines** — the count is evidence, not a claim. `retry-succeeds`: fail-then-succeed is **SUCCESS** on both; the first version reported `failure` because a failed attempt bumped the monotone build status, so a retry attempt now writes to a throwaway sink |
| FG-036 | P1 | **DONE** | `parallel`: siblings finish by default; `failFast` interrupts them | Three receipts PROVEN tier-1. `parallel-siblings-finish`: the slow branch's `slow.txt` is **present** on both engines despite its sibling failing. `parallel-failfast` and `parallel-always-failfast`: `late.txt` is **absent** on both — the sleeping branch was interrupted mid-`sleep 45`. Jenkins corrected three assumptions here: `failFast` is stage-level (Jenkins *rejects* it inside `parallel { }`), declarative emits no `[branch]` prefix, and a failFast build is `failure` not `aborted` (see `adr/0005`) |
| FG-037 | P1 | TODO | **No steps-per-stage ceiling** (`adr/0005` beat) | 400-step stage runs; Jenkins fails at 251; receipt records both |
| FG-038a | P2 | TODO | Windows executor via Job Objects, P/Invoke confined to one project (`adr/0006`) | Runs on a GitHub-hosted `windows-latest` runner — the hardware blocker is gone (technique observed on McLoving). Linux-only surface to replace: `/usr/bin/setsid` + `/proc` group-survivor scanning in `ProcessGroup.fs`, two `libc` DllImports in `Native.fs`. Acceptance: existing suites green on Windows AND a backgrounded child provably reaped via a Job Object, asserted the same way FG-032 asserts it on Linux. **This closes "Fogell RUNS on Windows" and nothing more** |
| FG-038b | P2 | TODO | Windows **differential** lane — Jenkins on the same runner | **MOVED to FG-113.** The only ticket that can license a platform-parity claim. Jenkins is Java and runs natively on Windows, so receipts are possible. Three costs, to be priced before starting: Windows CI minutes bill at **2x** on a private repo and each job pays JVM+Jenkins startup instead of hitting a warm controller; the CI Jenkins must be pinned to **2.568.1 with the identical plugin set** or its receipts cannot be compared with the Linux lane's, and plugin drift would masquerade as a Fogell bug; **CRLF** makes Windows workspace hashes differ from Linux by construction, and normalising that is a comparison *weakening* that must be declared in the contract like the FG-036 ordering relaxation — not applied quietly |
| FG-196 | **P1** | **DONE** | **An undeclared step runs under a 120-second deadline Jenkins does not have.** `WalkerStep.fs` defaults a step's `TimeoutMs` to `Some 120_000L` when no deadline is declared; the oracle's default is UNBOUNDED. Any pipeline whose step exceeds two minutes — which is nearly every production build — succeeds on Jenkins and ABORTS on Fogell. No existing receipt could see this: every tier-1 case finishes in seconds, so the constant sat inside 63 PROVEN receipts without once being exercised | MEASURED 2026-08-10, the duel: both engines built Jenkins itself (pinned `jenkins-2.566` source, identical four-stage pipeline — unpack + git init, bootstrap Maven 3.9.9, `mvn -pl war -am` with a workspace-local repository, verify) serially on the same host. First Fogell run ABORTED at 130 s by the undeclared deadline — narration `Cancelling nested steps due to timeout`, journal `build-finished aborted`, cause named correctly, policy wrong. With an EXPLICIT `timeout(time: 45, unit: 'MINUTES')` the same pipeline SUCCEEDED in **200 s against the oracle's 213 s** (`jenkins.war` 101,071,431 B vs 101,070,520 B; SHA-256 pairs and logs in the bench archive, luigi `~/duel-receipts.tgz` — informational evidence, not a sealed receipt). Acceptance: an undeclared step deadline matches the oracle — unbounded, limited only by a DECLARED `timeout()` step or option; the 120 s constant survives nowhere a receipt does not show Jenkins itself imposing one; a tier-1 receipt with a single step sleeping LONGER than 120 s proves the parity in both directions; and the walker's interrupt/deadline ordering invariants (FG-101) hold unchanged with no default deadline present. **DONE 2026-08-17**: `WalkerStep.fs` passes the enclosing deadline through unchanged — `None` when nothing is declared — and the constant is gone from the tree. Receipt `undeclared-deadline-unbounded` PROVEN tier-1: a 130 s step succeeds on both engines with the same workspace hash. Full suite re-run 163 of 164 — every timeout/cancellation receipt PROVEN, which is the FG-101 half of the acceptance; the one divergence (`script-nested-wrappers-env`) reproduces IDENTICALLY with this change stashed, was measured as pre-existing before this landed, and is filed as FG-201 **RENUMBERED FROM FG-171 ON 2026-08-14.** **THE COLLISION HAD A HISTORY.** Found 2026-08-15 after the renumber: worktrees at `../fogell-fg170` and `../fogell-fg171` held branches whose commits call these two tickets **FG-170** and **FG-171** — an EARLIER numbering under which the deadline ticket was FG-170's neighbour. That numbering was already wrong when the branches were cut, because the board's FG-170 is a different ticket and is DONE. So this pair has been numbered THREE ways: 170/171 on the branches, 171/172 on the board — which is where it collided with Wave 9 — and 196/197 now. **THE FIRST VERSION OF THIS NOTE SAID THE BRANCHES 'HOLD REAL COMMITTED WORK' AND LEFT A REBASE-OR-DROP DECISION OPEN. THAT WAS WRONG AND IS CORRECTED.** Both were FULLY MERGED — `fg-171-default-step-deadline` by PR #51 and `fg-170-executor-polling` by PR #52 — so their tips are ancestors of `main` and nothing was ever at risk. I inferred 'unmerged' from the worktrees being 130-odd commits behind, which measures only how long ago they were cut. The worktrees and local branches are removed. THE PROOF IS `git merge-base --is-ancestor <tip> main`, TRUE FOR BOTH, corroborated by `git branch --merged main` listing them: those name `main` explicitly. `git branch -d` also refused nothing, but that is the WEAKER witness and was nearly cited as the strong one — it tests reachability from HEAD, not from `main`, so it proves what is claimed here only because it happened to be run while `main` was checked out. Raised by review on PR #60. The numbering history is kept because it explains why the collision existed, not because anything needs recovering. This row and the Wave 9 row of the same number were two DIFFERENT tickets sharing one id, so the board asserted a single id was both TODO here and TODO there, and every reader and every cross-reference had to guess which was meant. The Wave 9 rows keep the number because they own all 3 code references and the queue slot; this pair had none, so the collision is resolved where it costs nothing and no differential case comment is touched — editing one would break its receipt seal (FG-164) and force a re-run for a numbering fix. FOUND BY DERIVING the queue section's own row counts rather than by reading the rows: 189 ticket rows against 187 unique ids is the only place the clash was visible. |
| FG-197 | **P1** | TODO | **Executor per-step latency is ~85% fixed-interval polling.** Replace the sleep cadences in `ProcessGroup.fs` — 5 ms pgid ticks plus a flat 50 ms marker fallback, a 10 ms exit poll, and `flushReaders`' 40 ms minimum stability window — with event-driven waits (process-handle wait, reader-completion signal, pipe-read pgid marker), preserving the daemon-safety property each sleep was guarding | MEASURED 2026-08-09 on luigi (fresh main @ 37090bd, 100-step delta method, EveryStep journal): **67 ms/step durable vs Jenkins 54.13 ms/step on the SAME host** — Fogell loses to the oracle it is chartered to beat, and the loss is sleep constants, not work: the journal alone is 0.859 ms/step (FG-024) and `flushReaders` taxes every step 40 ms flat even when the command exited instantly. Figures are from a bench archive (luigi `~/games-round1-receipts.tgz`), NOT a sealed receipt — re-measure before touching code, per the FG-035 probe-first rule. Acceptance: exit detected by handle wait with no poll loop; flush by reader completion with a bounded budget, never a fixed window; pgid from the pipe; the guards that justified each sleep keep their tests (backgrounded-daemon reap, pipe-holding daemon cannot wedge the reader, setsid-pid distinction); per-step cost re-measured on the same host as the Jenkins baseline at EveryStep, gate **< 8 ms** **RENUMBERED FROM FG-172 ON 2026-08-14.** **THE COLLISION HAD A HISTORY** — stated once on the FG-196 row above, which carries the three numberings, the merge status of both branches, and the correction to this note's first version. | FG-198 | **P1** | **DONE** | The line-208 queue rule is enforced by whoever is looking, and it has failed repeatedly in two shapes that survive review | **COUNTED, NOT RECALLED, AND THE TWO SHAPES ARE KEPT APART BECAUSE THEY NEED DIFFERENT CHECKS.** The CAUSE/FIX/SCOPE shape: six recorded in the rule's own paragraph, plus FG-180 + FG-141, FG-193 and FG-196 found by the verifier on this branch — nine. The REORDER-FRAGILE shape, first named here and the one a deny-list cannot hold: ten. Eight POSITIONAL (FG-196, FG-176 and FG-135 in review, then FG-179, FG-135 again, FG-193, FG-191, FG-193 again) and two UNIVERSAL, where a row quantified over what the OTHER rows say (FG-196, FG-114) and this branch falsified both by ranking two unmeasured rows — one of those written into FG-114 by the patch removing a positional claim from it. They are counted as ONE shape because ONE test finds them: could the sentence become false if the table were reordered or re-ranked, with no code changing. **AN ATTEMPT TO GENERALISE FURTHER — 'a row may only make claims about its own ticket' — WAS WRITTEN AND RETRACTED IN THE SAME PASS**, because a ranking table's rows exist to be compared and the absolute condemned the pairing arguments that are doing real work. That is the argument for a checker over more care: nine rounds of manual sweeps produced a stricter rule each time and a fresh violation inside each fix. A single total would be tidier and would hide that the second sub-class was invisible until someone re-ranked the table, which is the fact that argues for the checker. **NINETEEN INSTANCES ACROSS THE TWO SHAPES TALLIED ABOVE, MOST OF THEM FOUND BY THE PRE-PUSH VERIFIER AND BY REVIEW ACROSS FIVE ROUNDS ON A DOCS-ONLY BRANCH** — which is the argument for a script: the rule is old, the sweeps were sincere, and a row-by-row human reading against it still passed a predicted SCOPE three times because the rule as written named only CAUSE and FIX. A checker was written and run during that sweep and is not committed: it splits the queue table, strips quoted retractions, and matches a deny-list of overclaim tells (`no <x> change`, `self-contained`, `if real is`, `fires on`, `all <x> pipelines`, `the fix is`, `trivial`, `one-line`, `simply`). It was PROVEN TO FAIL on three planted rows and proven silent on a quoted retraction — the first version flagged one, because a bare apostrophe in `FG-183's` defeats naive quote-pairing and that is the failure mode this checker is most exposed to. **WHAT IT CANNOT DO, STATED SO A PASS IS NOT MISREAD**: a deny-list catches spellings, not overclaims, so it is a floor and not the rule; it cannot detect a cause named in words it does not know, and a row that passes it has not been proven compliant. Same honesty the FG-162 audit prints about prose numbers. **RAISED FROM P2 TO P1 ON 2026-08-15, MID-REVIEW, BY THE THING ITSELF.** The row was filed at P2 on the argument that every instance was caught before merge and the cost was review rounds rather than a wrong answer. Four more instances arrived in the two rounds after it was written, and THREE OF THOSE FOUR WERE INTRODUCED BY THE PATCHES FIXING THE OTHERS — a positional claim written into FG-135, FG-193 and FG-191 by the same pass that removed positional claims from FG-196, FG-176 and FG-179. That is the FG-195 shape exactly, where the fourth defect was found inside the guard written for the third, and it is the measurement the P2 argument lacked: the cost is not review rounds, it is that hand-editing prose against this rule does not converge. A fifth was invisible to the sweep because its vocabulary held superlatives and no ordering VERB | the checker fails a board with each planted overclaim, passes the swept board, and stays silent on quoted retractions; wired into `scripts/build-and-test.sh` with a `prove-queue-rows.sh` beside the other proofs **DONE 2026-08-17**: `scripts/audit-queue-rows.py` + `scripts/prove-queue-rows.sh`, both blocking in the gate, proof first. Proven to fail on all three planted shapes (cause/fix, scope, positional+universal), proven silent on a double-quoted retraction carrying `FG-183's` bare apostrophe — the exact trap the row above predicted — and proven to REFUSE a board where a track table went missing. THAT LAST ARM CAUGHT THE CHECKER'S OWN FIRST VERSION: renaming Track 1 left Tracks 2-3 parseable, rows were found, and the audit passed while checking a third less than it claimed — the proof lane paid for itself before the checker was an hour old. The floor is stated in the audit's own output: a deny-list catches spellings, not overclaims, and a pass is not proof of compliance |
| FG-199 | **P1** | **DONE** | Nothing checked that the expected REVIEWERS reported; an absent reviewer and a clean review looked identical | **MEASURED ON PR #60, 2026-08-15, AND IT ALREADY COST A MERGE.** The PR was open 3 h 24 min (09:52Z to 13:16Z) and `chatgpt-codex-connector[bot]` NEVER SUBMITTED — on #58 and #59 it had reviewed within 2 min 18 s and 3 min 00 s of opening, and on #60 it had not submitted in the 3 h 24 min before the merge. WHETHER MORE WAITING WOULD HAVE PRODUCED ONE IS NOT ESTABLISHED and the first version of this row asserted it was not — the API records an absence, never an intent, and a counterfactual is not a measurement however reasonable it sounds. I merged it and reported that review findings were addressed, which was true of the ONE reviewer that showed up and silent about the one that did not. THE DEFECT IS THE QUERY, NOT THE BOT: I asked 'are there unaddressed findings' and treated the empty answer as approval, so absence of a reviewer was indistinguishable from absence of concerns. That is the FG-158 shape — a checker that reads a missing verdict as a pass — recurring in the merge path rather than in a lane. **AND A THIRD WRONG CLAIM, WHICH IS THE SAME ERROR A THIRD TIME.** This row first said `copilot-pull-request-reviewer` STOPPED emitting a check-run after #57. It never stopped: its check-runs are present on `552c99f`, `4e48809` and `2fce430` — the SHAs it reviewed — and absent only on the final SHAs it never saw. I had queried the MERGE HEAD and read absence as cessation, which is the identical mistake as reading an absent reviewer as a clean review, and it reached the owner as a claim that Copilot had gone quiet. ALL THREE ERRORS COLLAPSE INTO ONE FACT: a review, and the check-run that accompanies it, attach to the SHA REVIEWED; querying the final SHA answers a different question than the one being asked. NOT A CLASS-A DEFECT IN THE ENGINE — it is a hole in the process that lands engine changes, which is why it is filed beside FG-198 rather than in a wave **SCOPE MEASURED BY `scripts/review-coverage.py`, AND IT IS NOT THREE PRs: 45 OF 60 MERGED PRs** landed a commit that at least one expected reviewer never saw. The three below are the ones I watched happen; the script found the rest, which is the whole difference between a habit and a checker — and the verifier had said three times across eight rounds that a script was the answer before I wrote one. Plan of correction, including what is NOT claimed about those 45: [→ detail](tickets/FG-199.md). **AND THE ABSENT REVIEWER WAS THE SMALLER HALF.** Writing the acceptance exposed a second gap, and it is stated here per PR because SIX ROUNDS ON THIS ROW WERE SPENT CORRECTING SENTENCES THAT GENERALISED FROM THESE SAME THREE FACTS. What each PR was MERGED at, and who had reviewed that exact commit: #58 at `ca6a0ea`, reviewed by Codex and NOT by Copilot; #59 at `021fcb5`, reviewed by NEITHER; #60 at `c481649`, reviewed by NEITHER, with Codex absent from the PR entirely. In #59 and #60 that unreviewed commit was the fix pushed in answer to a finding. Codex re-reviewed #58's head `ca6a0ea` at 06:16:37Z. So 'reviewed' has been weaker than I have been reporting it for the whole session, and the first draft of this row's own acceptance cited #59 as the PASSING fixture while #59 fails it — caught by the verifier, and the clearest possible evidence that the assumption was invisible to me | a pre-merge check that NAMES both expected reviewers and refuses unless each has submitted against the CURRENT head SHA. It must be PROVEN TO FAIL, and the fixtures are already on record: all three of #58, #59 and #60 must FAIL it, each for its own reason — #58 because Copilot never reviewed its head `ca6a0ea` though Codex did; #59 because neither reviewer saw `021fcb5`; #60 because Codex never reviewed at all and Copilot did not see `c481649` — so a passing fixture has to be constructed rather than found, which is itself the measurement that this has never held here |
| FG-200 | **P1** | **DONE** | No corpus file has ever been EXECUTED here, so the cost of the first corpus receipt is unknown | **FILED 2026-08-15 WHEN THE THREE-TRACK REFRAME FOUND THAT NOTHING OWNED IT.** "tier1=0" of 228 sat in the scorecard for months as a number with no ticket behind it, which is how it came to read as a permanent property rather than as work nobody had scheduled. The path is built and open — FG-090 put a filter exemption in `generate-scorecard.bb` precisely so a receipt naming a corpus file counts as tier-1 evidence — and the untrusted-input rule permits execution ALLOWLISTED BY EXECUTED SURFACE rather than forbidding it. So this is not a policy change; it is a cost nobody has paid once. **WHAT IS UNMEASURED, WHICH IS THE WHOLE TICKET**: how long allowlisting one real Jenkinsfile's executed surface takes, how many of the 169 admitted files have a surface that is allowlistable at all, and whether the oracle can run them without credentials, network or plugins this lab does not have. A ticket claiming corpus coverage is achievable is a hypothesis until one file has gone end to end. **NOT A CLAIM THAT THE SUITE IS WORTHLESS** — 187 of 187 hand-written cases are proven and they found real class-A defects all month; the point is only that they are a population written for this engine  **STEP 1 RUN 2026-08-15 AND ITS RESULT WITHDRAWN THE SAME DAY**: a keyword survey reported 89 of 169 admitted files as having no execution blocker, and inspecting the shortest of them falsified it — `buildPlugin(...)` and `openshiftBuild(...)` are ordinary-looking calls to steps that exist in Jenkins via a plugin or an implicitly loaded library and do not exist here, and one 'file' is the single line `jenkins/Jenkinsfile`. The question is not what a file MENTIONS but whether every step it CALLS is one both engines can run, which only the step registry answers. Corrected method and the withdrawn number: [→ detail](tickets/FG-200.md) **DONE 2026-08-17.** Receipt `cvitter_jenkins-pipeline-examples` PROVEN tier-1 — one stage, one `echo`, identical output and workspace on both engines; promoted by the FG-090 exemption, so `tier1=1` (admitted stood at 168 that day). THE COST, MEASURED: selection was the work — the withdrawn survey's three smaller 'survivors' are all implicit-shared-library calls that cannot run on either engine without the library, and this file was found by reading candidates against the step registry — while the run itself took 4.7 seconds wall clock. THE ALLOWLIST: the executed surface is one `echo` with a single-quoted literal — no `sh`, no interpolation, so no shell process starts and no network capability exists on either engine. WHAT DOES NOT GENERALISE, measured on the way: THE NO-EGRESS FENCE THE CONTRACT NAMES DOES NOT EXIST — probed before the run, `jenkins-lab` resolved DNS and fetched an external HTTPS URL (HTTP 200), and the Fogell side runs on a host with full egress — so this receipt's cheapness is a property of an inert surface, and every `sh`-bearing corpus file waits on that fence being BUILT before it can run under the rule. Also outside what this cost measures: implicit shared libraries (the shape that falsified the first survey), oracle-side tooling, and argument shapes (the registry check is by name only). Full account: [→ detail](tickets/FG-200.md) | ONE corpus file with a differential receipt, plus a written account of what it cost and which of the 169 admitted files that cost does NOT generalise to |

## Wave 3.5 — Cross-cutting invariants (BEFORE more step coverage)

Every ticket here replaces a class of defect that has already recurred, with a **sweep and
a test**, not another instance fix. The counts are findings from PRs #11–#17 that mention
the class; they overlap, because one finding often touches two.

This wave exists because of a measured pattern: each new step added to the walker
rediscovered these invariants independently, and the reviewer noticed the repetition
before I did — three separate times. Ordering them ahead of Wave 4's remaining steps
converts that recurring cost into a one-time cost.

| id | pri | status | scope | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FG-100 | **P0** | **DONE** | **One string model.** Literal / GString / expression provenance, escaped dollars, and the balanced placeholder scanner, decided in ONE place that every consumer routes through | **52 findings.** Today the rules are re-derived per consumer: `environment`, `withEnv`, `input`, `echo`, `equals`, `when` each learned them separat… [→ detail](tickets/FG-100.md) |
| FG-101 | **P0** | **DONE** | **One cancellation model.** A single predicate combining interrupt and deadline, a single classification of the CAUSE, and a single place that decides `aborted` vs `failure` | **54 + 40 findings.** Supersedes FG-002e. Abort-cause was wrong six times (shell, stash, unstash, deleteDir, input, then input again as an ordering RACE); "every early return is a missed poll" recurred five times. My sweep asked *does every site check?* — the right question was *does every site check in the right ORDER, and record which event actually happened?* Acceptance: one helper, every step routed through it, plus a test that drives deadline-first and sibling-first against every wrapper step and asserts the terminal status AND the selected `post` arm |
| FG-102 | P1 | **DONE** | **Output-narration contract.** No suppression by text a build could emit; every rule is context-gated or deleted | **17 findings.** FG-002f discharged the audit (six of eight suppressions were dead) and `Fogell.Differential.Tests` now emits look-alikes; this tic… [→ detail](tickets/FG-102.md) |
| FG-105 | **P1** | **DONE** | **Split the walker.** `Fogell.fs` broken into reviewable units — step execution, wrapper/block dispatch, stage/post orchestration, trace assembly — each with a stated contract | **Directed 2026-07-30, and the telemetry already said it:** 55% of all historical review findings land in `Fogell.fs` (~1,750 lines); leaf PRs sett… [→ detail](tickets/FG-105.md) |
| FG-103 | P1 | **DONE** | **Width and fail-closed audit.** Every duration is int64 to the executor boundary; every "cannot decide" path refuses by name | **6 + 19 findings.** The int32 narrowing appeared twice — the second time because I silenced a compiler error rather than asking why the types differed. Acceptance: no `int` conversion on a duration before the executor call; a test with a 30-day timeout on every wrapper step **Done.** The last narrowing (a clamped Thread.Sleep) is retired — the CLASS is banned, not the instance; the 30-day-budget acceptance test runs a real process under a 2,592,000,000 ms budget. The fail-closed half found its target: `survivorsIn` returned 0 when the /proc read itself failed, making the leak check a gate that could not fail while FG-032's claim rested on it — unknown is -1 now, treated as still-populated downstream, and the diagnostic says the check was unavailable. Checklist question added: does any new failure path catch-and-default to the value that means all-clear? Merged with FG-102 in PR #24 (9ae194a); the round-47 drain-before-snapshot fix is this ticket's fail-closed shape applied to the Terminated dedup |
| FG-104 | P1 | **DONE** | **Comment-as-specification drift.** A doc comment that describes behaviour must be covered by a test or receipt, or say it is unimplemented | Three times a comment of mine became the specification the code disagreed with — `resolveName` promising `env['X']`, a case comment asserting Jenki… [→ detail](tickets/FG-104.md) |

## Wave 3.6 — Harness capability (unlocked the blocked tickets together)

These tickets were blocked on the same thing: the differential harness could only produce
ONE build, of a plain job, on Linux, with no SCM. Each was filed separately as "needs a
harness that can…". Grouping them turned repeated stalls into one harness expansion; the
table below owns their current statuses.

| id | pri | status | scope | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FG-110 | P1 | **DONE** | **Multi-build lane**: keep a job across several builds | **MERGED as PR #26 (9b2e1a7) after ONE review round.** A case containing `//// NEXT BUILD ////` separators runs as consecutive builds of ONE job: Jenkins side creates once/updates config.xml between builds/polls by number; Fogell side keeps the workspace, threads each result as the next `previous` (OrchestrationDeps.PreviousBuild), increments BUILD_NUMBER, and scopes stashes per build (Codex P1, measured: `stash-not-carried` — build 2's unstash FAILS on both engines). One tier-1 receipt per build. Suite 88/88. Sequence claims receipt-proven by `sequence-carries-state` (workspace persists, BUILD_NUMBER increments). Pre-push self-review found 8 defects (BUILD_NUMBER pinned to 1, statusOf None-default = FG-103 shape, unproven persistence claim, discarded trigger status, evidence-clobbering exception path, asymmetric throw guards, receipt-name collision, malformed-file diagnostics) — all fixed before the PR. Final seal evidence/20260731T212811Z-fg-110 (manifest 620f88fbd8e6e89c) |
| FG-111 | P2 | **DONE** | **SCM + multibranch lane**: a real repo, a `CHANGE_ID`, a populated changelog | **Git half.** **MERGED as PR #28-era work in PR #27 (afa9e27) after 6 fix rounds + 1 reasoned decline.** The lane: fixture repo (main+master refs) via git daemon on the lab host, one URL reachable from both engines, preflighted. The `git` step (WalkerGit.fs) is fully receipt-proven — clone/refetch/deleteDir/default-branch shapes all MEASURED first (7 receipts), controller-side per-(url,branch)-per-build revision records mirror Jenkins' build data, ${GITVERSION} folds line-scoped, suite 94/94. ~30 corpus files use the git step; the branchless master default un-walls 13 more. **Residual, stated:** CHANGE_ID/multibranch context needs a forge mock (github-branch-source is the only change-request source installed) — FG-048c stays blocked on that half; `checkout scm` (49 files) needs the SCM-defined-job harness mode (FG-052, next). Final seal evidence/20260801T000612Z-fg-111 (manifest e04b992a9ebbeba9) |
| FG-112 | P1 | **DONE** | **Restart lane**: SIGKILL the controller mid-build and resume | **MERGED as PR #29 (156a19a) after TWENTY-THREE review rounds** — 21 findings fixed, 2 answered with evidence (one refuted from the code, one state… [→ detail](tickets/FG-112.md) |
| FG-113 | P2 | TODO | **Windows lane** (was FG-038b) | GitHub-hosted `windows-latest`; the CI Jenkins must be pinned to 2.568.1 with an identical plugin set or its receipts cannot be compared with the Linux lane's. **A FLEET WINDOWS HOST IS NOW AVAILABLE (owner, 2026-08-10)**, which removes TWO of the three costs priced in FG-038b: no 2x CI minutes, and a warm pinned controller instead of paying JVM+Jenkins startup per job — the same shape as the `jenkins-lab` box on luigi, which is what makes the Linux lane affordable at 132 cases. IT REMOVES NEITHER of the two things that decide whether the lane means anything: the controller still has to be pinned to 2.568.1 with an IDENTICAL PLUGIN SET or plugin drift masquerades as a Fogell bug, and **CRLF** still makes Windows workspace hashes differ from Linux BY CONSTRUCTION — normalising that is a comparison WEAKENING and must be declared in the receipt contract like the FG-036 ordering relaxation, never applied quietly. Note also that the lane is gated on FG-038a: there is no Windows EXECUTOR yet (`ProcessGroup.fs` needs `setsid`/`/proc` replaced by Job Objects), so the host unblocks the lane's HARDWARE, not its prerequisite. First concrete use, and much cheaper than the full lane: `bat` currently sits in `WalkerRules.stepsHonouringReturnFlags` on contract with no evidence |

## Wave 4 — Step coverage by measured demand

Ranked by corpus set-cover among the 119 Jenkins-ready files, not popularity.

FG-015b's PR #110 complexity closure is retained in
[evidence](../evidence/20260821T111508Z-fg-015b-pr110-complexity): reference-cycle
scanning is iterative O(V+E) over identity-memoized completed nodes, sibling
aliases remain legal, equality/ordering no longer re-expand completed shared-DAG
pairs, and invalid hosted calls no longer force expanded collection display
before their no-effect validation refusal. Compatibility counts are unchanged.

| id | pri | status | item | demand |
|---|---|---|---|---:|
| FG-040 | P0 | **DONE** | `sh` / `bat` with real subprocess, streaming log, exit-code propagation | Exit 0 → Success, exit 7 → Failure with the code named in the diagnostic; stdout/stderr separated; output streams line-by-line during the run; env passed through; unimplemented steps fail closed by name |
| FG-041 | P0 | **PARTIAL** | `echo`, `dir`, `environment` scoping (lexical, stage overrides pipeline) | `echo` and environment scoping proven by differential receipt (`env-scoping`, stage overrides pipeline, shared vars inherited). `dir` nests and auto-creates, refusing traversal. `withEnv` still outstanding — split to FG-041b |
| FG-041b | P1 | **DONE** | `withEnv(['A=1']) { … }` block-scoped environment, `PATH+` augmentation, `${VAR}` interpolation | `withenv-scoping` receipt PROVEN tier-1. MEASURED and matched: inside the block an added variable is visible and a shadowed one takes the new value; **after** the block the added variable is UNSET and the shadowed one reverts to its outer value. The argument is a Groovy list literal, split where its meaning is needed rather than in the parser (`adr/0002`) |
| FG-042 | P0 | **PARTIAL** | `archiveArtifacts` with ant-glob expansion | Proven by receipt: multi-pattern archive (`out.txt, target/*.jar`) and the empty-match case, which Jenkins **fails** rather than passing quietly. Artifacts land outside the workspace so archiving cannot perturb the compared hash. Retrieval over a stable URL awaits the API — FG-042b |
| FG-043 | P1 | **DONE** | `junit` test-result ingest | Proven by receipt for passing and failing reports. Failing tests → **UNSTABLE**, not FAILURE (the build worked, the code did not) — matching Jenkins. A malformed report is reported, never silently counted as zero |
| FG-042b | P2 | TODO | Artifact retrieval at a stable URL, byte-exact | requires the controller API (FG-060) |
| FG-002d | P1 | **DONE** | Read EVERY review round before merging, and diff the finding list | I merged PR #13 believing Codex was re-posting stale findings. It was not: it posted **18 distinct findings across 4 rounds** and I had read 9. The cause was mine — a `created_at` filter compared against a cutoff in the wrong timezone (local is UTC−5; comments were stamped 04:39Z–05:14Z, so `> 00:00:00Z` matched everything) — so each poll re-showed round 1 and I read repetition into it. The nine unread findings included two of the same class as the parallel-sink bug: success-only post arms running on a failed build. `scripts/review-rounds.bb <pr>` groups comments by `pull_request_review_id` — a round is a REVIEW, not a wall-clock minute, prints each round's reviewed commit, and marks every finding NEW or seen. Run on #13 it shows **21 comments over 5 rounds, all 5 rounds 100% NEW — zero repeats**, which is the opposite of what I asserted. Every NEW line must be triaged before merging |
| FG-002c | P2 | **DONE** | Handle Jenkins' multi-line `set -x` trace continuations | **Retired by fg-102.** Jenkins runs `sh` under `set -x` and the trace is excluded as provenance, but a command containing a literal newline produces a trace spanning several lines where only the FIRST begins with `+ `. A continuation is not distinguishable from real output — the line after a trace is *usually* real output — so it is currently compared as output. Found while proving `withenv-comma-value`. The final sort/range review measured no-argument `sort()` as an in-place operation returning the same list identity; aliases and the returned value now share the sorted receiver, while a cyclic comparison faults before replacement. Boolean sort overloads remain sandbox-refused and comparator closures now refuse explicitly instead of being ignored. `IntRange` has distinct immutable provenance: it stays list-like for positive/negative reads, equality, display, ascending/descending iteration, and fresh `reverse`/`collect` results, but plain/compound/postfix/alias writes and no-argument sort raise catchable `UnsupportedOperationException` ancestry at the measured write phase. Exact-head cycle review then separated legal direct-self rendering from coercion: interpolation and explicit display retain `(this Collection)` / `(this Map)`, but any cyclic typed collection passed to a hosted step faults before dispatch, including the direct-self shape. Display, equality, ordering, hosted coercion and map-key hashing now share one typed `StackOverflowError`-ancestry fault family, so `Exception` cannot catch any of them while `Error`/`Throwable` can. Distinct cyclic list/map equality and `contains` fault without host recursion; same-reference equality remains a fast true; cyclic map-key insertion faults before mutation. A lane that proves a build failed but not WHICH rule failed it cannot tell a correct refusal from a coincidental one — the engine could start refusing for an entirely different reason and all four keep passing. That is the class this session spent twenty verifier rounds on: a checker asserting less than it reads as asserting.
