Claims corrected in the lane; this ticket is the fix. Wants the reason recorded in the journal (a durable build record whose only explanation of a failure is the word `failure` is thin regardless of the lanes) and surfaced by the host, then E and O tightened to assert it. NOT folded into the board PR: it changes the differential core's record type, and the receipt contract is not something to alter inside a docs change **DONE 2026-08-18, additively.** A `step-reason` record journals the ERROR-shaped diagnostic beside the failed or aborted finish it explains; `Resume.plan` ignores it (a reason explains a disposition, never is one), and the host journals it best-effort — a lost reason costs an explanation, never a disposition. The capture ref is freshened per hooked step AND per retry attempt — the pre-push verifier's blocker on this diff: `attemptCtx` shares the ref across attempts, so without the per-attempt freshening a superseded attempt's absorbed shell failure journaled as the reason for a final-attempt human rejection it did not explain, an overstating record worse than none. E, O and Z6 now assert the refusal's own wording from the journal (J still asserts from the log, which its refusal does reach); wire format flattens embedded newlines/tabs at encode so emitted content cannot break the decode. Known additive-format cost, stated when shipped: a pre-FG-114 binary reading a journal that carries the record stops at it and forgets everything behind it — a downgrade re-reads a terminal journal as non-terminal; `repairTail` does not truncate it, so no data is destroyed |

| FG-119 | P2 | **DONE** | The differential oracle was unreliable — `sh -x` over pipelines interleaves on BOTH engines — xtrace line order is not deterministic | OBSERVED 2026-08-03 during FG-053: the case diverged with `jenkins=+ tr : \n fogell=+ head -3` on one run and was PROVEN on the next, with no code change between them. Both engines run `sh -xe`, and the trace lines for the stages of a SHELL PIPELINE are emitted by concurrent processes, so their interleaving is not fixed. A receipt that passes or fails by luck is worse than one that fails: it teaches a reader to re-run rather than to look. Either the case must avoid a multi-stage pipeline, or ordering within one pipeline's xtrace must join the documented relaxations with a stated reason. NOT diagnosed further here — it surfaced during an unrelated slice and guessing at it would be the same mistake as the timestamps assumptions. **ESCALATED 2026-08-04 (PR #36): the symptom is not only ORDERING, it is INTRA-LINE INTERLEAVING**, which is materially worse and which this row's original wording did not cover. Two cases diverged in one run — `fogell=+ printenv+  PATH` (from `+ printenv PATH`) and, in `stash-unstash`, `fogell=+ + lswc -l out` (from `+ ls out` and `+ wc -l`). Those are two writers interleaving mid-line on one stream, not two lines swapping places, so the sorted-multiset relaxation that covers `parallel` output cannot help: the LINES THEMSELVES are corrupt. Both cases are single-stage and neither involves the parser change on that branch; the same code gave 105/105 and 106/106 on adjacent runs, so it is a flake in the harness's own trace capture, not a divergence. `stash-unstash` is NOT a pipeline case, which widens this beyond the shell-pipeline hypothesis in the original text. Prime suspect is the xtrace reader assembling lines without holding `outputLock` for a whole write. This now costs real time — a green suite has to be distinguished from a lucky one on every run. **RATE MEASURED 2026-08-04 over four consecutive 106-case runs on PR #36: 106/106, 104/106, 106/106, 105/106** — so roughly one to two cases per run, ~1-2%, with a DIFFERENT case each time: `withenv-path-augmentation`, `stash-unstash`, `credentials-string`, and the three `parallel-*` receipts churning seals on nearly every run. That spread is the finding: it is not a property of any case family, it is the harness's trace capture. **THAT SENTENCE WAS WRONG AND IS RETRACTED**: it read `stash-unstash` and `credentials-string` as "single-stage non-parallel, so not a shell pipeline", which conflated a JENKINS-level `parallel` with a SHELL pipeline. All three carry one — `ls out 2>/dev/null | wc -l`, `env | grep -c "^TOKEN="`, `printenv PATH | tr ":" "\n" | head -3` — so this row's ORIGINAL hypothesis was right and I overrode it on a category error, without opening the case files. MECHANISM NOW CONFIRMED by decomposing the corruption: `+ ls out` and `+ wc -l` interleave as `+ ` / `+ ` / `ls` / `wc -l` / ` out` = `+ + lswc -l out`, which is CHARACTER-level interleaving of two writers, not line reordering. Each stage of a shell pipeline is its own process and `sh -xe` has each write its own `+ cmd` trace to the shared descriptor, evidently in more than one `write()` per line, so two stages interleave between those calls. STILL OPEN and the next thing to measure: why JENKINS does not corrupt on the same script — its durable-task capture path differs from Fogell's `2>&1`-into-one-pipe, and whether it is genuinely immune or merely luckier has NOT been established. **ROOT CAUSE FOUND 2026-08-04, and it retracts the previous paragraph of this row.** `dash` writes an `sh -x` trace line in more than one `write()`, so two stages of a SHELL PIPELINE interleave character-by-character: `+ ls out` and `+ wc -l` become `+ + lswc -l out`. ISOLATED WITHOUT .NET, 200 iterations each of the same four-pipeline script: **PIPE capture 16 corrupted runs, FILE capture 15** — statistically identical, so the capture mechanism is NOT the cause and Fogell's `2>&1`-into-a-pipe is exonerated. **RETRACTED: "the engines are not symmetric — jenkins=0, fogell=2".** That was 10 runs, and at Fogell's observed ~20%/run P(0 corrupt in 10) is 0.8^10 ≈ **11%** — an ordinary outcome, never evidence of immunity. Tested directly instead: the CONTAINER's dash corrupts at **5/200 runs (~2.5%)** against the local host's ~8%, the gap being load and hardware, not engine design. BOTH ENGINES ARE AFFECTED and neither side's xtrace can be assumed clean. Consequence for the fix: this is not a Fogell defect to repair but an inherent property of `sh -x` over pipelines, so the harness must FAIL CLOSED on it — detect a mid-line `+ ` in a trace row, declare the RUN unreliable and re-run, rather than record a verdict from corrupt evidence. Relaxing it into the comparison contract would be wrong: it would also swallow a genuine divergence that happened to look interleaved. Still separate, and still open: the ORDERING mode (`jenkins=+ cat fogell=+ wc -l`, both lines valid) which is legitimately nondeterministic and DOES belong in the relaxations. **FIXED 2026-08-04 by CONFIRMATION BY REPETITION, not relaxation.** A diverging case is now re-run up to twice before any verdict is sealed; a divergence that reproduces is real and still fails, one that does not is reported RECOVERED with the case name, the re-run count and the ORIGINAL divergence text. A corruption DETECTOR was considered and rejected: `+ echo a + b` is the ordinary trace of `echo "a + b"`, so a mid-line `+ ` has real false positives, and worse it would swallow a genuine divergence that happened to look interleaved. Repetition discriminates where a pattern cannot, and it covers the ORDERING mode too without having to decide in advance which mode a given divergence is. PROVEN BOTH DIRECTIONS: a permanent divergence (`sh 'printf "[\8]\n"'`, jenkins=failure fogell=success) re-ran twice and still reported DIVERGED(4) — retries do not mask real bugs; and over 12 runs of the race probe, 7 diverged and all 7 RECOVERED to clean receipts where every one would previously have reddened the suite. **HONEST LIMIT**: three attempts turn a p-per-run flake into p^3, so the ~2-8% race is now ~0.05%, but a genuinely 50%-intermittent defect would still pass 12.5% of the time. That is exactly why RECOVERED prints rather than being swallowed — a real intermittent bug shows up as the SAME case recovering run after run, and a recovery that recurs is a defect report, not noise. Watch for it. At that rate a clean suite was a coin flip and every green result needed a second run to mean anything, which is why this outranked the P2s queued behind it. **(HISTORICAL — that was the state BEFORE the fix below; the retry now handles it and green is trustworthy again.)** |
| FG-120 | P2 | TODO | Stage-level `options { timestamps() }` is refused, not supported | Jenkins 2.568.1 honours `timestamps()` inside a stage and stamps that stage's output. Fogell enables the wrapper for the WHOLE BUILD or not at all, so the stage form is REFUSED by name rather than ignored — honouring the pipeline form while silently dropping the stage form would produce unstamped output exactly where Jenkins stamps, and the engine would not announce it. Wants a scoped enable/restore around stage execution, the CLI's `DeclaresTimestamps` scan widened to stage options (it reads top-level only, so the lanes cannot currently see this), and a stage-level case. Raised by the pre-push verifier on FG-053 |
| FG-121 | P2 | TODO | The `timestamps()` refusals mark failure but do not STOP the build | Jenkins rejects a bad Declarative model at COMPILE time: no stage runs and no `post` fires. Fogell's three refusals — `timestamps(false)`/named args, and stage-level `timestamps()` — set `root.Failed` and then fall through to the stage loop and pipeline `post`, so the RESULT matches while the OUTPUT and any post side effects do not. | FG-122 | **P1** | **DONE** | `sh` passed `\033` through as literal characters where Jenkins emits an ESC byte | Receipt `sh-octal-escape`, suite 104/104. The Groovy string lexer mapped only `\n`, `\t` and `\r` and returned the escape's FIRST CHARACTER for everything else, so `printf "\033[31m"` reached the shell as `033[31m` — the two engines ran DIFFERENT COMMANDS for the same Jenkinsfile, and every single-quoted `sh` body carrying a backslash escape diverged, not only colour. Java's octal (1-3 digits) and unicode escapes are now handled, plus `\b` and `\f`. FOUND while writing the FG-053 `ansiColor` case, which conflated it with the option under test. TWO LESSONS RECORDED: the fix first went into `Fogell.Groovy.Parser`, which is NOT the parser a Declarative `sh` body goes through — the measurement caught it, not review, and that change was then REVERTED rather than carried, because no receipt exercises the scripted path and an unproven claim is what the claim audit exists to refuse (FG-124 covers it); and the simple-letter map existed TWICE in `Lexeme.fs`, so `numericEscape` is shared rather than copied. **ROUND 2 (PR #36 review) corrected three things the first pass got wrong**, receipt `sh-escape-edges`, suite 105/105. (a) the octal reader was greedy: Java takes a THIRD digit only when the first is 0-3, so `\400` is `\40` (a space) plus a literal `0` — MEASURED `jenkins=+ printf [ 0]` against `fogell=+ printf [Ā]`. (b) a numeric escape decoding to `$` was returned BARE on the GString path, so `"\044MISSING"` became a live interpolation — MEASURED `jenkins=success fogell=FAILURE`, a build Jenkins passes, and a REGRESSION this branch introduced (the old fallback yielded harmless `044`). (c) the claim above is only half true as written: the NUMERIC map was shared and the SIMPLE-LETTER map was left duplicated, then `\b`/`\f` were added to one copy only — the duplication got WORSE on the commit that claimed to delete it, and the single-quoted receipt passed throughout because it never exercised the double-quoted path. Both parsers now share `simpleEscape` AND `numericEscape` and differ by exactly one function, `keepDollar`. (c) was raised by the pre-push verifier's model review, (a) and (b) by Codex |
| FG-123 | P3 | TODO | Two exotic `ansiColor` argument forms are mishandled | Raised on #35 after CI and the verifier had both gone green, and DEFERRED on measured risk: all **4** corpus uses are plain `ansiColor('xterm')`, none uses either shape. (a) a GString argument — `ansiColor("${'xterm'}")` — is copied from the parser's UNEVALUATED source into `TERM`, where Jenkins evaluates it first; needs the argument rendered through the interpreter. (b) a trailing closure — `ansiColor('xterm') { ... }` — is accepted, because the validation checks positional and named arguments but not `Step.Block`; Jenkins rejects an option carrying a closure, so this should join the compile-shaped refusals |

| FG-125 | P2 | **DONE** | Slashy strings routed backslash sequences through the escape decoder | Receipt `sh-slashy-escape` PROVEN tier-1, suite 106/106. A slashy literal escapes ONLY its `/` delimiter and preserves every other backslash sequence, so `/\033/` is a backslash and three digits. All four call sites used `quoted "/"` and therefore `escapedChar`, which decoded an ESC byte: MEASURED `jenkins=+ printf [\033]` against `fogell=+ printf []` — the two engines ran DIFFERENT COMMANDS, the same defect FG-122 fixed for the quoted forms while leaving it standing here. Now routed through a `slashyQuoted` that passes non-delimiter backslashes through, and the `Lexeme.fs` comment claiming Groovy's escapes are Java's is narrowed to the quoted forms. **WHAT UNBLOCKED IT WAS A SPELLING, NOT AN INSIGHT**: this was deferred one round earlier as unmeasurable because the probe `sh /printf '[\033]'/` was refused by Jenkins at compile time. The PARENTHESISED call `sh(/.../)` compiles, and the pre-push verifier's model review supplied it. Raised by Codex on PR #36, spelling and confirmation from the verifier |
| FG-126 | P2 | TODO | An INVALID escape runs here and is REFUSED by Jenkins — the engines disagree about validity | MEASURED on Jenkins 2.568.1 (probe kept out of `differential/cases/` deliberately — it DIVERGES by design and would redden the suite): `sh 'printf "[\8]\n"'` gives **jenkins=failure fogell=success**, Fogell tracing `+ printf [8]`. `8` is not an octal digit and not a simple-letter escape, so Groovy refuses the SCRIPT at compile time while `escapedChar`'s `| c -> c` fallback drops the backslash and runs. Pre-existing — the fallback predates FG-122 — and NOT a value bug, so it needs the compile-rejection mechanism (`compileRejected`, see FG-121), not a decoding change. **THE TRAP, and the reason this is not a quick allowlist**: a naive scan for backslash-plus-non-escape-letter matches **17** corpus files, and every one is `\\` — an ESCAPED BACKSLASH then a letter (`\\d`, `\\s` inside regexes), valid Groovy that `escapedChar` already handles. Before FG-122 nothing could produce them — the old fallback dropped the backslash — so the in-band choice was safe and its comment said so; **FG-122's numeric decoding invalidated that**, and `"\000X"` rendered `$X` while a value starting `\001` was evaluated as an expression. STOPGAP SHIPPED on that branch: `rejectSentinel` refuses to decode into either code point, so the escape falls through to the literal fallback — precisely the pre-FG-122 behaviour for those two, no worse than before, and incapable of corrupting a value. The real fix is out-of-band provenance: carry `escaped-dollar` and `was-expression` as STRUCTURE beside the value rather than as bytes inside it, which also retires the `TrimStart '\u0001'` and `Replace("\u0000", "$")` sites. Do not widen `isPreambleBanner` unconditionally — the structure gate is anti-spoofing and was put there on purpose |

| FG-134 | **P1** | **DONE** | SEMICOLON-separated steps on one line silently DROPPED the whole stage | `stage('one') { steps { sh 'echo a > a.txt'; sh 'echo b > b.txt' } }` runs NEITHER step and emits nothing — the stage vanishes and the build reports SUCCESS. MEASURED against the host: only the following stage's output appears and only its file exists. Jenkins runs both steps (seen incidentally in an FG-053(b) probe where `sh '...'; unstable('flaky')` traced `+ echo n1` on the Jenkins side). Nothing to do with `unstable` — two plain `sh` steps reproduce it — and nothing to do with nesting, which works. PRE-EXISTING and P1 because it is SILENT: a Jenkinsfile using a perfectly ordinary Groovy statement separator loses work with no diagnostic and a green build, which is the worst outcome this engine can produce. Semicolons between steps are legal Declarative and the corpus should be counted for them before this is scoped. Found while disentangling a confounded probe: a nested case that changed BOTH nesting and semicolons at once looked like a nested-stage defect, and the real bug was the separator. **FIXED**: receipt `steps-semicolon-separated` PROVEN tier-1, suite 114/114, pinning both a same-line pair and a trailing separator. ROOT CAUSE, and it is worse than the symptom: `stepBlock` is `many (attempt stepParser)` between braces, so `many` stopped at the `;`, `between` then demanded `}` and found `;`, and the WHOLE step block failed — but the `steps` section is wrapped in `attempt`, so that failure BACKTRACKED and the section was never picked. `Steps` defaulted to `[]`. The stage therefore ran ZERO steps rather than some of them. **THE GENERAL HAZARD**: in this parser an unhandled syntax form does not fail loudly, it produces an EMPTY SECTION — the `attempt` that makes the grammar composable is what converts a parse error into silent data loss. Any construct the step grammar does not cover degrades to "did nothing, said nothing, passed", which is worth a sweep of its own rather than waiting for the next one to be noticed |
| FG-135 | **P1** | TODO | A stage `retry` CRASHING mid-attempt resumes by skipping the step it needed to re-run | Persisted steps are keyed by `(stage, index)` only. A crash after a failed retry attempt journals `step-finished <stage> 0 failure`, and on resume `Resume.shouldExecute` reports it durably finished — so the resumed build prints `Retrying` for each remaining attempt, NEVER re-runs the shell, and completes failure. Reproduced by the pre-push verifier with a rewound journal: `skip (durably finished): flaky#0` three times. The retry loop has no attempt dimension in the journal, and adding one touches the durability model FG-046b and FG-112 were built on — hook signature, journal format and `Resume` together — so it is NOT a bolt-on to FG-053(b). **NOT SHIPPED — IT FAILS CLOSED UNDER PERSISTENCE, and the second consequence is why.** The verifier's follow-up added one I had not recorded: an `input` before the failing step shares the identity `(stage, index, occurrence)` across attempts, so a PRIOR ATTEMPT'S APPROVAL can be reused for a later retry prompt. A wrong retry count is a bug; replaying a human's approval is the guarantee FG-046b exists to hold, and this project fails closed on that. A stage `retry` in a JOURNALED run is now refused by name with the reason; the differential harness runs without persistence, so the semantics stay receipt-proven and only the durable path is withheld. My earlier judgement, recorded because it was wrong: before FG-053(b) a stage `retry` was REFUSED outright, so those pipelines always failed; now they run correctly and degrade only on crash-resume mid-retry. That is a net improvement, but it is a REAL hole in a headline durability guarantee and must not be read as complete. Likely fix: a failed attempt's steps are not durably finished — do not journal `step-finished` for them, or namespace the records by attempt so a resume re-executes rather than replays **DONE 2026-08-17, the second option, additively.** A `retry-attempt` marker journals before every attempt after the first; `Resume.plan` DROPS a stage's step records at its marker, so dispositions always describe the LATEST attempt, and the loop CONTINUES from the attempt the journal shows started — a marker's absence IS attempt 1, so every pre-existing journal reads unchanged. Proven end to end in the restart lane: crash mid-attempt-2 refuses on the LIVE attempt's interrupted step (pre-fix it skipped it as attempt 1's durably-finished failure and completed failure without re-running anything), and the reconciled resume continues to attempt 3, each attempt's step exactly once across the crash. Six plan-segmentation unit tests. THE REFUSAL NARROWED, NOT DELETED — TWICE: journaled retry is for LEAF stages only, because nested and parallel content runs inside the loop while its steps journal under the NESTED names, which the parent's marker does not supersede — the same defect one level down, found while briefing the verifier and guarded the same hour; and `input` inside a journaled retried stage still refuses by name before any step runs — an approval's identity carries no attempt dimension, and replaying a human's decision is the FG-046b guarantee — with the lane asserting the refusal fires pre-step from the journal by record ABSENCE — this refusal fires before any step exists, so FG-114's `step-reason` (which journals beside a hooked step's finish) does not cover it and its reason text stays non-durable, a named FG-114 residual. FG-136 and FG-137's combination refusals now guard the journaled path too, where the deleted blanket refusal had been shadowing them |

| FG-138 | P3 | **DONE** | A `;` after an UNQUOTED argument value was not a separator | `sh script: 'echo a', returnStatus: true; sh 'echo b'` fails LOUDLY — `completed: failure`, empty workspace — because the raw-argument scanner runs to the newline and swallows the separator. Two of those regressions were silent: `sh script: env.PART + "printf 'x\\"; still literal' > out.txt"` exited 0 with `build-finished success`, NO `step-started` and no file, which is precisely the class FG-134 exists to remove. A loud failure on an uncommon form beats a scanner that produces silent no-ops. THE REAL FIX is not another terminator: `Lexeme` knows more string forms than a hand-rolled scanner does, and `balancedRaw` skips them escape-aware — though NOT completely, as FG-149 later found: it was not triple-quote aware either. A raw argument should consume string spans through THAT, not a fourth hand-rolled character scanner — three of them disagreeing about what a string is was the actual defect the semicolons exposed. **FIXED THAT WAY**: `Lexeme.stringSpanRaw` returns the RAW SOURCE of any Groovy string literal — every form, escapes respected — and sits beside `stringLiteral`, which enumerates the same forms, and `balancedRaw`, which skips single, double and (since FG-149) TRIPLE quoted spans and comments escape-aware, but NOT slashy — gaps this row claimed did not exist until the verifier checked, carried by FG-140 and FG-149. The four raw-argument scanners in `Parser` delegate to it, so `;` can be a stop character without truncating an expression carrying one inside a SINGLE- OR DOUBLE-QUOTED literal. Slashy is NOT covered — `/` stays ordinary raw text after assuming otherwise proved to be an approval bypass (FG-141). Receipt `steps-semicolon-after-raw-arg` PROVEN tier-1, suite 115/115, and PROVEN TO DISCRIMINATE by mutation: with `;` removed from the stop set it DIVERGES while the quoted-pair case stays PROVEN — so the two cases are not redundant. **STILL UNEXPLAINED, and deliberately not claimed**: two probe shapes (`env.P + '; …'` and a slashy `/; …/`) fail both before and after this change, so they are pre-existing and NOT regressions — but those probes also vary `&&` and `>>` inside the environment value, so nothing here establishes that semicolons are involved at all. FG-139 isolates them properly rather than assuming |
| FG-139 | P2 | TODO | An environment value containing `&&` breaks a CONCATENATED raw argument | **REWRITTEN — the first version of this row was wrong, and wrong in the way it warned against.** It blamed semicolons and slashy strings, citing `env.P + '; echo c'` and `env.P + /; echo e/` as failing. Isolating one variable at a time shows both shapes SUCCEED; the failing variable is the ENVIRONMENT VALUE. MEASURED with everything else held constant:\n`P='echo p >> log.txt'` + concat -> success; `P='echo p && '` + concat -> FAILURE, empty workspace; `P='echo p > p.txt && '` + concat -> FAILURE. Each value ALONE (`sh script: env.P`, no concatenation) succeeds, so it is the combination of `&&` in the value AND a concatenated expression. `>>`, `;`, and the string form are all irrelevant. I filed the original row from probes I had already noted were confounded — varying the env value, the operator and the separator together — and then reasoned about them anyway. That is precisely the mistake the FG-134 branch cost five rounds to, restated one ticket later, and it would have sent the next person at semicolons. Caught by the pre-push verifier running the cited shapes and finding they pass |
| FG-140 | P3 | TODO | An UNMATCHED `}` inside a slashy regex is refused | REPRICED ON EVIDENCE, not intuition. Two things changed since it was filed as P1. **The symptom is no longer silent**: the board recorded `no_stages at 1:1`, i.e. a quietly empty pipeline; it now reports `malformed_syntax at 4:5: opaque section` because FG-143/FG-153 turned that class of silent drop into a loud refusal. **The corpus frequency is zero**: the one file whose slashy contains a `}` is `${plugin_warning}` — a BALANCED interpolation, a false positive of my own grep. Balanced braces work today (`==~ /^PR-\d{1,3}$/` runs its stage, measured). What remains is `balancedRaw` counting depth through a slashy span, which needs slashy-aware scanning like the triple-quote fix in FG-149. Real, worth fixing eventually, not P1: nothing in 228 files hits it and the failure is loud | probe `when-unbalanced`; corpus: 0 files |
| FG-141 | **P2** | **DONE** | A slashy literal is unsupported in a raw argument AND in a `script { }` body — `/` is ordinary expression text | Distinguishing `/a}b/` from `10 / 2` needs context the raw-argument scanner does not have, so it treats `/` as ordinary text, which is what it did before FG-138 too. **SECOND SITE FOUND IN PR #53 REVIEW AND MEASURED, which is why this is now P2 rather than P3**: `balancedBody` — the scanner that finds a `script { }` block's closing brace — skips quoted strings and comments but not slashy literals, so a brace INSIDE one ends the block early. `script { def pattern = /}/; sh "printf saw:${pattern}" }` runs on Jenkins and Fogell REJECTS THE WHOLE PIPELINE (`malformed_syntax: opaque section`) — a false refusal of valid Groovy, and a tier-3 rejection rather than a stage failure, so it costs corpus admission wherever it appears. NOT PATCHED, and the reason is the same ambiguity one layer up: a scanner that treats `/` as a span opener swallows `def x = a / b`, and DIVISION IS FAR COMMONER IN A SCRIPT BLOCK THAN A SLASHY CONTAINING A BRACE. Trading a rare false refusal for a common one is a worse bargain, and it is exactly the over-broad fix this branch has already made twice. The two sites share one unsolved problem — slashy-versus-division needs context neither scanner has — so they are one ticket, and whichever fix lands must serve both. **ASSUMING IT WAS A DELIMITER WAS AN APPROVAL BYPASS**, and that is why this is a ticket rather than a quick fix: `input message: 10 / 2` sent `/` into `stringSpanRaw`, no closing delimiter was found, the argument failed to parse, `steps` is wrapped in `attempt` so the failure BACKTRACKED, the section became EMPTY, and the build reported SUCCESS having never published a prompt. MEASURED against merged main, which runs the gate and waits (step-started=1, prompts=1) where the change exited 0 with neither. A human gate silently skipped is the guarantee FG-046b exists to hold. Three things compounded: a feature nobody previously had, an assumption that cannot fail loudly (`/` is always a delimiter), and a parser whose failure mode is silent emptiness — the same `attempt`-swallows-failures mechanism as FG-134, reaching an approval gate. Any future attempt needs the slashy/division decision made where context exists, and an approval-lane case for `input message: 10 / 2` so a regression cannot pass silently **DONE 2026-08-18.** The context both scanners lacked is ONE CHARACTER OF LOOKBEHIND: after something that can END an expression (an identifier character, a closing bracket, a string) a `/` is division; with no left operand it can only open a slashy — the position rule Groovy itself lexes by. `Lexeme.endsExpression` is the one shared definition; `balancedRaw` tracks the last significant character as it scans (skipped string spans count as expression enders), and `rawArgValue` looks back one token at each `/`. An unterminated candidate span falls back to the ordinary character — the exact pre-fix reading, so the fix can only remove derailments — and in a raw argument the span must close on its own line, so a typo cannot hunt across statements for a closer. Both directions held by the lane: scenario W (`input message: 10 / 2`, this row's demanded case, present since FG-143) still gates, and Z2/Z3 — previously REFUSAL scenarios using slashy as the canonical unparseable form — are now POSITIVE: the parenthesised and mixed-positional slashy gates publish their EVALUATED words (`Deploy; prod`, `Deploy { prod`) and wait, which kills the historical raw-body-as-prompt downgrade harder than refusal did; scenario Z keeps the parse-time refusal class with an unterminated literal. Proven against the pinned Jenkins by three new tier-1 receipts — `script-slashy-brace` (the `def pattern = /}/` shape this row measured as falsely refused), `when-slashy-brace` (`==~ /a}b/` in an expression body), `echo-slashy-concat` (mid-expression slashy past a stop character) — suite 190/190. Seven position-rule unit tests. THE LANE REWRITE ALSO EXPOSED A LATENT GROOVY-PARSER BUG, fixed same pass: the slashy literal was the one primary not `lexeme`-wrapped, so ANY operator after a slashy failed to chain (`/Deploy; / + env.TARGET` stopped dead at the `+`); it hid because every existing receipt put `)` flush against the closing delimiter. The two SCANNER fixes moved no corpus file — measured, and consistent with FG-180's finding that no rejected file was blocked on slashy alone — but the latent LEXEME fix recovered `varunpalekar_php-ci-cd`, one of FG-180's five (admitted 183): its `no line break before '['` error text had been attributed on this board to FG-190 + FG-192, and the missing trailing-trivia skip after a slashy was the real cause — the postfix-index guard fired only because the slashy never consumed the space before the bracket. A wrong attribution corrected by a fix aimed elsewhere, which is the strongest form of the by-eye lesson this pair of tickets keeps teaching. RESIDUALS, each measured by the verifier and refusal-direction: dollar-slashy `$/…/$` stays unparsed everywhere, refused at evaluation; a KEYWORD before a slashy is judged division — a keyword's final letter is an identifier character, so `return /a}b/` in a script body leaves the slashy unprotected and the extraction derails to a LOUD false refusal, the exact pre-fix behaviour (zero corpus incidence, and the same class this ticket closed — named here so its next instance starts from this sentence); and `balancedRaw`'s candidate span hunts across newlines where `rawArgValue`'s is line-bounded — unreachable harm today (the only false-opener shape, postfix `++` division, is refused by the expression grammar anyway), worth a guard if postfix increment ever lands there |

A concatenated prompt message is an ordinary thing to write, which is what makes this P1. FIXED: the string-literal branch may claim an argument's value only when NO OPERATOR follows it, otherwise the raw scanner takes the whole expression including its quoted spans. **THE FIRST FORMULATION OF THIS GUARD BROKE FG-122 AND TOOK THE SUITE TO 111/115.** It whitelisted TERMINATORS (`,)}\n;`), but `stringLiteralWithKindBoth` is `lexeme`-wrapped and had ALREADY consumed the trailing newline, so the guard saw the NEXT STEP'S IDENTIFIER, rejected the literal, and sent `sh 'printf "\\033[31m"'` through the raw scanner UNDECODED — the two engines running different commands again. Only NON-LAST steps broke, because `}` and `;` were in the whitelist and an identifier was not. TWO wrong fixes preceded the real one, each blaming whitespace handling I had not measured and each written into a code comment as MEASURED. The lesson is the FORMULATION: a whitelist of what may follow an argument is only as complete as my list of what may follow an argument, and a negative check on continuation operators does not depend on that list being complete GUARDED by approval-lane scenarios X (named) AND Y (positional), each proven to fail by mutation. **THE FIX LANDED ONE BRANCH AT A TIME AND THE ROW CLAIMED IT WAS COMPLETE AFTER THE FIRST.** `wholeValue` went on the NAMED argument branch, so `input "Deploy " + env.TARGET` — positional, as common a spelling — still shipped past the gate; scenario X could not catch it because X exercises `input message: …`, and X CLAIMED to cover "a concatenation" while testing only the named form. Three routes now: FG-141's `10 / 2` via the raw scanner, and this one via the named and positional literal branches. Each time I fixed the instance in front of me and described the class, on an approval bypass. **IT IS A SECOND ROUTE, NOT A SECOND INSTANCE**: FG-141's `input message: 10 / 2` starts with a digit and reaches the raw scanner, while a quoted prefix is claimed by the literal branch first — scenario W could not catch this shape and X could not catch W's. Both end in the same silently empty `steps` section, the FG-134 hazard reaching an approval gate twice by different paths. A case only ever proves the route it takes |

| FG-047 | P1 | **DONE** | `stash` / `unstash`, controller-side storage surviving `deleteDir()` | `stash-unstash` receipt PROVEN tier-1, and the property that matters is the one tested: the stash is stored with the BUILD (outside the workspace), so it survives `deleteDir()`. Storing it under the workspace would pass a naive round-trip and fail this. A missing stash name FAILS rather than silently restoring nothing. `deleteDir()` implemented alongside, since the test is meaningless without it. Writing this receipt is what exposed the step-swallowing parser bug (FG-010b) |
| FG-048 | P1 | **DONE** | `when` conditions incl. `expression` via the bounded interpreter | Two receipts PROVEN tier-1. `when-conditions`: `expression { return false }` skips, `environment name/value` matches and mismatches correctly, `allOf` + `not` composes; a skipped stage leaves the build **SUCCESS** and its **`post` block does not run** (both measured). `when-scm-and-equals`: MEASURED that on a plain job Jenkins **skips** `branch` and `tag` stages because `BRANCH_NAME`/`TAG_NAME` do not exist — which is what lets those two be modelled rather than refused, and they are the corpus's heaviest `when` users. `equals` is a plain comparison. **Two parser bugs found by receipt:** `when { environment name: 'X', value: 'Y' }` did not parse at all (the code expected `environment X = 'Y'`, which Jenkins rejects), and an unparseable `when` fell through the stage's generic section fallback so the stage ran **unconditionally** — silently running a stage Jenkins skips, the worst failure mode available. A backstop now records any unrecognised `when` as unmodelled so evaluation fails closed. Unmodelled conditions: FG-048b |
| FG-049 | P1 | **DONE** | `post` blocks: correct *selection*, not just presence — Jenkins prints all branches in the console | Two receipts PROVEN tier-1 (`post-order-failure`, `post-order-success`), each declaring all nine conditions so the receipt proves which fire AND in what order. Selection and order were MEASURED across four consecutive builds of one job, not read from documentation: order is **always → changed → fixed → regression → result-arm → cleanup**, and `changed` **fires on a first build** (no previous result to differ from) — not what I would have guessed. Stage post is selected against the STAGE result, pipeline post against the BUILD result. Before this ticket the walker parsed `post` and then **ignored it entirely**. `changed`/`fixed`/`regression` on later builds: FG-049b |
| FG-050 | P2 | TODO | `agent { label }` / `agent { docker }` and capability matching | **RE-MEASURED: 26 files use `agent { label }`, 13 use `agent { docker }`.** A raw grep for `docker` hits 70 files, but 3 of those are `sh 'docker …'` — shell usage, not a directive. Counting the construct, not the word, is the difference between a P1 and a P2 |
| FG-051 | P3 | TODO | `tools` registry with auto-provision (7 corpus files failed *Jenkins* on this) | **RE-MEASURED: 9 files.** Demoted to P3: Jenkins itself fails these, so no parity receipt is obtainable — it is a "beat Jenkins" ticket, not a compatibility one, and the compatibility number does not move |
| FG-052 | **P1** | **DONE** | `checkout scm` and the `git` step | **RE-MEASURED: 49 files use `checkout scm`, 30 use the `git` step, 8 use `checkout([...])`.** The board said 15 — a 3x undercount that had this rat… [→ detail](tickets/FG-052.md) |
| FG-053 | **P1** | **DONE** | `options` (declarative directives beyond `timeout`) | All three slices merged: (a) PR #38 — accept the inert four, refuse everything Jenkins accepts whose semantics this engine does not reproduce; (b) PR #39 — stage `retry`, `skipStagesAfterUnstable`, and the `unstable('msg')` step the latter could not exist without; (c) PRs #34/#35 — `timestamps` and `ansiColor`. Suite 107 -> 113. Row marked DONE one session late: the slices were closed individually and the parent kept its TODO, which is the same stale-status defect this board has caught in comments all session. | **RE-DERIVED 2026-08-03** with a comment-stripping brace matcher over all 228 files (`scripts/count-options.bb`, committed — the scratchpad copy that produced the first numbers still has the broken `stage ` matcher and must not be cited), because a naive `rg -l` over-counts — the first sampled file carries `//retry(3)`, commented out. **32** files carry an `options` block. BY DIRECTIVE (pipeline/stage split in brackets): `buildDiscarder` 18 [18/0], `timestamps` 14 [14/0] (DONE, PR #34), `timeout` 14 [12/2] (DONE, FG-045), `disableConcurrentBuilds` 6 [6/0], `ansiColor` 4 [4/0] (DONE, PR #35), `retry` 2 [0/2], `skipDefaultCheckout` 2 [2/0] (DONE, receipt `checkout-scm-skip-default`), `parallelsAlwaysFailFast` 1 [1/0], `quietPeriod` 1 [1/0], `skipStagesAfterUnstable` 1 [1/0], `rateLimitBuilds` 1 [1/0] — **eleven** directives, 64 occurrences. **CORRECTED 2026-08-04, and the previous numbers were MINE and wrong**: this row said "33-35 files" and listed NINE directives, omitting `parallelsAlwaysFailFast` and `quietPeriod` entirely — neither word appeared anywhere on the board. `scripts/count-options.bb` has not changed since the commit that re-derived these (e4a5f47), so running it now IS the earlier run: I transcribed the directives I already expected and did not notice the counter had emitted two more rows, then vouched for the result as promoted "from corroborating to reliable". Re-derive every row from the corrected output, not the rows you went looking for. Five of these matched the 2026-08-02 count exactly and three differ by one. The counter's over-run — a quoted `options` starting a block at some distant brace, which emitted `steps`/`bat`/`try` as directives — was fixed on review by requiring the `{` to be the NEXT non-whitespace character; the junk entries disappeared and every real count above was UNCHANGED, which is what promotes these from corroborating to reliable. **TWO CORRECTIONS THAT ARE NOT NOISE:** `milestone` is a STEP, not an option — ZERO occurrences in any `options` block, 6 in stage bodies — so it was listed here in error and is NOT part of this ticket; and `rateLimitBuilds` was never listed. **STAGE-LEVEL OPTIONS DO EXIST**, and an earlier version of this row said they did not: the counter matched the literal `stage ` and so missed `stage('x')`, the form Declarative actually uses, reporting every stage-level block as pipeline-level. Caught by the pre-push verifier, which reproduced it. Corrected split: `timeout` is 12 pipeline / **2 stage**, and `retry` is **0 pipeline / 2 stage — entirely stage-level**. `timestamps`, `buildDiscarder`, `disableConcurrentBuilds` and `ansiColor` remain pipeline-only, so FG-120's refusal of stage-level `timestamps()` still costs nothing — but that is now a MEASURED statement about one directive rather than a sweeping one about the corpus. Split by what a RECEIPT can see: **(a) no observable effect on one build** — `buildDiscarder` and `disableConcurrentBuilds` are retention and queueing policy, 23 files between them, so accept-and-ignore WITH THE REASON STATED is the whole job; **(b) IS NOW DONE** — `retry` (2 files, both STAGE-level) and `skipStagesAfterUnstable` (1), suite 111/111. Receipts `options-stage-retry`, `options-stage-retry-exhausted`, `options-skip-after-unstable`, `options-unstable-runs-on`. Stage `retry` runs on the SAME `runWithRetry` the `retry(N) { }` step uses — extracted, not copied, so the measured subtleties it already carries (a human REJECTION is not retried, a nested one propagates to an enclosing retry, a nested `timeout` abort IS retried) did not have to be rediscovered; the step's own receipts passing is what says the extraction was behaviour-preserving. **`skipStagesAfterUnstable` NEEDED A PREREQUISITE NOBODY HAD NOTICED**: this engine had NO way to make a build UNSTABLE from inside a pipeline — no `unstable(msg)` step, no `junit`; the `unstable` at `Fogell.fs:838` parses a trace RESULT STRING and the existing `unstable { }` cases are POST CONDITIONS. The option's branch would have been unreachable, looking implemented and never exercised, which is the FG-129 shape. `unstable('msg')` is implemented alongside: prints `WARNING: msg`, sets the result, EXECUTION CONTINUES. The two skip cases are a PAIR — same pipeline with and without the option — which is what makes it a measurement of the OPTION rather than of unstable handling generally, and is how the missing prerequisite was found: the CONTROL diverged too. `milestone` was listed here and is NOT an option — zero occurrences in any options block, 6 as a step in stage bodies; **(a) IS NOW DONE** — receipt `options-accept-and-ignore` PROVEN tier-1 (suite 107/107) pins ONE thing: that `buildDiscarder`, `disableConcurrentBuilds`, `quietPeriod` and `rateLimitBuilds` are accepted with no single-build effect, in their VALID forms. An FG-014 parse-only preflight later regressed that exact case by globally refusing named collections; the closed pipeline-option descriptors now own both supported names and the sole collection exception (`rateLimitBuilds` / `throttle`) without widening other names, arguments or scopes. **It does not prove the refusals** — this row said it pinned both, and a receipt for a compile-shaped refusal cannot exist (FG-129). The refusal of unknown names, wrong-scope names and known-but-unimplemented names is measured and UNPROVEN BY RECEIPT. Measured before the fix: `options { notARealOption() }` gave jenkins=failure against fogell=success, because options dispatch by name-filter and anything unmatched fell through. **The allowlist is JENKINS' OWN** — its refusal enumerates every accepted type, which is the evidence FG-126 says an allowlist needs; a hand-rolled one would refuse working pipelines to fix nothing. It is PLUGIN-DEPENDENT (`withAWS`, `podTemplate`, `dockerNode` are plugin-provided) so it is pinned to the lab's 2.568.1, not a claim about Jenkins in general. **THREE-WAY, default refuse** (corrected on review): HONOURED **per (name, SCOPE)** — at pipeline scope timeout, timestamps, ansiColor, skipDefaultCheckout, parallelsAlwaysFailFast; at STAGE scope only `timeout`, because the orchestrator calls `deadlineFromOptions stage.Options` and reads none of the others there. A first cut honoured by NAME and let any supported option into a stage block, so `stage { options { ansiColor('xterm') } }` ran to success with TERM=dumb — the verifier built the pipeline and read TERM. That is the same conflation as the allowlist one, on the other axis: name-vs-behaviour fixed, name-vs-scope left; INERT = the four above; everything else Jenkins accepts is REFUSED, because accepting a name whose SEMANTICS this engine does not reproduce is the silent divergence FG-103 exists to prevent. The first cut of this allowlist held every name Jenkins knows, which conflated "Jenkins accepts the NAME" with "Fogell implements the BEHAVIOUR" — `checkoutToSubdirectory('src')` moves the automatic checkout and was accepted and ignored. SCOPE IS MEASURED TOO: Jenkins enumerates a DIFFERENT valid set for a stage `options` block and refuses pipeline-only names there, so `stage { options { buildDiscarder(...) } }` is jenkins=failure where one global list ran the build. **TWO COSTS, both measured and neither hidden**: `checkoutToSubdirectory` and the other Jenkins-known options this engine does not implement are refused rather than silently ignored; **`retry` and `skipStagesAfterUnstable` were in that set and are NOT any more — FG-053(b) implements both**, and this sentence said they were still refused for one commit after it landed. What remains refused around them is three COMBINATIONS (FG-135 journaled runs, FG-136 nested + skip, FG-137 stage post), not the options themselves. Also: refusal CREATES divergence where an option is contextually inert, measured as `checkoutToSubdirectory` with no SCM giving jenkins=success fogell=failure. That is the price of refuse-don't-diverge and it should be read as a deliberate choice, not an oversight. The refusal itself is UNPROVEN BY RECEIPT and cannot currently be otherwise — see FG-129, which is one harness limit explaining why FG-121, FG-126 and this all lack receipts. **(c) console-shaped** — `timestamps` (14) prefixes build-output lines and `ansiColor` (4) wraps it. **`timestamps` DONE** — receipts `options-timestamps` and `options-timestamps-compact`. The caution was right: the contract said *"excluded: timestamps"* while no code stripped one, true only because no case used the option. Now the prefix is stripped ONLY when the parsed script declares `timestamps()` (an unconditional strip collapsed a build's own `[ISO-8601] value` output in every case), the engine emits it, and coverage is compared as none/partial/all over the exact final compared-survivor set (FG-118). This reconciliation did not rerun Jenkins or regenerate the three historical timestamp receipts; their bytes and original evidence remain unchanged and must not be cited as a fresh exact-survivor run. MEASURED and contrary to what three comments claimed: Jenkins stamps BUILD-OUTPUT lines only, not its own `[Pipeline]` annotations, banners or Started/Finished rows (2 of 21 raw lines on a two-step build). Remaining in (c): `ansiColor` (4 files). Highest-demand UNBLOCKED ticket, and cheaper than 33 files sounds: it leads Wave 4 |

All five routes return 401 unauthenticated. Malformed pipeline → 422 with code **and** source position. Submit 201 fresh / 200 replay. Non-UUID → 400, unknown build → 404, never 500. 15 tests |
| FG-061 | P0 | **DONE** | Scheduler: capability-filtered claim, FIFO within pool, wait diagnostics | Tenant advisory lock + `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`: **16 concurrent schedulers over 4 attempts yield exactly 4 claims, none twice**. Capability containment checked in SQL (`<@`), not application code. FIFO by admission order. Wait diagnostics distinguish empty queue / trust-pool mismatch / **named missing capabilities** |

| FG-064 | P1 | **DONE** | Progressive console over the API (`adr/0005` match) | `GET …/logs?from=N` returns chunks plus a `next_sequence` cursor, so a client tails a running build rather than waiting for completion |
| FG-224 | P0 | **PARTIAL** | Runnable, restart-discovered single-node controller | Local exact-tree acceptance closes the review findings: outer Run.Host cleanup binds every leader/group signal to the launcher's captured Linux birth identity and fails closed on PID/PGID reuse; durable raw-byte idempotency is resolved before decoding or parsing, so an exact malformed legacy replay remains 200 while changed malformed bytes remain the bound key's 409 conflict; and invalid-definition quarantine atomically moves attempt → node → build with exactly one reasoned event/outbox pair. Review25 closes Codex `3866957698`: shutdown interrupts the active poll promptly and retains the durable `controller_shutdown` disposition. It also closes `3866957703`: readiness detects a missing or unwritable state root beyond startup through lazy ordered dependency checks and a lock-protected one-second cache; the worker checks cached readiness before claiming, forces a fresh probe after an offer, and fenced-requeues an unstarted offer on failure, so cached success never authorizes materialization after storage loss. Review26 closes Codex `3867130539`: after an offer, the lazy launcher-first guard performs the fresh state-root probe only when launchers are available, and either dependency loss makes one immediate fenced `RequeueOwnedAttempt` before logging. The unstarted attempt/node/build return to `queued` without materialization, execution, reconciliation, event, or outbox publication; the exact owner/fence/unexpired-lease/current-restore-epoch contract applies in attempt → node → build lock order, a replacement advances the fence, and concurrent cancellation remains set for it to observe. Review27 closes Codex `3867267809`: `RequireReconciliation` now needs the exact owner/fence, live lease, and current restore epoch, so expired or pre-restore workers publish nothing while the lease scanner or restore path owns disposition. It also closes `3867267816`: whitespace-only required configuration, including the local trust pool, refuses startup before admission. Review28 closes Codex `3867404726`: after `BeginExecution` but before any `Process.Start`, a setup failure disposes partial process ownership and performs one fenced requeue before logging, restoring queued lineage without reconciliation publication; an actual attempted launch remains conservatively reasoned reconciliation. Review29 closes Codex `3867560435`: after natural exit, verified extinction, and a complete drain, final-refresh cancellation delegates to Store terminal arbitration and publishes `aborted` when cancellation won; shutdown, lease loss, or incomplete drain still reconcile. Review31 closes Codex `3867886652`: a fresh retry now atomically reopens its node/build, migration 0011 repairs pre-existing queued/offered retry children whose aggregate still carries the parent result across forced RLS and publishes a build-bound compensation, and the FG-224 claimant requires all three durable levels to be queued; cancellation after reopening aborts before launch. Review32 closes Codex `3868139456`: cancellation first observed between bounded slices after natural exit and verified producer extinction no longer truncates the frozen event boundary or forces reconciliation; the finite tail drains exactly once to EOF and `PublishTerminal` arbitrates `aborted`, while shutdown, lease loss, incomplete evidence, and a captured forced exit remain reconciliation boundaries. Review33 closes Codex `3868227937` and `3868291517`: fresh controller admission rejects every `input` that is not provably deadline-bounded before binding a build or idempotency key, because Run.Host's standalone filesystem inbox is not an authenticated broker against build code sharing its OS identity; and startup actively proves runtime/maintenance capabilities reach the same live PostgreSQL database with a random cross-session advisory-lock challenge rather than trusting aliases or matching role/schema metadata. The adjacent lease-expiry audit remains closed: expired `accepted`/`running`/`finalizing`/`cancelling` attempts atomically emit one `lease_expired` pair, while safe `offered` → `queued` recovery emits neither. Engine-created inner step groups are registered before release and reaped across Run.Host death; progressive publication is FIFO-serialized outside the output lock; and ambiguous post-launch exits enter reconciliation with a stable reason event and outbox while retaining the event fence until terminal publication. The fresh-database proof uses a real `NOSUPERUSER NOBYPASSRLS` role and crosses HTTP admission → immutable definition → restarted discovery → supervised whole-pipeline execution → fenced progressive logs → atomic attempt/node/build/event/outbox terminal truth, plus graceful-shutdown reconciliation and byte-exact recovery evidence. Controller API is 36/36, Differential is 279/279, Store is 96/96, and the aggregate locked gate is 920/920. PR #148 merged exact source head `6ab95958fa1d77c9a994a4e07de90c09df7a5d7a` as `20d4b6382df26c17113b8e73d3f613f9e54da2ae` after both exact-head hosted `gate` checks passed. Codex covered that head, but `scripts/review-coverage.py --pr 148` reports Copilot reviewed only an earlier commit; the repository's exact-head review rule therefore keeps this PARTIAL despite merge. The single-node Linux and explicit residual boundaries remain — [→ detail](tickets/FG-224.md) |

## Wave 6 — Security

| id | pri | status | item | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FG-070 | P0 | **DONE** | Secret delivery: value bound as Jenkins binds it, plus a file companion | **Re-scoped.** **Measured first:** a secret in the environment is readable from `/proc/<pid>/environ` by any process running as the same user, for the whole life… [→ detail](tickets/FG-070.md) |
| FG-071 | P0 | **DONE** | Masking is defence-in-depth and **never silent** (`adr/0005` beat) | **Now genuinely wired to the output path.** A Codex review on PR #11 found the masker and detector were reachable only from their own tests — `Executor.runShell` forwarded `OnLine` and returned stdout/stderr untouched, so a step running `cat "$TOKEN_FILE"` streamed the literal secret while this row claimed masking was done. Masking now happens on the **streaming** path and the buffered stdout/stderr, and three tests fail if that wiring is removed. Masks the same forms Jenkins was measured to handle (literal, base64, case-folded), and uppercase hex was added to the detector after a Copilot review found only lowercase was generated. It does **not** mask reverse/hex/char-split — masking every encoding is impossible — but it **detects** them and names the encoding. That is the difference between a known gap and a silent one: Jenkins leaks these with the build green and no warning |
| FG-072 | P0 | **DONE** | Interpreter sandbox assurance — [→ detail](tickets/FG-072.md) | Exact schema for every type reachable from `Value`; named-inventory and generic fallback denials through the real host; successor halt and sanctioned-call controls; thirteen independent known-bad states |
| FG-073 | P1 | TODO | Threat model document: untrusted Jenkinsfile, hostile agent, multi-tenant boundary | reviewed and committed |
| FG-074 | P1 | **PARTIAL** | Dependency audit + project-local NuGet lock graphs. Signed local commit `485bf59ff45d5124850920efd099f10b60db936e` (tree `f717179223d8fda5fe20d9e878f972111ba9eceb`) is the pre-reconciliation input; the status follow-up is included in the current signed local candidate, whose amended identity is bound in external handoff rather than self-referenced here. The 28-path implementation boundary is `Directory.Build.props`, one blocking proof, its build-gate wiring, and 25 `packages.lock.json` files for the 25 solution projects. The proof reconciles the repo-wide project/solution/lock inventory (with named generated-tree prunes), evaluates both lock properties on every project, populates a new empty isolated cache through a network-enabled locked restore, regenerates and compares every lock with sources cleared, then performs a source-cleared locked restore and `--no-restore` build against that cache. Seven planted states are rejected: two non-first-project policy overrides, an out-of-solution project, a missing lock, PackageReference drift, content-hash tampering, and dependency-edge tampering. This is bounded source-cleared warm-cache evidence, not OS no-egress, a whole-gate offline/cold-airgap result, vulnerability freshness, an SBOM/provenance claim, or an attestation of cache/package trust. `global.json` disables roll-forward and pins SDK `10.0.301` exactly; changing the SDK-owned `FSharp.Core` graph therefore requires an intentional SDK change and lock regeneration. The exact pre-reconciliation commit passed the authoritative full HeMan gate, including every blocking lane. Exact-head formal review/coverage, CI, publication, and merge remain | 25/25 project locks; empty→populated isolated cache; source-cleared locked restore and no-restore build pass; all seven proof mutations reject [→ detail](tickets/FG-074.md) |

## Wave 8 — Release gates and differential evidence

| id | pri | status | item | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FG-090 | P0 | **DONE** | Compatibility scorecard generator: per-file tiers from `adr/0001`, machine-readable ledger, **no scalar percentage** | `scripts/generate-scorecard.bb` runs the corpus gate FIRST, scores parse-only (corpus is untrusted third-party code and is never executed), classifies each file and writes `docs/COMPATIBILITY-LEDGER.tsv` + `docs/COMPATIBILITY-SCORECARD.md`. AT LANDING, the measured corpus claims were "tier1=1", "tier3=44", and "admitted=183" (parse-only, not an ADR tier); the generated ledger is the current source. **ADR TIER 2 IS NOT ASSESSED** — the ADR defines it as parses AND EXECUTES, and nothing here executes a corpus file, so not every file maps to an ADR tier. This row said "maps each file to an ADR-0001 tier" and "tier2=149" for one commit AFTER the scorecard stopped saying it: the ninth instance of fixing the artifact and the code and leaving the row that describes them. **THE TRAP ADR 0001 WARNS ABOUT WAS LIVE HERE, AND THE NUMBERS BELOW ARE THE STATE AT LANDING**: there were then 118 receipts and 228 corpus files with a NAME OVERLAP OF ZERO — every receipt proved a hand-written case. Printed together they invited 118/228 = 52% when proven corpus parity was 0 of 228, the prior engine's 146-vs-5 error exactly. (CORRECTED 2026-08-17 when FG-200 made the overlap ONE and the sentence stale as a live claim — the ledger's `tier1=1` is the live number, and this row's figures are its landing-day history. Raised by Codex on PR #94.) The two populations are therefore separate sections, each count stating its own denominator, and no ratio is computed anywhere. Wired into the gate as `--check` — **but only where the corpus is mounted**. CI excludes corpus work by design, so on GitHub the check does not run and a stale artifact would pass; drift is caught on luigi/HeMan only. This row first said "a stale scorecard fails the build", which claimed more than the code does, and the skip branch was already there when I wrote it | ledger + scorecard, gate-checked where the corpus exists |
| FG-091 | P0 | **DONE** | `KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.md` generated from the ledger, not hand-written | Generated by the same script, grouped by the parser's own MESSAGE rather than its code — all 79 rejections carry the single code `malformed_syntax`, which names nothing a reader can act on. Grouping by message turns the corpus into a RANKED WORK LIST: `opaque section` 52 files, then the expression-grammar gaps. Denominators stated on the page; tier 2 is explicitly NOT a parity claim | docs/KNOWN-LIMITATIONS.md, regenerated by the gate |
| FG-092 | P0 | **DONE** | Differential receipt count is the headline metric | Published in the scorecard as its own section with its own denominator: 118 proven of 118 receipts, over the hand-written case suite. Stated adjacent to, and explicitly NOT divided by, the 228-file corpus. FG-090's comment carries the reason a reader must not combine them | scorecard, case-suite section |
| FG-161 | P2 | **DONE** | Nothing verifies a differential receipt's SEAL | FIXED: `--verify-seals` on the differential CLI recomputes every receipt's seal from the receipt's own content, wired into the gate. It hashes through `Compare.sealedContent`, the SAME function the writer uses — the hash rule is not reimplemented, because this file already records three copies of the timestamp rule drifting apart and a fourth in babashka would be that defect by construction. Only EXTRACTION from the rendered text is new, and a wrong extraction mismatches loudly rather than passing quietly. **THE SEAL DID NOT BIND THE VERDICT** — found by testing the exact attack this row describes: editing `VERDICT: PROVEN (tier 1)` to `VERDICT: DIVERGED (7)` on a real receipt left the seal VALID, and the scorecard classifies a receipt as proven by reading that line. The one edit the entire published count rests on was the one edit the seal ignored; derived-therefore-safe is only true if something re-derives it, and nothing did. The verdict block is now sealed. Shipping the verifier without this would have put a green check in front of the hole. Receipts now record `case-digest:` and `sealed-output:`. The digest makes verification self-contained — otherwise a verifier must map receipt→case, and `foo.b1`/`foo.b2` both come from one multi-build case (the reversal FG-166 files as a defect) — and it SPLITS the check: seal integrity needs no case files at all, so it runs in CI where the differential cannot; case freshness stays with the mtime warning. `sealed-output` states which order was hashed, rather than coupling a hash check to the wording of a sentence in the contract block. **AND THEN THE FIX ITSELF HAD THE SAME SHAPE OF HOLE.** The seal binds the FIRST verdict block, but `generate-scorecard.bb` classifies tier 1 with a regex matching ANY line — so APPENDING one `VERDICT: PROVEN (tier 1)` line to a diverged receipt left `--verify-seals` reporting VALID and the scorecard promoting it. Measured on when-conditions.receipt.txt. Raised by the pre-push verifier's review, which returned NOT SAFE TO PUSH on the commit that added seal verification: the check that closes a hole shipped with a green tick in front of it. FIXED by refusing DUPLICATE fields outright — every reader here and in the scorecard takes the FIRST match, so a second copy of any field is unsealed text a later reader can be steered to. Refused as a CLASS (title, core, seal, case-digest, sealed-output, verdict, both side headings, contract) rather than special-casing the verdict, and not by teaching the scorecard which block is sealed — that would put 'which verdict counts' in two languages, the split this design exists to avoid. **AND A THIRD TIME, SAME SHAPE.** `sealed-output` decides HOW the verifier hashes the output, and it was not itself sealed — an unsealed field steering verification is a key under the mat. A `sequence` receipt whose output happens to be sorted could be relabelled `multiset` without breaking the seal, and from then on its output could be reordered freely and still verify; substantiated by the verifier on archive-empty-fails.receipt.txt, where original, mode-flipped and mode-flipped-plus-reordered all recomputed the same stored seal. The mode is now in the hash. THREE ROUNDS OF THE SAME LESSON in one ticket: anything a later reader keys off must be bound, whether it is the verdict a scorecard reads, a duplicate field a regex may find, or a mode the verifier obeys. **AND A FOURTH.** `String.concat` serialises `[]` and `[""]` identically, so a receipt with a ZERO-output side could gain a blank output line without breaking its seal — while the contract in the same file promised that adding or dropping a line breaks it. The line COUNT is now in the hash. FOUR ROUNDS OF ONE LESSON in a single ticket, each found by the pre-push verifier and none by me: anything a later reader keys off must be bound — the verdict a scorecard reads, a duplicate field a regex may find, a mode the verifier obeys, and a delimiter that cannot tell 'no elements' from 'one empty element'. Three of the four were introduced BY the fix for the previous one. **AND A FIFTH.** `output (N lines):` is a printed fact the seal does not bind — it binds the payload — so editing it to 999 left verification passing while the receipt read as something it was not. Now CHECKED rather than sealed: the count is derived from the payload, so the honest test is that the document agrees with itself. FIVE ROUNDS OF ONE LESSON in a single ticket, every one found by the pre-push verifier and none by me, and most introduced BY the fix for the one before: anything a later reader keys off must be bound or cross-checked — the verdict a scorecard reads, a duplicate field a regex may find, a mode the verifier obeys, a delimiter that cannot tell 'no elements' from 'one empty element', and a printed count nothing compared to the thing it counts. THE PATTERN, stated because it will recur: I kept sealing the CONTENT and leaving the METADATA ABOUT the content unbound, then writing a contract sentence that claimed both. **AND A SIXTH.** The duplicate-field refusal covered TOP-LEVEL receipt fields and stopped there, so a second visible `result: failure` line INSIDE a side block still verified — the reader takes the first match and hashes that, while the document shows two conflicting engine results. Second time in this ticket that a fix was scoped to the level I happened to be looking at rather than to the rule. **AND A SEVENTH, which is why FG-169 exists.** The comparison contract, the printed workspace file listing and the engine notes are rendered, trusted and NOT bound — editing a contract line or a printed workspace hash leaves every seal matching. Substantiated by the pre-push verifier. NOT FIXED HERE by binding three more fields, because that is the move that produced the previous six rounds; the receipt and the scorecard now NAME these regions as unsealed instead, and FG-169 carries the redesign that ends the class. **AND AN EIGHTH**: `timestamps():` was missing from the duplicate-field list while that list was described as closing the duplicate class — a class-level fix one member short, which is the same failure as scoping a fix to the level you happen to be looking at. **AND A NINTH, from PR review**: a VALID receipt COPIED onto another case's name verified fine — the seal binds which case was compared, nothing bound WHERE the document sits, and `generate-scorecard.bb` attributes evidence by the ON-DISK filename. Copying any proven receipt over another case name granted that case tier 1 from a run that never tested it. Verification now checks the on-disk name against the name the sealed title derives, through ONE `receiptFileName` used by writer and reader (so FG-163 fixes both or neither). Also fixed from the same review: `Describe` called `Substring(0, 12)` on a possibly-short seal and would have thrown out of a checker whose job is to report cleanly, and both new gate invocations now pass `-c Release --no-build` like the rest of the gate. **AND A TENTH**: `(did not run)` was trusted as a SUMMARY of a side block rather than checked against it, so a NOT COMPARABLE receipt — whose seal binds `<none>` for the absent side — could be edited to display full fake side evidence and still verify. The marker is now exclusive. **ROUNDS 11-13 WERE DISCLOSURE DRIFT, NOT BYPASSES** — the bypass class stopped at the tenth. The list of unsealed regions lived in THREE places (receipt contract, scorecard prose, proof-lane header) and drifted in four consecutive rounds: each fix completed the copy I was editing and left the others short, then the next round found the gap. Collapsed to ONE `unsealedRegions` list in `Compare`, with the scorecard now POINTING at the receipt instead of restating it — the same move that ended the fallback drift and the board-number drift, applied four rounds later than it should have been. The completed list also admits a real gap found on the way: the timestamps() line's COUNTS are printed but only its none/partial/all CLASSIFICATION is sealed, so `all (2)` may be edited to `all (999)`. **ROUND 14 FOUND A VACUOUS ARM IN THIS LANE ITSELF**: arm 9's mutation was unchecked, and `set -uo pipefail` does not abort on a failed heredoc — so if the planting silently failed, the arm verified the ORIGINAL receipt and reported 'accepted'. An ACCEPT arm is exactly where that hides: a rejection arm fails loudly when nothing was planted. Both accept arms now assert the file actually changed, and the guard is mutation-proven (neuter the planting, the arm fails). A checker that cannot fail is the defect this lane was written to prevent, found inside the lane. **THE LENIENT-PARSER FAMILY, CLOSED.** Three bypasses of one shape reached review — an unindented line inserted inside a sealed section, arbitrary text riding behind a recognised coverage word (`StartsWith "all"` accepted `alligator`), and a FABRICATED `## Output comparison notes (1)` heading over a two-space line. Root cause was that extraction collected what it recognised and IGNORED THE REST, so anything it did not understand rode along unbound — and 'not recognised' is exactly where tampering lives. FIXED by ACCOUNTING FOR EVERY LINE: each nonblank line must match a shape the reader knows, in the section it appears in, or the receipt is refused; plus exact coverage syntax and a length on EVERY sealed list. That last one is finding #4 of this ticket returning on a different list — I had bound the output count and not the fold count, fixing the instance instead of the rule, for the seventh time here. This is FG-169's principle applied to the existing extractor, and it is what ended the class after 14 rounds. Round 15 completed two checks I had written too narrowly: heading SHAPE was whitelisted rather than heading IDENTITY, so `## Forged claim` plus an indented body sailed through on the catch-all 'indented is fine' rule; and `(did not run)` exclusivity listed three side shapes and missed `workspace:`, manifest rows and engine notes, so a not-comparable receipt could display fake evidence under a `<none>` seal. Both are the same habit as the fold-count miss — enumerate what is in front of me instead of stating the rule. Round 16 found the FOURTH catch-all in this ticket that turned out to be the hole: the header accepted any indented line via `StartsWith "  "`, and nothing there is hashed except the named fields and the VERDICT block, so `  forged header claim` inserted after `sealed-output:` verified and was bound by nothing. Only VERDICT continuations may be indented there now. THE RULE, learned four times: name what is ALLOWED, never what is SHAPED like it — every catch-all in this verifier ('indented is fine', 'any ## heading', 'StartsWith all', 'these three side shapes') became a bypass. A CI-ONLY FAILURE OF THIS LANE, caught by its own guard: the coverage arm selected its fixture with `rg -l ... | head -1`, and ripgrep walks directories in PARALLEL, so 'the first match' is not a stable choice — the runner picked a receipt with no `jenkins=all (N)` to rewrite, the sed changed nothing, and the guard refused rather than reporting a pass. Fixtures are now selected SORTED and on the shape the mutation needs. The vacuous-arm guard added a round earlier paid for itself immediately. Two more from PR review, both live after the earlier fixes: the timestamp pattern stopped at 'prefix text excluded', leaving the rest of that sentence unmatched on a line the receipt presents as a sealed fact (now anchored end to end); and `int` THROWS above Int32.MaxValue, so a tampered count crashed the checker rather than being reported — a checker that crashes on malformed input is a checker that stops reporting. PROVEN TO FAIL, 36 arms: flipped verdict, flipped sealed-output mode, doctored output count, blank line added to a zero-output side, edited output line, deleted line, edited result, workspace hash, case digest, jenkins-core, reordered SEQUENCE output, edited MULTISET output, edited note, forged SECOND verdict, duplicated jenkins-core, no verdict at all, missing `sealed-output`, truncated file, and an empty directory (refuses rather than reporting a vacuous pass). Two REJECTION KINDS kept distinct: REFUSED is structural, UNREADABLE is present-but-unparseable. Two arms ACCEPT: a reordered MULTISET block and an added RECOVERED block, both declared unsealed by the receipt itself — if the first ever starts failing, the verifier has begun calling every re-run tampering, the false positive FG-167 existed to prevent. The verdict arm was proven by resealing 118 receipts with the verdict UNBOUND and watching the lane accept the flip | 36 arms; 118/118 receipts verify |
| FG-167 | **P1** | **DONE** | A CONCURRENT receipt's seal is NOT STABLE across runs | MEASURED while resealing for FG-164: of 118 receipts, exactly 2 moved — `parallel-post-selection` and `parallel-siblings-finish` — and the only content change was a line ORDER change (`+ sleep 6` moving). The seal bound each engine's LITERAL output, and a concurrent case's branch interleaving is decided by OS scheduling (FG-151), so those receipts resealed differently every run with nothing about the comparison changed. A seal that cannot tell a re-run from an edit is not detecting tampering. FIXED: for cases compared in MULTISET mode the seal binds the output SORTED — what was actually compared. **TWO SOURCES OF CHURN, NOT ONE**: the folded-pair disclosure is built by walking the multiset difference in OUTPUT order, so it churned too and the seal binds the folds; a fix closing only the obvious one would still have blocked FG-161. Multiset mode is decided by the DISJUNCTION exactly as `compareOutput` decides it — a per-trace test would seal one side sorted and the other literal. DELIBERATELY NOT CANONICALISED, against this row's own earlier proposal: env canonicalisation is deterministic run to run and so is not a source of churn, and sealing the canonical form would stop binding the inherited VALUES the engines printed. Sorting removes exactly the nondeterminism and nothing else. DISCLOSED: the receipt now states that ORDER of the printed output is NOT sealed for these cases and may be rearranged without breaking the seal — an unsealed region of a sealed document that nobody names is how a seal starts being trusted for more than it covers. EVIDENCE: two full differential runs on an unchanged tree, **118/118 seals identical, 0 moved** (was 2). Residual run-to-run content churn is 4 receipts, all in regions the receipt itself declares outside the seal — one FG-119 RECOVERED provenance block (FG-128) and displayed literal ordering in three parallel cases. PROVEN TO FAIL, 5 mutations: no sort fails 3 tests; sort-and-dedupe fails multiplicity; unconditional sort fails the ordered-case test; per-trace concurrency fails the disjunction test; an unsorted fold list fails the disclosure test. **THE DEDUPE MUTATION INITIALLY PASSED** — the multiplicity test appended a line, and the seal moved because the multiset disclosure embeds the line COUNT, not because the render bound multiplicity; the test was reading a number from a sentence beside the thing it meant to check. Rewritten to hold total length fixed. **UNBLOCKS FG-161** | 8 tests, 5 mutations; 2 runs, 118/118 seals stable |

What this rules out is a REGRESSION INTRODUCED BY THE PR — there is no code in the diff to have caused it. **THAT IS NOT THE SAME AS RULING OUT A CODE CAUSE, AND THE FIRST VERSION OF THIS ENTRY SAID 'IMPOSSIBLE' AND MEANT THE WRONG ONE.** Caught in review on PR #59, and it matters because the wrong reading steers this ticket at a capability skip when there is a NAMED RACE IN EXISTING CODE that produces exactly this symptom: `daemonScript` (`tests/Fogell.Execution.Tests/Tests.fs`) backgrounds the child with `nohup … &` so the shell LEADER exits immediately, and the success path in `ProcessGroup.fs` reaps the group as soon as the leader finishes — so on a loaded runner the child can be killed before it writes `daemon.pid`, and `waitForPidFile` then times out with this exact message. VERIFIED BY READING BOTH SITES, not taken from the report. IT ALSO COST WHAT THE ROW PREDICTS IT WOULD: the `--watch --fail-fast` that was supposed to catch this exited 0 while a job was red, so the red was found only by polling the head SHA's check-runs endpoint — the row's own argument that a habitually-red gate trains you to discount it, arriving as a tooling failure instead of a human one **AND AGAIN ON PR #61, 2026-08-15, WHERE IT DID SOMETHING USEFUL FOR THE FIRST TIME.** Same failure, same message, one of two `gate` jobs, sibling green — and because `main` had been branch-protected hours earlier, the PR went `BLOCKED` instead of `CLEAN`. THAT DOES NOT SOFTEN THE TICKET — a gate that goes red for reasons unrelated to the change now BLOCKS rather than merely misleads, which raises the cost of leaving it unfixed rather than lowering it |
