Every verifier Git subprocess and downstream Git receive `GIT_NO_REPLACE_OBJECTS=1`. A filter-free raw stage-0 audit binds regular-file bytes/modes and symlink-target bytes directly to index blobs. The external artifact is hashed into a sealed anonymous snapshot; downstream receives the immutable inherited `/proc/self/fd/N`, so pathname replacement or in-place writes cannot change the verified bytes. The 65-outcome scratch proof covers the original tuple/JSON/file/gitlink/raw-byte/artifact-boundary/cleanliness/environment cases plus deterministic replacement-object, artifact-swap, in-place-write, and seal-removal attacks with load-bearing mutations. Signed commit `8b59a1e01fd6ef8f1ab6a2cc202dbdf6f18eb982` and tree `e171c8e790607afd1a5c2d21d62ec0c6b064ee60` remain historical pre-hardening evidence; current exact identity and authoritative-gate evidence belong in the external handoff. Live release still needs a pristine Linux checkout, external artifact, canonical FG-080 package definition, and trusted manifest authority. The sealed descriptor is inherited-descriptor-lifetime scoped and must be consumed before a container/remote boundary. PATH-selected Git compromise, hostile administrator/kernel, command use of different bytes, signatures, SBOM, reproducibility, and corpus-content verification are not claimed; formal exact-head review, CI, publication, and merge remain — [→ detail](tickets/FG-093.md) |
| FG-094 | P1 | **PARTIAL** | Exact-set compatibility regression gate over a strict external baseline and Jenkins `reached_agent` oracle | Local checker runs scorecard `--check` first, binds the baseline ancestor/tree/historical-ledger digest plus exact corpus/current-ledger/oracle schemas, digests, and unique equal 228-filename sets (ledger rows are sorted; the pinned manifest's historical row order is canonicalized for set comparison), and refuses partial corpus-host configuration. `accepted` means tier-1 OR `admitted` (which is parse-only, NOT tier 2). PASS iff baseline tier-1 filenames remain tier-1 and every baseline-accepted `reached_agent=True` filename remains accepted; gains and accepted losses confined to pinned `reached_agent=False` pass. Counts never decide, so same-count swaps fail. The proof's synthetic `vinsguru_jenkins-pipeline.Jenkinsfile` (`fixture/repo`) is the load-bearing compiled-but-not-reached allowed-loss control. The real pinned `vinsguru_docker-selenium-jenkinsfile.Jenkinsfile` row is also compiled but not reached, but remains admitted baseline/current; the live gate measured zero losses. The 43-outcome offline proof covers exact-set/schema/digest/environment rules and direct mutants; it runs everywhere. Live comparison runs only on a corpus host with both operator-provided external files, while corpus-free CI explicitly skips and remains uncovered. The candidate baseline's authenticity/owner authority, semantic correctness, progress for already-rejected files, cross-version Jenkins behavior, and automatic baseline updates are not claimed — [→ detail](tickets/FG-094.md) |

## Wave 9 — Script blocks and the Groovy interpreter

Twenty-one tickets from this line of work had accreted into **Wave 8 — Release gates**,
which is a table for scorecard generators and receipt seals. They were filed there
because it was the last table in the file, not because a release gate is what they are.
The ten below are the interpreter and `script { }` body of work; the receipt- and
seal-shaped tickets stayed in Wave 8, which is retitled to say what it actually holds.

Nothing here is reprioritised by the move. The rows are verbatim; only their address
changed, and the queue above is what orders them.

| id | pri | status | item | acceptance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| FG-160 | **P1** | **DONE** | 48 corpus files were ADMITTED while their sections were silently dropped; they are now refused | MEASURED against baseline `a2d9da8` with the same scorer over the same pinned corpus: admitted 197 → 149, rejected 31 → 79. All **48** newly-rejected files report `opaque section`, and **0** files moved the other way. Those 48 — **21% of the corpus** — previously parsed 'fine' while a `steps`, `options`, `environment`, `stages` or `post` block was consumed opaquely and its contents dropped, so Fogell would have RUN them missing work the pipeline declares. FG-143/150/152/153/155 converted that into a refusal, which is ADR 0001's stated preference and a large honest step backwards in the admitted count. THE WORK NOW RANKED BY DEMAND: making those 52 `opaque section` files parse is the highest-value parser work available, and it is measurable per file from the ledger | scorecard delta, both directions **RANKED BY CAUSAL MEASUREMENT (2026-08-06).** Strip a construct from the 53 rejected files (52 in the `opaque section` bucket plus 1 whose message reads `an opaque section` — same class, different article), re-parse, count what now admits — presence is not blame. **THE RECOVERY COUNTS ARE AN UPPER BOUND**: stripping removes the very content a real implementation must handle, so 25 localises WHERE the parse stops, not how many files `script` support will admit. `script { … }` blocks: **25 files**. Then `tools { … }`: **6 more**, cumulative 31 of 53. Of the 28 still failing after `script` removal, **10** contain a `tools` block — 9 written `tools {` and one with the brace on the next line (`pavankjadda_BookStore`) — so **4 fail for further reasons even without it**. This parenthetical first said "the 8 that CONTAIN it", which labelled a POSITION count (files failing AT a `tools` line) with a CONTAINMENT meaning, and undercounted anyway because a line-based `rg` cannot match `tools` and `{` on separate lines. Counted with `rg -U`. **MEASURED 2026-08-09, and it reframes this ticket: `script { }` DOES NOT EXECUTE AT ALL.** Three differential probes against the pinned Jenkins, receipts in scratch: `script { sh 'echo INSIDE-SCRIPT' }` — no control flow whatever — gives jenkins=success fogell=FAILURE with **zero output lines** from Fogell; an `if/else` body behaves the same; a `for` body is refused at parse time as `opaque section`. So the parser ADMITS the simple and if/else forms while the runtime cannot run any of them. THE TICKET IS NOT 'make 25 files parse' — parsing is not the binding constraint for the admitted ones; it is 'route a `script` body to the Groovy parser and interpreter', both of which ALREADY EXIST (`Fogell.Groovy.Parser`, `Fogell.Groovy.Interpreter`) and are not wired to the Declarative walker. The corpus counts below still rank WHERE parses stop and remain the right ordering for parser work; they do not measure how many files would then RUN. **DONE 2026-08-09. `script { }` RUNS.** The body is captured verbatim by the parser (`ScriptBody`), parsed by `Fogell.Groovy.Parser`, screened by `StepValueUse.find`, interpreted, and its collected `StepCall` effects replayed through the walker's own dispatch. MEASURED, corpus: **admitted 149 → 174 (+25), tier3 down 79 → 59, both as measured that day** — exactly the 25 files the strip-and-reparse ranking predicted for `script`, which is a good independent check on that method. ADMITTED IS PARSE-ONLY, as always: these 25 now PARSE; whether each RUNS is unmeasured, because corpus files are never executed. EXECUTION is proven by two new differential receipts against pinned Jenkins — `script-block-runs` (two sequential `sh` steps) and `script-block-if-branch` (only ONE branch may run) — and the case suite is now **120/120**. **THE TWO-STEP CASE EARNED ITS SECOND STEP IMMEDIATELY**: `Interpreter.run` already reverses its consed effect list at every exit, I reversed it again, and Fogell ran the steps BACKWARDS while the comment above the line claimed to be preventing exactly that. A one-step case cannot see it. That is the whole argument for cases that can distinguish order. The value-use refusal fires as designed: `def out = sh(returnStdout: true, …)` fails the build with the position named rather than binding null, and `DiagnosticSilence` did NOT fire on it — which after FG-170 means the refusal reported its reason. The step vocabulary is CLOSED (16 names taken from the dispatch table); a name outside it is refused by the sandbox and the build fails with a reason, rather than being collected as an effect that silently does nothing when replayed. TWO DEFECTS CAUGHT BY THE PRE-PUSH VERIFIER BEFORE THIS SHIPPED, both silent-wrong-answer class. (a) PROVENANCE: replayed arguments are ALREADY EVALUATED by the interpreter, but I left them unmarked, so `renderStepArgs` interpolated them a SECOND time — `script { input message: 'Deploy ${TARGET}?' }` is single-quoted and LITERAL on Jenkins, and Fogell would have re-rendered `${TARGET}` before publishing the gate a human approves. Every replayed argument is now marked literal. (b) WRAPPERS: the interpreter runs a trailing closure IMMEDIATELY and flattens it, so a replayed `dir`/`timeout`/`withEnv` arrives with `Block = []` — and those wrappers are driven ENTIRELY by `step.Block`. `script { dir('x') { sh 'pwd' } }` would have run `sh` in the STAGE ROOT and reported success. Now REFUSED by `findWrapperCalls`, verified failing closed on a probe. My `StepValueUse` comment had called a trailing block 'perfectly runnable' — it is safe from the VALUE question and unsafe from the BLOCK one, and one sentence tried to answer both. The wrapper finder also had to learn that a block reaches a call as `trailing` OR as a closure ARGUMENT depending on parser path: matching `trailing` alone found NOTHING for `node { sh 'make' }`, so it asks 'is there a block anywhere in this call' instead — representation-independent, and it errs toward refusing. (c) DURABLE APPROVAL REPLAY, the worst of the three and one I would not have found: `input` inside a `script { }` replays through the normal dispatcher, but durability is keyed on the TOP-LEVEL step (`Resume.fs`), so a replayed nested effect has no journal identity. The verifier REPRODUCED it — journal `step-started Gate 0 script` plus `input-decision … approved alice`, rewind before `step-finished`, and the resume exits 3 with `needs-reconciliation`, LOSING AN APPROVAL A HUMAN ALREADY GAVE. That is the guarantee FG-046b exists for and the one no receipt can cover. `input` is now OUT of the script vocabulary with a named reason, and approval-lane scenario **Z6** proves the refusal — scenario B proves the durable path for a TOP-LEVEL `input` and would have passed happily while this one lost approvals. Z6's reason TEXT is unasserted, said plainly: this host does not surface emitted console text to the captured log, the same gap E/J/O record against FG-114. (d) STRICT VARIABLE READS: I used `Interpreter.run`, the LAX mode where an unbound name reads as null — kept for consumers modelling scripted Groovy's laxer contexts, and a script block is not one. Jenkins raises MissingPropertyException, which this repo ALREADY MEASURED in the `gstring-unresolved-property` receipt, so the evidence to get this right was sitting in the suite. `script { sh "echo bare:${MISSING}" }` wrote `bare:null` and exited 0 where Jenkins FAILS. Now `runStrictVars`, with case `script-unresolved-variable`; suite **121/121**. (e) BODY-LESS WRAPPERS: `findWrapperCalls` refused a block-taking step WITH a body and left the spelling WITHOUT one running — `script { dir('child') }` CREATED the directory and exited 0 where Jenkins fails with 'dir step must be called with a body'. I refused half a shape. Block-taking steps were dropped from the script vocabulary entirely, which answered both spellings at once. **SUPERSEDED BY FG-172**, and corrected here rather than left to read as current: `dir`, `timeout` and `retry` are ADMITTED again now their arms run a hosted body, and a body-less `dir` is refused by the `dir` ARM before it creates the directory. `script-bodyless-dir` was also narrowed — it proves agreement on the terminal result and output, NOT on side effects, because the manifest hashes files and cannot see an empty directory (FG-173). Suite was **122/122** at that point. **THE VERIFIER NAMED THIS ONE AS A REPEAT ITSELF** — 'this class has now appeared twice' — which is the signal added to it this session working on first use, and it also inferred the ticket correctly from the diff with no brief supplied. **ALL FIVE FG-160 FINDINGS ARE ONE CLASS**, which is worth naming because the count matters more than any single defect: the script path did not reproduce a Jenkins semantic that Fogell ALREADY MODELS CORRECTLY ELSEWHERE — literal-vs-interpolated arguments, wrapper block bodies, durable approval identity, strict variable reads, and whether a shape is legal at all. Wiring a new front end onto existing machinery silently opts out of every rule that machinery applies at its own boundary. The lesson for `tools { }` and any future block: enumerate what the EXISTING path guarantees and check the new one against that list, rather than testing the happy path and waiting for review. SELF-AUDIT BEFORE THE NEXT REVIEW ROUND, applying this row's own lesson rather than waiting for finding six: probed the two script-specific shapes left after the vocabulary closed. CLOSURES WORK — `['a','b'].each { sh "echo item:${name}" }` is PROVEN against Jenkins and is now case `script-closure-each`, which matters because effect collection through a closure is idiomatic and was untested. NESTED `script { script { } }` diverges the OTHER WAY: Jenkins SUCCEEDS, Fogell REFUSES it (`script` is not in its own vocabulary). That is the acceptable direction under ADR 0001 — a refusal, not a false success — and it is recorded here rather than left to be rediscovered. Not fixed: nesting is rare, the inner block would arrive as a wrapper call the replay cannot carry, and refusing costs less than a half-right implementation. STILL OPEN, and not smuggled in: nested `script`, `tools { }` is the next 6 files, and a body needing a step's RETURN VALUE is refused rather than run — a call-back interpreter is slice 2 if the corpus justifies it. **THE DESIGN CONSTRAINT, established 2026-08-09 before writing any of it — and it is not obvious.** The Groovy interpreter is a BATCH model: `Interpreter.run` COLLECTS `StepCall` effects and returns them for the host to perform, and a step call evaluates to `VNull` on the spot (`Interpreter.fs`, the `Ok(Step s)` branch). It never calls back. So wiring `script { }` to it naively runs the steps in the right ORDER while binding NULL wherever a step's RETURN VALUE is used — `def out = sh(script: 'x', returnStdout: true)` proceeds with `out = null` and every branch on it is decided wrongly. That is a silent wrong answer, the class ADR 0001 calls worse than rejection, and it is what a straightforward implementation of this ticket produces. NOTE WHY `when { expression { … } }` IS ALREADY SAFE: `WalkerWhen` passes `registeredSteps = Set.empty`, so `Sandbox.admitCall` DENIES any step call rather than returning null for it. Enabling steps for `script` is precisely what turns that denial into the hazard. **SLICE 1a LANDED (the refusal, before the thing it guards):** `StepValueUse.find` walks the parsed body and reports every step call whose value is consumed, with the POSITION NAMED — a refusal saying only 'a step value is used' sends someone hunting. 12 tests, held from BOTH sides: a no-op mutation fails 7 of them, and an over-refusal mutation (treating statement-level calls as value uses) fails the two that assert the common shapes must still RUN — a bare `sh 'make'` statement and a trailing block like `node { sh 'make' }`. That second direction matters more than the first: an over-eager check would refuse the corpus this ticket exists to admit. Arguments are value positions even for a bare statement call, so `echo sh(returnStdout: true, …)` is caught where a statement-level check would pass it. **SLICE 1b, DESIGN VERIFIED against the code (2026-08-09), three pieces that must land ATOMICALLY:** (1) `Step` gains `ScriptBody: string option` — only TWO files construct that record, so the field is cheap; (2) the step parser special-cases the name `script` and captures its block with `balancedRaw '{' '}'` instead of `stepBlock`, leaving `Block = []`; (3) `WalkerStep.runStepInner` sees `ScriptBody = Some src`, parses it with `Fogell.Groovy.Parser`, runs `StepValueUse.find` and FAILS THE STEP with the named position if anything comes back, otherwise interprets it and replays each `StepCall` effect by building a `Step` and calling `runStepInner` recursively — the seam exists and takes exactly that shape. **WHY ATOMICALLY, and this is not caution for its own sake:** capturing the body raw WITHOUT execution moves the `for`-body files from correctly REFUSED (`opaque section`) to ADMITTED-but-unrunnable, which is the wrong direction and precisely what FG-143/152 spent five tickets undoing. A partial slice here would be a regression wearing the shape of progress. THE ORIGINAL PLAN, kept for the record: **SLICE 1 IS: run the body, and REFUSE any body that uses a step call in a VALUE POSITION** — assignment RHS, condition, argument, interpolation — which is a static check over the parsed AST and needs no interpreter change. Bodies that are sequences of steps and control flow over `env` (the common corpus shape) then RUN; bodies that need a step's value are rejected with a named code rather than mis-run. Slice 2, if the corpus justifies it, is a call-back interpreter so return values work. Do NOT ship slice 1 without the refusal: `script` is currently fail-closed at runtime, so a partial implementation that silently binds null would be a REGRESSION from today's honest failure. The remaining 22, EXHAUSTIVELY: `stages` 11, `parameters` 3, a closing `}` 3, `stages{` 1, `stage("Quality Gate")` 1, `environment` 1, `else` 1, blank line 1. **CO-OCCURRENCE IS A FALSE LEAD, THOUGH NOT UNIFORMLY WRONG HERE**: it would have ranked `script` first by luck (38 of 53 contain it, and it IS the top cause), then `post` second at 31 files — and `post` is fully supported, so that slot is worthless. Presence is not blame; the ranking below is by strip-and-reparse. This note first claimed co-occurrence "puts `post` on top", which is false since `script` co-occurs more, and listed 16 of the 22 while presenting them as all of them — two overclaims in a note about not overclaiming. `script` support is therefore the single highest-value parser item on the board: it alone moves admitted from 149 toward ~174 of 228 |
| FG-171 | **P1** | **DONE** | A crash inside a `script { }` re-runs the WHOLE block on resume, duplicating effects that already ran — **AND THAT CLAIM WAS FALSE, WHICH THE TICKET'S OWN FIRST HALF PROVED** | FOUND BY SELF-AUDIT, not by review — applying FG-160's own lesson (enumerate what the existing path guarantees, check the new one against it) instead of waiting for a fifth finding. The stage loop makes each step a durability unit: `hooks.ShouldExecute`, `OnStepStarted`/`OnStepFinished`, and a per-step `DurabilityKey`. A `script` body's replayed steps go straight to `runStepDispatch`, so THE WHOLE BLOCK is one durability unit. Crash after `sh 'a'` and before `sh 'b'`, and the resume re-runs `sh 'a'` — Jenkins records each step inside a `script` in its flow graph and resumes AFTER the completed ones. WHAT IS ALREADY SAFE, checked rather than assumed: `DurabilityKey` has exactly ONE reader, the `input` path, and `input` is refused inside a script (FG-160c) — so no approval can be misattributed by this. The exposure is DUPLICATED SIDE EFFECTS, not a lost or misapplied answer. UNPROVEN, and deliberately not asserted as measured: the restart lane could plant a SIGKILL between two script steps and assert the second runs exactly once, but that case does not exist yet — writing it IS the first half of this ticket. Same family as FG-135 **DONE 2026-08-17, BY MEASUREMENT THAT FALSIFIED THE ROW.** The lane case the row asked for was written and it disproves the title: a SIGKILL between two script children leaves ONE `step-started` for the block, the resume REFUSES by name (exit 3, `needs-reconciliation: Build#0`), and s1/s2 stay exactly-once — the whole block is an ordinary interrupted step under the FG-025 rule, and the self-audit that filed this read the child dispatch (which does bypass the hooks) without measuring what the TOP-LEVEL unit already guarantees. No class-A exposure exists for the claimed mechanism. WHAT THE MEASUREMENT DID FIND, asserted in the lane so it cannot drift: operator attestation covers the WHOLE block, so a child the crash never reached does not run on a reconciled resume — the wrapper limit Resume.fs documents, now naming `script { }` explicitly. Fine-grained resume inside a block is a capability gap (class D, honest, named), and it shares the journal redesign FG-135 carries rather than being its own machinery | none yet; the lane case is the ticket |
| FG-172 | **P1** | **DONE** | FG-160 slice 2: the interpreter calls back per step instead of batching effects | **OWNER'S CALL, 2026-08-09**, taken after the pre-push verifier argued it unprompted: 'the batch-interpret-then-replay design is the wrong boundary for Jenkins-compatible script semantics'. SIX findings on FG-160 were ONE class — state that must cross the batch boundary — and each fix was a REFUSAL, so the supported surface kept shrinking: step return values, wrapper bodies, `input`, `env` mutation, nested `script`. **SEAM LANDED**: `Interpreter.PerformStep` — `Perform: name -> positional -> named -> (unit -> unit) option -> Value`, plus `SetEnv` — with `runHosted` entering live mode. ADDITIVE: `Host = None` keeps the batch model byte-identical, so `when { expression }` and every existing consumer are untouched, which is what makes this safe to land before the walker side exists. The trailing block is handed over UNEVALUATED as a thunk, so a wrapper runs it inside whatever context it establishes — batch mode evaluated it immediately, which is how `dir('x') { … }` lost its directory. `Outcome.Effects` is EMPTY in hosted mode deliberately: a caller reading it has asked for the wrong model, and an empty list makes that visible rather than returning a replayable-looking log of work already done. 6 tests, PROVEN TO FAIL both ways: reverting to batch fails 5 of them; running the body BEFORE handing it over — the exact flattening bug — fails precisely the two wrapper tests. **THE SEAM IS NOW LOAD-BEARING**: the script path calls `runHosted`, so every step in all 123 receipts goes through the callback rather than the replay loop. Observationally identical — the refusals still exclude every shape that needed the boundary moved — and that is why it was done as its own step: an unexercised callback that everything else is about to be built on is the shape of dead code this session keeps finding under a passing gate. The replay loop is DELETED, not left beside it. Both host callbacks FAIL LOUD if reached: a body or an `env` assignment arriving here means a refusal above stopped covering its shape, and a silently dropped body is the exact defect those refusals exist to prevent. **WRAPPER BODIES WORK, `dir` FIRST.** `BranchCtx.HostedBody` carries a body runner that takes the context the WRAPPER established — the whole difficulty, since the body re-enters the interpreter and calls back, and those inner steps must use the wrapper's directory rather than the one captured when the script started. The host reads its context from a CELL for the same reason; capturing it would be the original `dir` defect in a new place. Restored in `finally`, because a step inside the body can fail the build and a cell left pointing into an abandoned wrapper would run the REST of the script in the wrong directory. Case `script-dir-body` proves it, and its SECOND step is load-bearing: `where.txt` in `sub/` and `top.txt` at the root proves the context is established AND restored, which a one-step case cannot distinguish. Only the workspace manifest's PATHS catch it — content is identical either way, exactly how the original defect hid. **`script-bodyless-dir` CAUGHT ITS OWN REGRESSION** the moment `dir` rejoined the vocabulary: a body-less `dir` reached the arm again and created the directory. Fixed at the ARM, not in the script bridge, because the rule is Jenkins' — `dir('x')` alone is as wrong at stage level. That case was written one round earlier and repaid itself immediately. The wrapper refusal is now DERIVED — vocabulary minus arms taught to run a hosted body — so adding a block-taking step without teaching its arm fires the refusal instead of dropping its block. Suite **124/124**. **`withEnv` ATTEMPTED AND BACKED OUT, with the blocker found: ARGUMENT MARSHALLING IS LOSSY FOR NON-SCALARS.** The host builds a `Step` from interpreter `Value`s via `Value.toDisplay`, so `withEnv(['A=1'])` — a Groovy LIST — arrives as the single string `[A=1]` and the arm binds nothing. Measured: `script { withEnv(['SCRIPT_SCOPED=inside']) { sh 'echo in:$SCRIPT_SCOPED' } }` printed `in:` against Jenkins' `in:inside`. Backed out rather than shipped half-working; `withEnv` is refused again and the suite is 124/124. THE FIX IS STRUCTURAL AND BLOCKS `withEnv`, `withCredentials` AND ANY LIST/MAP-ARGUMENT STEP: the bridge must carry typed values through to the arm instead of rendering them to display strings. `Step` holds strings by design (ADR 0002 — the interpreter decides what an expression means), so this is a real design question, not an oversight to patch. TWO GUARD BUGS FOUND ON THE WAY, both kept: the wrapper arms are guarded on `not (List.isEmpty step.Block)`, and a hosted body leaves `Block` EMPTY — so `withEnv` never matched its arm and fell through to the fallback, doing NOTHING while the build reported success. `dir` escaped only because its guard keys on the positional argument. The guard now also accepts `ctx.HostedBody.IsSome`; the same trap waits on `timeout`, `retry` and `withCredentials`. **`timeout` AND `retry` NOW RUN HOSTED BODIES**, both proven against Jenkins. `script-retry-attempts` is the clearest evidence the thunk model is right: `retry(3)` performs THREE attempts, which the batch model could not express at all — it had already evaluated the closure once before the wrapper saw it. The marker file is appended so the COUNT lands in the workspace hash; an engine that ran the body once and reported success diverges on the manifest instead of passing quietly. `script-timeout-bound` proves the bound HOLDS rather than being announced: a 3-second timeout over a 30-second sleep, with `done.txt` ABSENT on both engines. Comparing only the terminal result would pass an engine that let the sleep finish and then failed. This needed `BranchCtx.HostedDeadline` — a deadline normally travels as a dispatch ARGUMENT, and a hosted body's inner steps are dispatched by the script host, which has no way to be told; without it the budget printed and did nothing, a safety bound defeated. Suite **126/126**. REMAINING WRAPPERS ARE BLOCKED, not merely unfinished: `withEnv` and `withCredentials` take LIST arguments and the bridge renders values to display strings, so `['A=1']` arrives as `[A=1]`. That is the marshalling question above and it is a design decision, not a patch. **THE `env` PRE-SCAN IS DELETED AND REPLACED BY A RUNTIME BOUNDARY**, which is the fifth finding of the script-bridge class and the one that settles the method. `findEnvMutations` walked STATEMENTS and missed `[1].each { env.FOO = 'x' }` — the assignment lives inside a closure the scanner never entered, and Fogell exited 0 with empty values where Jenkins applies the mutation to later steps. A static pre-scan of a dynamic language is incomplete by construction, and closure and control-flow shapes will keep escaping it; the verifier made exactly that argument and it is right. The interpreter now calls `PerformStep.SetEnv` AT THE ASSIGNMENT, so every mutation that actually EXECUTES is seen whatever syntax reached it — including a COMPUTED index (`env["FOO$suffix"]`), which the scanner recognised only as a literal. The host refuses there. The refusal is now COMPLETE rather than best-effort, and the scanner is DELETED rather than supplemented: one that catches some shapes reads as coverage while leaving the rest silent. Both shapes verified failing closed; suite **126/126** unchanged. NOTE the seam earned this: `SetEnv` existed on `PerformStep` from the start of FG-172 and was unused, so the fix was wiring rather than redesign. **OWNER'S DECISION 2026-08-09: typed values through the bridge; KEEP THE JOURNAL FORMAT.** TYPED ARGS LANDED. `BranchCtx.HostedArgs` carries a hosted call's arguments still as interpreter `Value`s. `Step` keeps holding strings — ADR 0002 stands, the parser must not pre-empt meaning — but by the time a script block CALLS a step the interpreter has already decided what the expression means, and rendering `['A=1']` to display text produced `[A=1]`, unquoted, which the arm's quoted-element parser could not read. The typed form rides on the CONTEXT, a layer that already depends on the interpreter, so arms that want structure ask for it and every other arm is untouched. `withEnv` is admitted and PROVEN (`script-withenv-body`), whose second `sh` asserts `out:` is EMPTY — block scoping is the part a bridge leak would break, and a case reading only the first line would call that a pass. Suite **128/128**. **`withCredentials` IS NOT THE SAME MECHANISM**, and the earlier note saying so was wrong — corrected before acting on it. Its argument is a DSL OF NESTED CALLS: `withCredentials([string(credentialsId: 'x', variable: 'Y')])`. `Credentials.parseRequests` reads that from RAW SOURCE with a regex, and in a script block the interpreter would try to EVALUATE `string(...)` as code — it is neither a registered step nor a `Sandbox.builtins` member, so `admitCall` denies it and the script faults at the inner call, before the marshalling question is even reached. ANALYSIS, not measurement: read from the sandbox's own lists rather than probed, because a probe needs a provisioned credential store. That failure is FAIL-CLOSED, so the current refusal is safe; what it is NOT is a marshalling gap that typed args would close. Supporting it needs the credential DSL modelled as pseudo-builtins returning structured values, then requests reconstructed from those — a bigger piece, and the one place in this ticket where getting it wrong puts a SECRET IN A LOG. Deliberately not rushed at the end of a long session. **FG-171 STAYS OPEN BY DECISION, not by omission**: nested durable identity needs a journal FORMAT change and the owner has kept the format. So a `script { }` remains ONE durability unit — a crash mid-block re-runs it — and `input` stays refused inside a script, since durable identity is exactly what it needs. **CORRECTED 2026-08-17, when FG-171's own lane case falsified the re-run half of that sentence**: a crash mid-block REFUSES resume by name and duplicates nothing; the one-unit fact and the `input` refusal stand, and FG-171 closed by measurement rather than by per-step journaling, which remains FG-135's redesign. | FG-174 | **P1** | **DONE** | `sh(returnStdout:)` / `sh(returnStatus:)` are NOT IMPLEMENTED, which is what blocks step return values | FG-172's callback CAN return a value — `PerformStep.Perform` returns one and the interpreter uses it — but the walker has nothing to give it: `rg returnStdout src/` finds only COMMENTS, no capture, and no case uses it. So 'step return values' is not plumbing a value through the seam; it is implementing the step semantics first, and only then does `def out = sh(...)` stop being refused. **SEMANTICS MEASURED against pinned Jenkins 2.568.1 BEFORE writing any of it**, because these are exactly the details that become the next review finding: (a) `returnStdout: true` returns stdout VERBATIM INCLUDING THE TRAILING NEWLINE — `printf 'value\n'` piped through `od -c` gives `[ v a l u e \n ]`, which is why pipelines call `.trim()`, and an implementation that helpfully strips it would silently change every script that does; (b) `returnStatus: true` returns the exit code AND DOES NOT FAIL THE BUILD — `sh(script: 'exit 7', returnStatus: true)` yielded 7, a following step ran, and the build reported SUCCESS. Getting (b) wrong turns a deliberate status check into a failed build, and getting it wrong the other way hides a real failure. **SCOPE CORRECTED after reading the code rather than trusting my own note.** I recorded that this was 'a feature, not plumbing' because `rg returnStdout src/` found only comments. That was the wrong query: `StepResult` ALREADY carries `ExitCode: int option` AND `Stdout: string`, and `runShell` populates both. The executor captures everything needed; what is missing is that `sh` never READS the `returnStdout`/`returnStatus` named arguments, and no value can travel back. So the work is four small pieces, not a capture implementation: (1) `WalkerStep` honours the two flags and, for `returnStatus`, does NOT fail the build; (2) the value reaches the host — a `Value ref` on `BranchCtx` in the style of `HostedBody`, rather than changing dispatch's return type from unit, which is invasive and touches every arm; (3) the host returns it from `Perform`; (4) `StepValueUse.find` stops refusing a value use once a value can actually be supplied. **PLUMBING DONE, SEMANTICS NOT — and the refusal is BACK ON.** `BranchCtx.HostedResult` carries a typed value (a ref cell, like `HostedBody`, because `runStepDispatch` returns unit and threading a return type through every wrapper arm to serve one caller is the invasive change). TYPED deliberately: `returnStatus` must yield a Groovy INTEGER or `if (code == 0)` compares Integer to String and is quietly false. `WalkerStep` honours both flags and `returnStatus` no longer fails the build. A fresh slot PER CALL, so a step that returns nothing cannot hand back the previous step's value. THEN I LIFTED THE REFUSAL AND MEASURED, WHICH IS THE RIGHT ORDER AND IT PAID: two semantics I had not probed made the returned value WRONG rather than absent. (1) Jenkins does NOT echo captured stdout — `returnStdout` captures instead of printing, and Fogell streamed it, so the compared output gained lines Jenkins never produced. (2) `result.Stdout` includes the `sh -x` TRACE, so `out` came back as `"+ printf value\nvalue"` instead of `"value\n"`. A returned value that is WRONG is worse than one refused, so the refusal went back on and the suite is 129/129. **THE REMAINING BLOCKER, found by lifting the refusal a SECOND time: Fogell's `sh -x` writes its TRACE TO STDOUT**, where Jenkins writes it to stderr. `ProcessGroup.SuppressStdoutEcho` was added and works, but with the trace on stdout, suppressing that echo also swallowed the `+ printf value` line JENKINS PRINTS — and the captured value still contained the trace. So stdout cannot be captured alone until the trace moves to stderr, and THAT changes how the shell is invoked for every step and every one of the 129 receipts. Its own piece of work, not a finishing touch. `CaptureStdout` is therefore wired end to end and DELIBERATELY LEFT FALSE: turning it on now would trade a missing value for missing OUTPUT, and for every `sh(returnStdout:)` anywhere rather than just in a script. The refusal is back on and the suite was 129/129 at that point. LIFTING THE REFUSAL AND MEASURING IS WHAT FOUND BOTH ROUNDS OF THIS — the trailing newline and returnStatus-does-not-fail were measured up front and were the EASY half; the echo behaviour and the trace stream only appeared by running it. UNPROVEN in-repo: both probes DIVERGE, since Fogell refuses the value use today, so they cannot join a suite requiring 128/128 — they become receipts as part of this ticket. **THE PLUMBING WAS HIDING A FALSE SUCCESS, and the verifier RAN it rather than reading it.** `returnStatus` suppression was keyed on THE FLAG ALONE, so an ABORT took the same path as a non-zero exit: `timeout(time: 1, unit: 'SECONDS') { sh script: 'sleep 5', returnStatus: true }` followed by another step reported SUCCESS and ran the following step. A SAFETY BOUND DEFEATED, which ranks with a bypassed approval here — and the same hole `script-timeout-bound` exists to close, which passed throughout only because its `sh` carried no flag. A SECOND defect sat beside it, found by reading the slot rather than the guard: `defaultArg result.ExitCode 0` handed the script STATUS 0 for a process the engine had just killed, so a script checking the code would have seen success. THE RULE IS NOW DERIVED FROM THE MECHANISM, not from a list of statuses: an exit code exists IFF the shell produced one (`Completed` and `Signalled` set it; `TimedOut` and `Cancelled` do not), so `ExitCode.IsSome` IS the test rather than a proxy for it, and it needs no second copy of a mapping that already exists to drift from. Comparing the terminal result alone would not have distinguished them; the mutation diverges on ALL THREE axes (`jenkins=aborted fogell=success`, output, and a workspace containing `after.txt`). Two real resolver gaps came out of it — a typo'd build number resolved through its family, and a glob on a hyphenless family was never extracted at all, so that citation went UNCHECKED. Blocking in the gate, proof first. **THE TRACE WAS NEVER `sh`'s DOING, and this row said otherwise.** It recorded that 'Fogell's `sh -x` writes its TRACE TO STDOUT, where Jenkins writes it to stderr', and concluded the fix 'changes how the shell is invoked for every step and every one of the 129 receipts'. That named the SYMPTOM and mis-stated the cause, and the mis-statement is what made this look like its own expedition: `sh -x` writes to STDERR here exactly as it does on Jenkins, and the process wrapper MERGED the two streams with `2>&1`. The merge is there so console interleaving is exact — one pipe cannot reorder against itself — and that reason DOES NOT APPLY when stdout is captured, because none of it reaches the console. Splitting the streams only on the capture path leaves every other step byte-identical, which is what keeps the receipts valid. A day of estimated work was a one-line condition; the estimate was wrong because I wrote down what I observed instead of what produced it. **THE ELEVENTH FINDING OF THE ADMITTED-OPTION CLASS**, and the narrowest yet: `StepValueUse` refuses a VALUE USE, so `def out = sh(returnStdout: true)` was rejected — but a STATEMENT-position `sh script: 'printf value', returnStdout: true` is not a value use, nothing refused it, and Fogell STREAMED the output Jenkins captures. The flags were read AFTER `Executor.runStep` returned, which made `returnStdout` a post-hoc reinterpretation of a step that had already printed. They are now read BEFORE dispatch, so the option affects the RUN rather than the report. **CAPTURE IS BYTES, NOT LINES — found by the case, not by review.** The first `script-sh-returnstdout` filled the sink through `AppendLine`, so a captured value always ended with a newline whether the program wrote one or not: `printf value` returned six bytes where Jenkins returns five. `.trim()` hides that in most pipelines and nothing downstream would ever report it. A line reader CANNOT fix it — `OutputDataReceived` never says whether the last line was terminated — so capture mode skips the line reader for stdout entirely and reads the stream, which costs nothing because nothing is echoing it. Both pipes still drain CONCURRENTLY; reading one to completion first is how a process writing to both deadlocks. The case now asserts BOTH shapes (`echo value` -> two lines, `printf value` -> one), because a case asserting only the newline shape passes the engine that re-terminates every line — which is exactly how the defect reached a differential run. **AND A THIRD DEFECT THE SAME CASE EXPOSED, whose rule is JENKINS' rather than the capture path's**: `echo` handed `OnLine` one record containing a newline, so a multi-line message was ONE log line where Jenkins has two. No existing case echoes a multi-line message — none could, until `returnStdout` made multi-line values possible — so it was invisible. Fixed in the step for every caller, not at the site that exposed it; that is the third time on this branch checking whose rule it was turned a script-path patch into a fix for everyone. **THE REFUSAL IS LIFTED, on the third attempt.** The two earlier attempts put it back because measuring showed the value would be WRONG rather than absent, and both causes are now closed at the source. A call must opt in with a LITERAL `true`: `sh(returnStdout: someVar)` cannot be decided by a static reader, so it stays refused rather than assumed. Receipts `script-sh-returnstdout`, `script-sh-returnstatus` and `script-returnstatus-timeout`, all tier 1. Jenkins' `echo` is void-returning and merely WARNS about the unknown parameter, so it runs that branch. A FALSE SUCCESS where the evidence is WORK THAT DID NOT HAPPEN, which no output or workspace comparison can see: the skipped step leaves nothing behind to differ. `WalkerRules.stepsHonouringReturnFlags` is now the ONE definition, read by both ends — the static refusal takes it as a PREDICATE so the interpreter layer still holds no step vocabulary, and `WalkerStep` reads the same set, because two copies is exactly how the two ends come to disagree about which steps answer. NOT RECEIPTED, and deliberately so: Jenkins ACCEPTS this pipeline and Fogell REFUSES it, so the case is NOT-COMPARABLE by construction, the same shape as `stage-input-directive`. Fogell returned the stdout, so a following `if (code == 7)` compared String to Integer, went quietly false, and the guarded step never ran while the build reported SUCCESS. Three findings in a row (universal flags, then per-step, then precedence) were the same mistake at different resolutions: I kept encoding the contract as BOOLEANS AT EACH CALL SITE, and a boolean cannot express precedence, so the two readers were free to resolve the combination differently — and did. `WalkerRules.returnContract : step -> returnStdout -> returnStatus -> ReturnContract` is now a TOTAL FUNCTION over the inputs, returning `NoValue | ExitStatus | CapturedStdout`. `StepValueUse` takes it as a parameter (still no step vocabulary in the interpreter layer) and `WalkerStep` publishes straight from it, so precedence is decided ONCE and neither end can drift. CAPTURE KEYS ON THE REQUEST, NOT ON THE WINNER, and that was MEASURED not assumed: `script-sh-return-both` prints `captured` to stdout AND exits 7, and the string appears in NEITHER engine's console — durable-task captures because `returnStdout` was ASKED FOR, while the status is what comes back. THE ASSERTIONS ARE FILES ON PURPOSE: this false success's only evidence is WORK THAT DID NOT HAPPEN, which terminal result and console output cannot see, because a skipped step leaves nothing behind to differ on. Only the workspace shows it. 6 contract tests live in Fogell.Differential.Tests beside the rule; the Groovy tests keep a STUB predicate rather than a project reference, since keeping the vocabulary out of that layer is why `find` takes a parameter at all **THE FOURTEENTH: THE FLAG'S TYPE DECIDES, AND RENDERED TEXT CANNOT CARRY A TYPE.** The flags were read as `Trim().ToLowerInvariant() = "true"`, and BOTH shapes that exposes were MEASURED on the pinned lab rather than argued about — the last verifier finding that was materially wrong (`retry(0)`) was also a claim about Jenkins argument validation, so this one was probed first. (a) `returnStatus: ' true '` — Jenkins treats it as FALSE and `exit 7` FAILS the build; Fogell trimmed, suppressed the exit and ran the following step. (b) `returnStatus: 1` — Jenkins REJECTS the call before running anything (`IllegalArgumentException: Could not instantiate {script=…, returnStatus=1} for ShellStep`, workspace EMPTY); Fogell ran the shell and reported success. `returnStatus: true` and `returnStatus: 'true'` RENDER IDENTICALLY, so no amount of string inspection can separate them — the type has to come from somewhere else, and it already exists in two places: `Step.ExpressionArgs` records a stage-level argument written UNQUOTED, and inside a `script` block the typed `HostedArgs` value IS the argument. `WalkerRules.returnFlag` now returns `FlagOn | FlagOff | FlagRejected reason`, and a rejection STOPS THE STEP BEFORE IT RUNS via `Executor.refusedBeforeRunning` — Jenkins leaves the workspace empty, so an engine that ran the shell and complained afterwards would still differ on the hash. There is NO 'assume off' state, because assuming off is precisely what turned an unusable flag into a silent success. **A DECLARED NARROWING, sized against the corpus rather than guessed**: only a literal boolean counts, so the quoted `'true'` Jenkins would coerce is REFUSED instead. All 134 uses across the 228-file corpus are the literal form (117 `returnStdout: true`, 11 `returnStatus: true`, 6 unspaced), so it costs nothing measurable, and the alternative was importing `Boolean.valueOf` semantics inferred from one data point — permissive guessing being the entire class of finding here. UNPROVEN by receipt and deliberately so: after the fix both probes agree on terminal result AND workspace hash and differ only in OUTPUT TEXT — Jenkins runs the doomed shell, or prints a Java stack trace no engine can match. That is the FG-129 shape, not an oversight **THE FIFTEENTH: A DUPLICATE NAMED ARGUMENT, and the first fix on this branch placed at PARSE TIME.** `sh(script: 'exit 7', returnStatus: true, returnStatus: 'false')` — Fogell took the FIRST flag, suppressed the exit, ran the following step, reported SUCCESS. The verifier substantiated the Fogell half and ASSERTED the Jenkins half, so it was probed before acting, exactly as the `retry(0)` round taught: MEASURED in BOTH spellings (inside a `script` block and at stage level), Jenkins fails with NOTHING in the log but `Started by user unknown or anonymous` and an EMPTY workspace. Groovy assembles a call's named arguments as a MAP LITERAL and the duplicate does not survive it, so the rejection is at COMPILE time. **WHERE it goes is the whole judgement.** Refusing at dispatch — the smaller change, and what every previous fix in this ticket did — would have been indistinguishable ON THIS PROBE, because the duplicate sits in the first step. It is plainly wrong for a duplicate in a LATER stage: Jenkins runs nothing, while a dispatch-time refusal lets every earlier stage run first. Matching one example is not matching Jenkins. BOTH PARSERS enforce it, declarative and scripted, and the scripted one in BOTH its paths (parenthesised and command form) — a rule held by only one path is the shape of half the findings on this branch. UNPROVEN by receipt (FG-129: a compile-shaped refusal emits nothing comparable); after the fix the script-form probe agrees on terminal result AND workspace and the stage form is NOT-COMPARABLE, both the documented shape. Same class, opposite placement; getting it backwards diverges on everything before the failing stage. | FG-175 | **P1** | **DONE** | A parse-time REFUSAL inside a `when` condition is swallowed by the section fallback, so the stage skips where Jenkins rejects the pipeline | FOUND AS THE SIXTEENTH FINDING of FG-174's class, and split out because it is a DESIGN change rather than the patch that round wanted. MEASURED on the pinned lab: `when { equals expected: 1, actual: 1, actual: 2 }` and `when { environment name: 'T', value: 'a', value: 'b' }` both make Jenkins fail with nothing in the log but `Started by user unknown or anonymous` and an EMPTY workspace — Groovy rejects the duplicated named parameter at COMPILE time, so no stage runs. Fogell runs the EARLIER stage, skips the gated one, and reports SUCCESS. A false success. THE DUPLICATE RULE IS ALREADY WIRED INTO BOTH CONDITIONS and still cannot reach the caller: `when` falls back to `whenSectionOpaque`, which consumes any unparseable body as raw text and marks the condition UNMODELLED, so the refusal backtracks INTO the fallback. Making the keyword commit was tried and does not help — the fallback sits above it — and it would turn conditions Fogell currently tolerates into hard failures, which is risk with no benefit. Reverted rather than left in. THE REAL PROBLEM is that the section fallbacks cannot distinguish a shape Fogell DOES NOT MODEL (fail closed, keep going — FG-152, deliberate and load-bearing) from a shape Fogell REFUSES (reject the pipeline). One backtracking mechanism serves both, so the second is unreachable wherever a fallback exists. Same root as the FG-046b approval bypasses, where `steps` backtracked to EMPTY and published no prompt. Fix: a refusal must be a distinct signal that a fallback re-raises instead of absorbing — a user-state flag or a Result-carrying parser, both of which touch every section. STEP ARGUMENTS ARE ALREADY FIXED and unaffected: that path has no fallback, so its refusal reaches the caller (FG-174). **SCOPE WIDENED BY A SEVENTEENTH FINDING, which was MY OWN SENTENCE rather than new behaviour**: the FG-174 comment claimed `tag`, `branch`, `changelog` and `triggeredBy` 'take a SINGLE named argument and cannot duplicate one'. They can. `when { tag pattern: 'v1', pattern: 'v2' }` leaves the second pair UNCONSUMED, the condition fails, and the SAME opaque fallback absorbs the section — identical outcome by a different route, so the enumeration was right about the shapes and wrong about their consequence. Guarding those parsers would change nothing while the fallback stands, which is why they belong here and not in another patch. Corrected in place, and note the shape of the miss: a wrong FACTUAL sentence is invisible to the citation checker built earlier in this branch, which only proves that a named receipt EXISTS. SCOPE: audit every `attempt`-wrapped section for the same swallow, not just `when`. **SCOPE IS THE FALLBACK, NOT A LIST OF CONDITIONS — arrived at after FOUR rounds of my enumerations each missing one more.** `equals`, then the single-key conditions, then `changeset`: every time I listed the callers from memory and review found the next one. The tripwires now cover the seven conditions that accept a named argument (`equals`, `environment`, `tag`, `branch`, `changelog`, `triggeredBy`, `changeset`), enumerated FROM THE PARSER's own `whenCondition` alternatives rather than recalled, and the count is grep-checked. **A SECOND ROUTE FOUND BY A TEST THAT WAS WRONG**: a test written to assert the general property — that leftover arguments always reach `whenSectionOpaque` — FAILED, because `changeRequest target: 'a', target: 'b'` instead parses as TWO conditions implicitly ANDed, `WhenAllOf [WhenChangeRequest; WhenUnmodelled("target", …)]`. Stray argument text becomes an EXTRA CONDITION rather than a parse failure. The user-visible outcome is the same (unmodelled → fail closed → the stage SKIPS where Jenkins rejects the pipeline) but the MECHANISM differs, so a fix aimed only at the opaque fallback leaves this route open. That is now pinned too. So the acceptance criterion is behavioural, not a checklist: no `when` body that Jenkins rejects at compile time may leave Fogell reporting SUCCESS. Counting conditions was never the hard part **DONE 2026-08-22.** A per-parse mutable refusal cell now preserves the first conclusive semantic refusal across every FParsec `attempt`; admission checks it before returning a parsed pipeline. All eight named-argument conditions consume command and parenthesised groups through one value-independent duplicate guard, so expression-, call-, list-, map-, and closure-valued duplicates cannot escape through an opaque fallback; `changeRequest` filters can no longer become an implicit sibling, and the signal survives `allOf`/`anyOf`/`not` recursion. Empty/directive-only gates, nested or malformed directives, the measured-invalid `changeset glob:` key, and non-empty parenthesised arguments on genuinely zero-argument conditions are also admission refusals. The deliberate opaque lane remains for unknown/plugin-owned condition names. Receipt `when-context-conditions` proves only that a valid unsupported `changeRequest target:` spelling is admitted and skips in absent change-request context while a control stage runs; the parser test pins its fail-closed representation, and present-context filter semantics remain open under FG-048c. Six direct Jenkins probes cover command, parenthesised, nested, invalid-key, and invalid-directive routes: all fail compilation before Jenkins creates a workspace, with raw consoles, job/build IDs, Jenkins version, and a SHA-256 manifest preserved in the evidence bundle. | parser 86/86; authoritative gate 501/501; `prove-section-refusals.sh` covers command, parenthesised, opaque-value duplicates, a nonduplicate fail-closed overshoot control, invalid-key, invalid-directive and nested routes before effects; oracle probes in `evidence/20260822T023145Z-fg-175-probes`; receipt `when-context-conditions` re-sealed PROVEN with the valid-filter overshoot control |
| FG-176 | **P1** | **DONE** | A step failure inside `script { }` is not CATCHABLE, so `try/catch` cannot recover and Fogell fails a build Jenkins passes | **DONE 2026-08-17, WITH FG-186.** A failing SHELL step now raises the catchable fault Jenkins raises (`StepFailed`, surfaced through the one sanctioned host channel — the interpreter's `Stop` stays private, and the first probe run found F#'s cross-module inliner exposing the private constructor, fixed with NoInlining): `try/catch` recovers, the handler's own steps RUN, and an uncaught failure still fails the build with the step after it never running — receipts `script-try-catches-sh-failure` and `script-uncaught-sh-still-fails`, the fix and its overshoot guard sealed together. THE SCOPE IS THE DISTINCTION: only `sh` failures convert (`bat` is outside the script vocabulary, so a bat arm would be dead code wearing a parity claim) — a Fogell refusal caught by a script would recover from a gap in this engine while Jenkins ran the real step, so refusals stay uncatchable, and non-shell step failures keep the old fail-loud path until their own measurements move them (each is a false-failure divergence, conservative, recorded). The catch-type list mirrors AbortException's ancestry — NOT RuntimeException, and bare `AbortException` itself is deliberately absent (`hudson.*` is not a Groovy default import and the script arm discards preamble imports, so Jenkins likely cannot resolve the name — catching here would risk a false success; the verifier constructed it, unprobed). RECORDED LIMIT: the bound exception text carries the step name and not the EXIT CODE — `StepFailed` does not carry it — so a handler printing `${e}` would diverge on the code; measured at the untyped spelling, ancestry unprobed. The original measurement:  RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #53 and MEASURED on the pinned lab: `script { try { sh 'exit 1' } catch (e) { sh 'echo recovered > recovered.txt' }; sh 'echo after > after.txt' }` gives Jenkins **success** with both files written, and Fogell **failure** with an EMPTY workspace. `runStepDispatch` marks `BranchCtx.Failed` and returns normally, so the interpreter never sees a fault, `STry` never runs its handler, and `halted` suppresses everything after. Jenkins throws a catchable step exception instead. DIRECTION MATTERS: this is a FALSE FAILURE, not a false success — the build stops loudly rather than reporting green on work it skipped, so it is the conservative error and does not belong to the ADR 0001 worst class. That is why it is filed rather than rushed. WHY IT IS NOT A PATCH: making the host raise into the interpreter is the easy half. The hard half is what CATCHING means for branch state — `halted` currently guards dispatch, so a caught failure would still suppress the handler's own steps, and clearing `Failed` has to not lose a genuine build failure that nothing catches. Uncaught throws must still fail the build, which means the `script` arm has to distinguish a propagated Groovy exception from a refusal. COST OF THE ALTERNATIVE, measured rather than assumed: refusing `try` inside a script block would fail closed instead, but 31 of the 228 corpus files use `try {` and roughly 4 have one inside a `script` block, so it trades a visible wrong answer for lost admission. Not taken without a decision. Same family as FG-175: both are places where `script { }` cannot yet express a control-flow shape Jenkins has | scratch probe (jenkins=success+2 files, fogell=failure+empty) |
| FG-177 | **P1** | **PARTIAL** | Hosted call validation is data-driven; genuine-null, wrapper-body, retained-history SCM-map, and JUnit four-count/marking returns are modelled, while JUnit's object/raw-UI/report-ingest residuals remain | THE PATTERN, not a defect: `hostedSignatureError` has one arm per step, and across FG-172/FG-174 that design produced **ELEVEN** findings — `withEnv` non-list, `withEnv` malformed entries, `dir` body-less, `retry` type-not-sign, `timeout` MISSING ENTIRELY, `timeout` two positionals, `timeout` mixed positional+named, `timeout(1, time: 2)`, the return flags treated as universal / orthogonal / textual, and finally `retry(count: 2)` REFUSED THOUGH VALID. **BOTH DIRECTIONS**, which is the part that makes this architectural rather than sloppy: the arms have been too PERMISSIVE (a false success, work Jenkins never ran) and too STRICT (a false refusal, a pipeline Jenkins accepts). A hand-written arm encodes what I happened to think of on the day, and there is no shape that tells me what I missed. Each fix was individually correct and individually measured; the count is the evidence. `WalkerRules.hostedWrappersWithSignatureCase` (since retired into `hostedSignatures` row coverage, FG-177 slice 1) closed the ONE case a checker could close — a wrapper with NO arm — and everything else remains hand-checked. WHAT REPLACES IT: Jenkins derives call validation from a step's `StepDescriptor` — parameter names, types, whether each is required, and whether a positional form exists. A declarative schema per step, checked by one normaliser before dispatch, turns 'which spellings does this step take' into DATA that can be reviewed in one place instead of eleven arms read one review round at a time. It also gives the corpus something to be scored against. SCOPE: the hosted wrappers first (`dir`, `timeout`, `retry`, `withEnv`), then `sh`/`bat`'s own options, which `WalkerRules.returnContract` already models separately and would fold in. NOT urgent-by-defect — every known shape is now handled — but urgent-by-trend: the twelfth is otherwise found the same way as the first eleven **THIRTEEN NOW**, and the twelfth and thirteenth are what changed the fix from an arm to a RULE: `script('ignored') { … }` ran its body (the `script` arm guarded on `ScriptBody` alone), and `sh('cmd', 'ignored')` ran the FIRST positional and dropped the second, because `hostedSignatureError` had a SILENT PASS for every step without an arm. Both measured — Jenkins rejects each and leaves the workspace EMPTY. Rather than a twelfth and thirteenth arm, the catch-all became a DEFAULT DENY on arity: Jenkins maps a single positional onto a step's sole required parameter, and none of the fourteen steps in the script vocabulary takes two — checked against that closed list, not assumed. One rule closed `sh` and every other unmodelled step at once. Receipts `script-takes-no-arguments` and `script-sh-two-positionals`. IT IS STILL ONLY AN ARITY RULE, which is the honest limit and the reason this ticket stays open: no unknown NAMED argument is rejected anywhere. **THIS SENTENCE ALSO SAID `deleteDir('x')` IS ADMITTED WITH AN ARGUMENT, WHICH THE SAME SLICE MADE FALSE** — `positionalArity` records `deleteDir` at ZERO and receipt `script-deletedir-argument` holds the refusal. The example was written as the motivation for the rule and then left standing as an outstanding gap after the rule closed it, in the row that exists to track exactly which gaps remain. A per-step schema is what closes those **FIFTEEN**, and the fifteenth finally located a rule in the right SCOPE. `sh('exit 7', returnStatus: true)` — positional AND named together — was admitted, ran, returned a status, and a `code == 7` branch wrote a file off a value Jenkins never produced. The mixed-form rule already existed, but only inside the `timeout` arm: the RIGHT RULE IN THE WRONG SCOPE, which is this ticket's thesis in one line. Jenkins' CPS `DSL.parseArgs` throws `Expected named arguments but got …` for ANY step, so it now sits ABOVE the per-step match, and `timeout`'s branch was DELETED rather than left as an unreachable second opinion. MEASURED ON TWO STEPS before generalising — `sh` and `archiveArtifacts`, same failure, same workspace hash — because assuming a rule is step-independent from one observation is how the arity default got written. Receipt `script-mixed-positional-named`. THE SHAPE OF THE REMAINING WORK IS NOW CLEARER: of the fifteen, some were genuinely per-step (`deleteDir` takes no argument) and some were universal (mixed args, extra positionals). A StepDescriptor schema needs BOTH layers — the CPS call-shape rules that hold for everything, and per-step parameter data — and conflating them is what produced both a too-narrow rule and a too-broad default in the same ticket **SIXTEEN, AND SLICE 2 LANDED.** `dir(path: 'sub')` and `withEnv(overrides: […])` — a sole required parameter written BY NAME, which Jenkins treats as identical to the positional form — were REFUSED. Measured: both succeed on Jenkins, both failed here with an empty workspace. That is the layer `positionalArity` could not supply: it says HOW MANY positionals a step takes, not what the one parameter is CALLED. `WalkerRules.soleRequiredParameter` supplies the name, and `normaliseHostedCall` rewrites the named spelling into the positional one ONCE — before validation AND before dispatch, so no arm learns two spellings. **ORDER IS THE SUBTLETY, and getting it backwards would have broken every existing receipt**: the CPS mixed-argument rule reads the call AS WRITTEN, and only then is it normalised. Normalising first would move `script` out of `sh(script: 'x', returnStatus: true)` and MANUFACTURE the mixed shape the rule rejects. `script-withenv-named-overrides` is the case that proves normalisation reaches DISPATCH rather than only validation — `withEnv` reads its typed list from the positional slot, so admitting the named form without rewriting it would have bound NOTHING and reported success, which is the silent-empty-binding defect FG-172 already fixed once for the display-string form. A test now holds the two tables in agreement: arity 1 iff a named parameter exists, so a step cannot be added to one and forgotten in the other **RETURN-VALUE SLICE IDENTIFIED AND MEASURED: WHAT A STEP RETURNS.** The two tables so far describe how a step is CALLED — arity, and the sole required parameter's name. Neither says what it RETURNS, and that gap is a false refusal: `def got = echo('hello'); if (got == null) { sh 'echo ran > ran.txt' }` SUCCEEDS on Jenkins and runs the guarded step, because `echo` returns null and the branch is taken; Fogell REJECTS THE WHOLE SCRIPT in `StepValueUse`, which treats every non-`sh` value use as unsupported. The refusal exists for a good reason — a value Fogell cannot model must not silently become null — but 'cannot model' and 'is genuinely null' are DIFFERENT FACTS and the pre-scan conflates them. The hosted callback ALREADY returns `VNull` for these steps, so the runtime is right and only the gate is wrong. **THE TRAP, and why this is not a one-line relaxation**: `checkout` returns a MAP of SCM variables in Jenkins (`def scmVars = checkout scm` is ordinary), so a blanket 'everything except sh returns null' would turn a false refusal into a FALSE SUCCESS — a script reading `scmVars.GIT_COMMIT` would get null and branch wrongly. That trades the safest class for the worst one. So it needs the same per-step data as the other two slices: a RETURN contract per step, `Null` for most of the vocabulary and something modelled for `checkout`, with `sh`/`bat`'s flag-dependent contract folding in as the case that already exists **SLICE 1 LANDED 2026-08-18: THE ARMS ARE DATA.** `WalkerRules.hostedSignatures` — one row per hosted wrapper (arity, named-key allowlist, shape text, the step's value rule) — checked by ONE validator; the four arms (~130 lines) are deleted, every measured caveat preserved as a row, and a hosted wrapper without a row fails a TEST. The verifier reconstructed each arm from git and diffed verdicts shape-by-shape: NO behavioural divergence in either direction; refusal wording unified (nothing asserted the old texts — swept). Its blocker was a citation dropped in the move (restored: receipt `script-retry-zero`), and the hand-written signature-case mirror set retired with its two-literals test — the table's own coverage test holds the same line. Full suite 187 of 187 on the landed binary. **SLICE 3 PROVEN 2026-08-21.** `dir`, `timeout`, `retry` and `withEnv` now publish the typed result of the body invocation selected by the host. The host seam remains a unit thunk; the interpreter captures explicit or implicit closure results per invocation, clears stale results before a retry, and refuses if a declared wrapper returns without running its body. Receipt `fg177-wrapper-body-result` proves all four wrappers, typed integer/list values and a failed-first/successful-second retry against Jenkins. CURRENT STATE: slices 1-5 plus FG-208 and FG-209 cover the shared descriptor/validator, directly proven genuine-null returns, wrapper body-result propagation, retained-history SCM maps, JUnit total/fail/skip/pass counts, independent build/stage marking, malformed/zero-byte recovery, and testcase-child authority. FG-210 closes the zero-result/default-plus-allowEmptyResults contract with six retained tier-1 receipts. FG-211 closes reached-owner direct-testcase identity with six retained tier-1 receipts. FG-212 closes the adjacent missing-testcase-name boundary with three retained tier-1 receipts and immutable 22-row bundle `evidence/20260823T015250Z-fg212-junit-missing-name`. JUnit object/raw-UI, generic decimal/numeric-width, and other report-ingest residuals remain open; FG-215 closed the bounded namespaced/local-name lookup semantics | 16 findings across FG-172/FG-174, each with its own receipt or probe; slices 1-5, the four-count JUnit projection and independent build/stage marking matrix landed; JUnit object/raw-UI/report-ingest surface remains |
| FG-178 | **P1** | **PARTIAL** | A hosted body re-enters the interpreter with the PRE-WRAPPER environment and no closure semantics — four defects, one seam | FOUND IN PR #53 REVIEW, and found late because I was polling `pulls/N/comments` FILTERED BY TIMESTAMP and missed whole threads — the CI monitor said green and I reported 'no new comments' twice while five unread P1s sat on the PR. The lesson is the one already recorded about hand-rolled polling: enumerate UNRESOLVED THREADS (`reviewThreads`), never a time window. **(a) THE WRAPPER'S ENV IS INVISIBLE TO ITS BODY — MEASURED.** `script { withEnv(['TARGET=prod']) { sh "echo saw:${env.TARGET}" } }` prints `saw:prod` on Jenkins and **`saw:null`** here. FG-172 made the wrapper's context reach the body's STEPS (cwd, overlay) but the interpreter's `env` MAP is built once before `runHosted` and the body's thunk closes over it, so GString interpolation reads the pre-wrapper snapshot. The seam's own promise — 'a wrapper runs its body inside the context it establishes' — holds for dispatch and not for evaluation. **(b) A COMPUTED `env[...]` INDEX IS EVALUATED TWICE**, so `env[sh(script: 'select-key', returnStdout: true)] = 'v'` RUNS THE SHELL TWICE. REPORTED, NOT MEASURED, and deliberately left so: `env` assignment inside a `script` is already REFUSED at the host, so the double evaluation happens and then the build fails closed. The wasted side effect is real; the wrong ANSWER is not reachable while that refusal stands, which is why this one is ranked below the other three rather than beside them. **(c) `return` INSIDE A HOSTED BODY IS NOT CLOSURE-LOCAL — MEASURED.** `script { dir('sub') { return }; sh 'echo after > after.txt' }`: Jenkins WRITES `after.txt` (Groovy returns from the CLOSURE), Fogell's workspace is EMPTY — `ReturnSignal` escapes the thunk and ends the whole script. Silently skipped work, reported as success. **(d) CAPTURED LOCALS DO NOT PERSIST ACROSS RE-INVOCATIONS — MEASURED, and it inverts a build's result.** `def n = 0; retry(2) { n = n + 1; …; if (n == 1) sh 'exit 1' }`: Jenkins SUCCEEDS — attempt 1 fails with n=1, attempt 2 passes with n=2 — while Fogell FAILS, because the thunk discards the environment `execBlock` returns and n is 1 on every attempt. A retry that cannot make progress is not a retry. ONE CLUSTER, not four tickets: each is the same missing idea — the thunk is a CLOSURE and is being treated as a detached block. It needs the wrapper's environment AT INVOCATION rather than at creation, and it needs closure semantics for `return` and for mutated captures. That is a design change to `PerformStep`, which is why it is filed rather than patched at the end of a long branch **THREE OF THE FOUR ARE FIXED AND RECEIPTED**, all by the one idea the ticket names: the body is a CLOSURE. (a) `CurrentEnv` is asked at each invocation instead of captured, and BOTH spellings are rebound — a bare `TARGET` and `env.TARGET` — because refreshing only the map fixes the measured interpolation and leaves the bare name stale, which is half a fix that looks whole. Receipt `script-withenv-body-sees-env`. (c) the thunk catches `ReturnSignal`, exactly as the ordinary closure-call path already did; the hosted path simply never did. Receipt `script-return-is-closure-local`. (d) the environment `execBlock` RETURNS is carried across invocations in a cell. Receipt `script-retry-keeps-locals`, whose marker file APPENDS so the attempt count lands in the workspace hash. KNOWN LIMIT of (d) — **RETRACTED 2026-08-13, it was closed by the same slice**: a variable DECLARED inside the body was said to persist between invocations where Groovy creates it afresh. The post-body environment is filtered back to the name set present BEFORE the invocation, so a body's declarations are dropped. The filter and the sentence doubting it sat ten lines apart in one function. Visible only to a body that declares a name and then depends on it being unset next time — far narrower than losing every mutation, and the opposite direction from the defect it replaced. **A SEVENTEENTH FINDING WAS MATERIALLY WRONG, and probing it is the only reason a false refusal did not ship.** Review reported that Jenkins REJECTS `echo()` and asked for a required-argument lower bound beside the arity upper bound. MEASURED: Jenkins ACCEPTS it, prints the literal `null`, and runs the following step. Implementing the finding as written would have refused a pipeline Jenkins accepts. The real divergence was one output line — Fogell printed nothing, because the arm set `Stdout` without ever calling `OnLine`, and the differential compares what STREAMED. Receipt `script-echo-no-message`. SECOND TIME on this branch a review claim about Jenkins has been wrong (`retry(0)` was the first), and both were caught the same way: probe before implementing. The verifier's hit rate is high enough that acting directly is tempting, and these two are why the rule holds. (b) REMAINS: a computed `env[...]` index is evaluated twice. Still ranked last for the reason given above — env assignment inside a script is refused at the host, so the duplicate side effect WAS real until FG-193's restructure removed it (2026-08-17, the index evaluates once now) and the wrong ANSWER stays unreachable while that refusal stands | 3 fixed with receipts; 1 open |

WHAT IS NOT ASSERTED BY THE CORRECTION: whether FG-188's preamble made the parameter spelling measurable again. | FG-181 | **P1** | **DONE** | A shell's captured stdout is DISCARDED when an escaped descendant holds the stream open, so `sh(returnStdout: true)` returns the WRONG VALUE under a green build | RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #53 AS P2 AND RE-RANKED ON MEASUREMENT. The case: `script { def out = sh(script: 'setsid sleep 20 & printf token', returnStdout: true); sh "printf 'captured:[%s]' '${out}'" }`. Jenkins prints `captured:[%s] token`; Fogell prints `captured:[%s]` — the captured value is the EMPTY STRING where Jenkins captured `token`. **BOTH ENGINES REPORT SUCCESS**, and that is the whole ticket: the divergence is a VALUE, not a result, so every branch taken off that value is decided wrongly under a build that looks clean. ADR 0001's worst class, which is why the reviewer's P2 does not survive contact with the receipt. Reproduced on all THREE harness attempts — the differential re-runs a divergence twice — so it is not the known `sh -x` trace race. MECHANISM: the capture path reads the stream with `proc.StandardOutput.ReadToEndAsync()`, which cannot complete while a descendant that escaped the process group still holds the inherited write end open. The bounded wait expires and the fallback substitutes `""`, throwing away bytes ALREADY READ. **THE FALLBACK IS MINE, AND IT REPLACED A WORSE ONE.** FG-174 first fell back to `stdout.ToString()`, which is always empty in capture mode — wrong every time. The fix made it honestly empty, and honestly empty is still wrong here; it is now wrong only when a descendant escapes, which is rarer and therefore harder to find. That is a second-order defect of my own fix and it is recorded as one. COST BEYOND THE VALUE: the case took 26s of wall clock where Jenkins finishes at once — the five-second bound, paid three times. FIX DIRECTION, and the constraint on it: retain what was read before the bound expires — incremental reads into a buffer, or a temporary capture file as Jenkins' own durable-task does — so that a timeout TRUNCATES rather than ERASES. Do NOT simply wait longer: the bound is what stops an escaped descendant from holding the build open, and FG-031/032 containment reaps it deliberately. The divergence is in what was captured BEFORE the reaping, not in the reaping **FIXED.** The capture accumulates into a buffer this scope owns, read incrementally, so an expired bound TRUNCATES what arrived instead of erasing it. The bound itself is unchanged — it is what stops an escaped descendant holding the engine open, which is the one failure mode worse than a wrong value, and truncation removes the need to choose between them. Receipt `script-capture-escaped-descendant`, PROVEN TO FAIL against this code reverted: `raw:[token]` vs `raw:[]`, diverging on the value AND the workspace hash, on 2 of 2 divergence re-runs. The case uses `setsid sleep 10` against a five-second wait so the read is still open when the bound expires on any host — a shorter sleep would let it pass for the wrong reason on a slow one | receipt `script-capture-escaped-descendant` proven tier 1; the three existing capture receipts stay proven; suite 151/151 |
