| FG-182 | **P1** | **DONE** | A hosted call Jenkins REFUSES halts the SCRIPT but not the retry ATTEMPT it is running in, so the next call in the same closure still runs | RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #53 AND MEASURED. `script { retry(1) { sh('invalid', 'extra'); sh 'touch ran-after-invalid.txt' } }`: Jenkins rejects the two-positional call, STOPS THE CLOSURE THERE, and the workspace is EMPTY; Fogell refused the call correctly and then ran the next one, writing the marker. A durable effect from a body Jenkins had already abandoned — the ADR 0001 class, not a bookkeeping slip, and BOTH ENGINES FAIL THE BUILD so a result-only comparison passes it. Same shape as FG-181 one layer up. **THE DEFECT WAS A SCOPE, AND THE DESIGN IT BYPASSED WAS ALREADY CORRECT.** `runWithRetry` gives every attempt a fresh `Failed` ref and a throwaway status sink on purpose — a body that fails once and then succeeds is a SUCCESS build (FG-035, receipt `retry-succeeds`) — and `runBodyIn` points `hostAt` at that attempt while its body runs. The script host's refusal path marked the captured `ctx` instead, so the OUTER flag was set, the attempt still looked clean to `halted`, and the second call was admitted. FIXED by declaring `hostAt` above `fail` and reading the active context from it; outside a wrapper body the cell still holds `(ctx, cwd)`, so the argument and parse refusals are unchanged. NOT ANOTHER SCOPING STAND-IN, which is why it was patched rather than filed behind FG-179: there is one right answer here — the attempt currently installed — and the fix makes the existing per-attempt design APPLY rather than adding a mechanism beside it | receipt `script-retry-halts-attempt-on-refusal`, proven tier 1 and PROVEN TO FAIL against this code reverted (workspace `e3b0c44298fc` vs `051220bdb26b`, on 2 of 2 divergence re-runs) |
| FG-183 | **P1** | **DONE** | `break` and `continue` outside a loop are ADMITTED, the earlier stages RUN, and the engine then faults with an uncaught signal where Jenkins refuses the file at compile time | RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #53 AND MEASURED ON BOTH ENGINES. The case: a first stage that writes `early.txt`, then `script { dir('d') { break } }`. **JENKINS NEVER STARTS A STAGE** — `CpsFlowExecution.parseScript` fails the Groovy compile with `break` naming line 16 column 25, the build is FAILURE, and the workspace is empty. Fogell ADMITS the file, runs stage `Early` to completion (`step-finished Early 0 success`, `early.txt` on disk), enters the script, and the run ends `run failed: BreakSignal`. TWO DEFECTS IN ONE MEASUREMENT, and they rank differently. **(a) A DURABLE EFFECT JENKINS NEVER PRODUCED** — the file exists here and cannot exist there, which is the ADR 0001 class and the reason this is P1 rather than a tidiness ticket. **(b) THE ENGINE FAULTS RATHER THAN REJECTING.** `BreakSignal` escapes to the top; the differential harness scores the case NOT-COMPARABLE because the Fogell side did not produce a build at all. ADR 0001 tier 3 means REJECTED WITH A NAMED CODE AND A POSITION, and an uncaught exception is not that — it is the one outcome no tier describes. Jenkins hands back a code and a position for exactly this input, so the bar is set by the oracle. WHERE IT BELONGS: admission, not runtime. A parser SUCCESS is not a validity verdict — the Groovy parser accepts `break` unconditionally because placement is a context rule, not a grammar one — so the check is a walk of the parsed body asking whether each `break`/`continue` has an enclosing loop, with a closure boundary counting as a stop. SAME SHAPE AS FG-175, which is the reason to expect more of these: a refusal that exists at one layer and not at the layer the HTTP endpoint actually enters. Do NOT fix it by catching the signal at the top — that would convert an engine fault into a stage failure and STILL run the earlier stages, trading a loud wrong answer for a quiet one **FIXED AT ADMISSION.** `LoopControl.misplaced` walks the parsed body and refuses a `break`/`continue` with no enclosing loop, inside `scriptBodyErrors` — so every entry point inherits it, including the one FG-185 had left open. Fogell now answers `malformed_syntax at 30:17: script block: \`break\` outside a loop`, a named code with a position, which is what tier 3 means. THE CLOSURE BOUNDARY IS THE LOAD-BEARING PART: `inLoop` RESETS entering a closure or a function body rather than being inherited, because Groovy rejects `break` inside a closure even when the closure sits inside a loop — a check that inherited the enclosing loop would admit `while (x) { [1].each { break } }`. The expression walk has NO WILDCARD ARM on purpose: a closure can appear wherever a value can, so a catch-all would silently stop checking whichever form was added next, and FS0025 makes that a build error instead. THE POSITION IS THE SCRIPT STEP'S, not the keyword's — a stated limit, since this AST carries none, and the same granularity the malformed-body path already reports at. **NOT A DIFFERENTIAL CASE, and the reason is the ticket's own point**: Fogell now REFUSES where Jenkins FAILS A BUILD, and the harness cannot compare a refusal against a build — it scored the attempt NOT-COMPARABLE. The proof is `prove-section-refusals.sh`, which asserts the refusal AND that no earlier stage ran; its existing helper could not tell an admission refusal from a runtime fault, since both leave a diagnostic in the log, and only the absent marker separates them | `prove-section-refusals.sh`: both arms fail against this change reverted with `run failed: BreakSignal` / `ContinueSignal`, and the `break`-inside-a-loop control still completes success **AND IT NEARLY SHIPPED AS A FALSE REFUSAL — THE PRE-PUSH VERIFIER CAUGHT IT.** The Groovy parser lowers `switch` to nested ifs, so an ordinary `case 'a': break` reached the new check looking identical to `dir('d') { break }` and was REFUSED: valid Groovy that Jenkins runs, rejected at admission. THE THIRD OVER-BROAD FIX ON THIS BRANCH, and the first stopped before it landed. WHY THE PROOF SCRIPT COULD NOT CATCH IT: every arm in `prove-section-refusals.sh` asserts that something IS refused, and over-refusal satisfies all of them — the controls there cover the sections that file is about, not `switch`. A checker only fails in the direction it was written to test. FIXED IN THE LOWERING, where the switch context still exists: an arm's TRAILING `break` is consumed, because that is the statement's whole meaning in a nested-if form whose branches are already mutually exclusive — which is also why the admission check needs no notion of `switch`. Receipt `script-switch-case-break`, tier 1, and NOT-COMPARABLE against the lowering reverted because Fogell refuses the file. A `break` elsewhere in an arm (`case 'a': if (x) break; more()`) is still refused: the nested-if form cannot leave an arm early, so it is a shape Fogell does not model, and the refusal is now WORDED FOR BOTH READINGS rather than claiming Jenkins rejects it — which for that shape would be false **AND THE SECOND INSTANCE OF THE SAME CLASS ARRIVED ON THAT VERY FIX, AGAIN FROM THE VERIFIER, AGAIN BEFORE PUSH.** Consuming only the arm-final `break` left `case 'a': if (x) break; more()` in the tree as a bare `SBreak`. With no enclosing loop the admission check refused it, so the fix looked complete — INSIDE A LOOP `inLoop` is true, the check admits it, and the interpreter's loop handler catches the signal as a LOOP break. MEASURED: `while (true) { switch ('a') { case 'a': if (true) break; … }; sh 'touch after-switch.txt' }` reported **SUCCESS** having skipped `after-switch.txt`, where Groovy leaves only the switch and runs it. A green build skipping work — ADR 0001's worst class — from a `break` that means one thing to the language and another to the tree it was lowered into. **IT ALSO FALSIFIED THE PREVIOUS FIX'S OWN COMMENT**, which said such a break 'is left for the admission check to refuse': true with no enclosing loop, false with one, written without the qualifier that decided it. FIXED by refusing any remaining arm `break` IN THE GROOVY PARSER, before the lowering, while the arm is still known — which also lets the admission check go back to naming the one thing it knows, since nothing reaching it is a switch arm any more. Proof case `switch-arm-break-in-loop`; against the parser refusal reverted it reports `completed: success`. **THE SEAM IS NOW TWO-FOR-TWO AND THAT IS A STOP-RULE SIGNAL, RECORDED RATHER THAN ACTED ON**: both instances trace to one root cause — the AST has no `switch`, so the lowering destroys the context the checker AND the interpreter need. The refusals are honest and narrow, and a third finding here should be spent on modelling `switch` (an `SSwitch` node with its own break boundary), not on a third guard **THE THIRD ARRIVED IMMEDIATELY, THE VERIFIER CITED THAT VERY SENTENCE BACK, AND `SSwitch` WAS BUILT.** The parser-level refusal over-refused `case 'a': if (true) break` — valid Groovy that Jenkins accepts and continues past. Three positions on one question, each defensible against the previous failure and wrong about the next, which is the signature of compensating for missing structure instead of supplying it. `switch` is now a REAL AST NODE with the arms in source order: the interpreter catches `BreakSignal` at the switch (so an arm's break leaves the switch, not an enclosing loop) and lets `ContinueSignal` pass through, and the admission walk carries TWO flags instead of one because a switch legalises exactly ONE of the two keywords — a single `inLoop` could not say 'break is fine here, continue is not', which is why every earlier position had to guess. **FALLTHROUGH IS NOW MODELLED**, which the lowering could not express at all (nested ifs are mutually exclusive) and which the old comment recorded as a gap justified by the corpus rather than by the language. TWO IMPLEMENTATION BUGS CAUGHT BY MEASURING RATHER THAN REASONING: folding each arm with `execBlock` discarded every assignment made before a break, because the unwind skips the fold's return — Jenkins `log:[aBbDz] fall:[PQ]` against `log:[abDz] fall:[P]`. Fixed by executing arm statements individually. KNOWN LIMIT STATED: an assignment inside a NESTED block before a break is still lost, a shape `SWhile` has had all along; both dissolve under FG-179's ref cells, and a second partial-env mechanism here would be the guard that ticket exists to stop. Receipts `script-switch-break-and-fallthrough` (break inside an if inside an arm inside a LOOP, default by position, and a genuine fallthrough) and `script-switch-case-break`, both tier 1. The `switch-arm-break-in-loop` refusal arm was DELETED rather than kept green: it asserted a refusal for a shape that is now correctly supported, and a proof arm that outlives its defect is a check asserting the wrong thing |

| FG-184 | **P1** | **DONE** | A closure passed as an ARGUMENT to a step that takes no block was stripped before validation, so the call ran and the build went GREEN where Jenkins fails | RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #53 AND MEASURED. `script { def body = {}; sh('touch ran.txt', body) }`: Jenkins FAILS with an EMPTY workspace, Fogell reported **SUCCESS** and wrote the file. **THE PUREST FALSE SUCCESS ON THIS BRANCH** — not a divergent value like FG-181, not a divergent effect under a shared failure like FG-182, but a green build performing work Jenkins refused to start. **THREE CORRECT RULES ALL MISSED IT, and the order is the lesson.** The static block scan reads `body` as an `EVar` — it is only a closure at RUNTIME, so no scan of the source can see it. The FG-177 arity default-deny refuses a two-positional `sh` but never saw two, because normalisation had already moved the closure into a hosted body. And `sh`'s dispatcher ignores a hosted body, so the move was silent — a wrapper would at least have run the block. The value never reached any of the three. FIXED by asking the host whether a step takes a block before normalising a final closure into one, answered from `WalkerRules.scriptWrappersWithHostedBody` — the same table `runStepDispatch` uses, so the normalisation and the dispatch cannot disagree. The interpreter cannot answer this itself: which steps take a block is a property of the step vocabulary the host owns, and copying the set into the interpreter is how the three copies of the timestamp rule came to disagree. Left in place for a plain step, the closure stays a second positional and the arity rule refuses it — the rule FG-177 already established, now actually reached. WITHOUT A HOST the shape is unchanged, and that is not a second behaviour: the batch model never DISPATCHES a step, so there is no call for the answer to be wrong about | receipt `script-closure-argument-to-plain-step`, proven tier 1 and PROVEN TO FAIL against this code reverted (jenkins=failure/empty vs fogell=success/`faa23ed5806b`, on 3 of 3 divergence re-runs) |

| FG-185 | P2 | **DONE** | Script-body validation was applied by `parse` only, leaving the equally public `parseWithLimits` fail-open | RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #53. A caller choosing custom limits received `Ok` for a pipeline containing `script { def x = }` that the default entry point REJECTS, restoring for that caller exactly the delayed execution-time failure the check exists to prevent. **FOURTH ENUMERATION OF THE SAME RULE** — stage steps, then `post`, then the HTTP endpoint, now custom limits — and the third time the fix carried a comment claiming completeness: `parse` said 'which entry points are covered stops being a question: there is one', written while a second public entry point stood one function above it. FIXED by moving the validation into `parseWithLimits` and reducing `parse` to supplying the defaults, so there is no second path left to forget. THE TEST IS A PAIR, both entry points on one source, because asserting only the `parseWithLimits` half would pass a build that moved the check and broke `parse`; the invariant is that the two cannot disagree about admissibility. It uses NON-DEFAULT limits deliberately — `Limits.defaults` goes down the path `parse` already covers and would prove nothing | `Fogell.Pipeline.Parser` 52 tests; the new pair fails against this change reverted (`parseWithLimits admitted a malformed script body`) |

| FG-186 | **P1** | **DONE** | `retry` DOES NOT RETRY a body that faults, so Fogell fails a build Jenkins passes | **DONE 2026-08-17, WITH FG-176 AND BY THE ONE DISTINCTION BOTH ROWS ARGUED FOR** — catchable faults (a throw, a missing property, a failed SHELL step) versus refusals, which stay untouchable: `Interpreter.catchRetryable` converts the first class into an attempt failure and the FINAL attempt re-raises the held fault so an uncaught failure carries its own diagnostic out. Both measured shapes receipt-PROVEN: `script-retry-exhausts-fault` (three markers, failure with the fault's own diagnostic) and `script-retry-recovers-fault` (fault-then-success is SUCCESS with two markers), plus the COMPOSED shell-failure-under-retry pair the verifier demanded sealed rather than traced — `script-retry-sh-exhausts` and `script-retry-sh-recovers`. The original measurements:  RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #53 AND MEASURED TWICE, the second time because the first measurement could not decide the ticket's CLASS and guessing it is what the ranking rule forbids. **(1) THE ATTEMPTS.** `retry(3) { sh 'printf a >> attempts.txt'; echo "${MISSING_VARIABLE}" }`: Jenkins prints `Retrying` and its workspace holds THREE attempt markers; Fogell holds ONE. An interpreter fault — an explicit `throw`, or a MissingProperty read — escapes the hosted body straight to `Interpreter.runHosted`, and `runWithRetry` only advances after `runBody` RETURNS and inspects `attemptCtx.Failed`, so the loop never gets its turn. **(2) THE CLASS.** With a body that faults on attempt 1 and succeeds on attempt 2 — `def n = 0; retry(3) { n = n + 1; …; if (n == 1) echo "${MISSING}" }` — Jenkins reports **SUCCESS** and Fogell reports **FAILURE**. So this is a FALSE FAILURE (class B): Fogell fails a build Jenkins passes, which is the conservative direction but exactly the guarantee `retry` is bought for. A retry that cannot recover from the faults it exists to absorb is not a retry, which is also the sentence FG-178(d) used about the mutation half of this same step. FIX DIRECTION: convert a CATCHABLE body fault into an attempt failure, and re-raise it on the final attempt so an uncaught fault still fails the build with its own diagnostic rather than a generic exhausted-retries one. THE HARD PART IS 'CATCHABLE', and it is shared with FG-176: a refusal (Fogell cannot model this) must NOT be retried, while a Groovy exception must — the `script` arm has to tell a propagated fault from a refusal, which is the same distinction FG-176 needs for `try/catch` and the reason these two should land together | 3 probes: attempts 3-vs-1 with `Retrying` on the Jenkins side, and the recovery case jenkins=success against fogell=failure |

| FG-187 | **P1** | **DONE** | A postfix index CONTINUES ACROSS A NEWLINE, so two statements become one expression and the second never runs | FOUND WHILE FIXING FG-179, and it had been masking that ticket's evidence for months. `def a = false` on one line and `[1].each { … }` on the next parses as the single expression `false[1].each { … }`: the postfix index does not stop at the line break, `each` gets a non-list receiver, and the build fails `method 'each' is not modelled by the bounded interpreter`. Groovy ends a statement at a newline when the statement is complete, so both lines run there. MEASURED at the interpreter: identical source with a `;` after the first line gives `fault=None`; without it, the fault above. **IT CAUSED A WRONG DIAGNOSIS THAT TWO OBSERVERS AGREED ON.** Review reported `[1].each { a = true }` as proof that `applyClosure` discards its environment, and I recorded that as FG-179's own evidence. The symptom was real, the mechanism was invented, and it survived because every probe of it was written across newlines — so the confound was present in the evidence for both sides of the argument. Removing one variable from the probe settled it in a minute. **DIRECTION**: a FALSE FAILURE today (Fogell fails a build Jenkins passes), but the same rule can be a FALSE SUCCESS wherever the swallowed line indexes something real — `def xs = [1,2]` then `[0].each { … }` yields `[1,2][0]`, an element rather than a list, with no error at all. That second shape is UNMEASURED and is the reason this is P1 rather than P2. FIX DIRECTION: the postfix `[` loop must not cross a line break; a leading `[` on a fresh line starts a list literal. Same shape as the FG-141 slashy question — one token meaning two things — but WITHOUT its ambiguity, because the line break is exactly the signal Groovy itself uses **FIXED IN THE GRAMMAR.** A postfix index now refuses to begin a line: `notAfterLineBreak` walks back over spaces and tabs and fails if it meets a line break, so a complete statement ends there as it does in Groovy. IT HAS TO LOOK BACKWARDS, because `ws` skips line breaks and a token's trailing `ws` runs before the next token is attempted — by the time the `[` is tried the newline is already consumed and unrecorded. **THAT STATED LIMIT WAS WRONG ABOUT ITS OWN DIRECTION AND IS NOW CLOSED.** The first version walked spaces and tabs only, and recorded that a COMMENT between the break and the `[` would defeat it — described as erring toward the old behaviour, hence safe. The old behaviour in that shape is a SILENT FALSE SUCCESS: `def xs = [1, 2]` then `/* c */ [0].each { … }` swallows the statement, runs neither it nor its block, and reports success. A limitation that quietly drops work is not a narrower fix, it is the defect with a smaller footprint. The scan now steps back over ORDINARY block comments and counts a break INSIDE one, with a same-line control (`xs /* pick */ [1]`) proving it still admits an ordinary index. A comment whose own TEXT contains `/*` still defeats it — **FG-190**, filed rather than patched, because two backward scans have now been wrong about an opener that only forward lexing can establish. Raised by the pre-push verifier on PR #54 | receipt `script-newline-ends-statement`, proven tier 1 and PROVEN TO FAIL against the grammar reverted (jenkins=success/`c3c871b836dd` vs fogell=failure/`9cc0402b0e14`). It covers BOTH shapes — the loud fault and the SILENT one where the swallowed line indexes a real list and yields an element with no error — plus a same-line control so the rule cannot over-refuse ordinary subscripting. **AND THE PROMISED CHECK RAN**: `script-closure-mutates-ordinary` and `script-break-keeps-assignment` had their workaround semicolons REMOVED in this commit and both stay tier 1, which is what proves the confound is gone rather than avoided |

| FG-188 | **P1** | **DONE** | A top-level `def` helper is INVISIBLE inside `script { }`, and it is the corpus's commonest escape construct | MEASURED 2026-08-13. `def greet(v) { return v }` before `pipeline { }`, then `sh "printf got=${greet(7)}"` inside a script block: Jenkins runs it, Fogell fails with an EMPTY workspace. The walker parses only the `script` BODY, so `Ast.definedFunctions` never sees a declaration made outside it and the call is denied as an unknown name. The AST comment on `SFunc` calls this 'the single most common escape construct in the corpus (56 files)', which is what makes an invisible one expensive. **FIX DIRECTION, INVESTIGATED 2026-08-13 AND THE FIRST GUESS WAS WRONG.** I wrote that the top-level statements 'are already parsed by the Declarative parser' and only needed plumbing. They are NOT: `Pipeline` (in `Fogell.Ir/Pipeline.fs`) has no field for them, and `pipelineParser` locates the `pipeline { }` block and discards everything around it. The existing test asserting a 'block found amid surrounding statements' proves only that the surroundings do not BREAK the parse, not that they are kept. SO IT IS THREE CHANGES, not one: a field on `Pipeline` carrying the preamble, the parser capturing it instead of skipping it, and the walker parsing it with `Fogell.Groovy.Parser` and merging the declared functions into the interpreter's scope. That is an IR change, which by this board's own batch rule wants its own PR. **IT ALSO BLOCKS TWO RECEIPTS**: the FG-179 by-value capture fix and the caller-scope isolation fix are both verified only by interpreter probe, because no spelling of either runs on both engines while a top-level `def` is invisible here and a `def` method inside `script { }` is rejected by Jenkins. Landing this converts two probe-verified fixes into receipted ones | interpreter + host probes, both directions **DONE 2026-08-14, and it was the three changes the corrected design predicted.** `Pipeline` gains `Preamble` (raw TEXT, not an AST — the Declarative IR has no business holding a Groovy tree); `pipelineParser` captures it with `withSkippedString` over the very parsers that used to skip it, so the capture cannot drift from the skip; `BranchCtx` carries it to the `script` arm, which parses it and takes the FUNCTIONS only. **A FOURTH CHANGE THE DESIGN MISSED**: `Interpreter.runWith` computed `Defined` from the script body alone, so a caller supplying helpers in `env.Funcs` had them DENIED BY NAME at `Sandbox.admitCall` — the env said the function existed and the sandbox said it did not. Found by the fix not working, which is the cheapest way to find a missing step. A PREAMBLE THAT DOES NOT PARSE IS IGNORED, not fatal: it holds shebangs, annotations and imports this Groovy subset does not model, and refusing a pipeline over an unsupported `@Library` line would reject work Jenkins runs **AND IT INTRODUCED A FALSE SUCCESS THAT REVIEW CAUGHT BEFORE IT SHIPPED.** Making `env.Funcs` names admissible let a LOCAL of the same name be bypassed: `def x = { 'LOCAL' }` then `x()` reached the PREAMBLE's `x`, so Fogell ran different code from Groovy and reported success. REFUSED with a named reason while the local could not be invoked — that was FG-189, and the refusal was the correct behaviour until it landed, since a silent wrong answer is the worse trade. **THE REFUSAL IS RETIRED 2026-08-17 BY FG-195**: a local closure now shadows the helper and is invoked, receipt `script-local-closure-shadows-helper` | receipt `script-toplevel-helper`, tier 1, exercising BOTH a plain call and a helper returning a closure passed to `dir` — **which also gives the FG-179 by-value capture fix its first receipt**, since every spelling that exercised it was blocked behind this ticket or FG-189 |
| FG-189 | **P1** | **DONE** | A closure held in a LOCAL cannot be invoked — neither `f(x)` nor `f.call(x)` | MEASURED 2026-08-13 at the interpreter. `def f = { v -> v }; echo "${f(1)}"` is DENIED with 'not a registered step, a script-defined function, or a pure builtin'; the `.call` spelling is denied as 'not a pure builtin'. `Sandbox.admitCall` decides on the NAME, and a name bound to a `VClosure` is not in any of its three categories — so the sandbox is right about what it knows and the gap is that a closure value never becomes callable. **IT ALSO BLOCKS A RECEIPT**: the FG-179 by-value capture fix cannot be proven by differential, because every spelling Jenkins accepts needs this and the one spelling Fogell can run (a `def` method inside `script`) JENKINS REJECTS. Recorded on that fix rather than left implicit **DONE 2026-08-17 inside FG-195**, where the queue said it folds: both spellings run through one invocation path with one argument-forming rule; receipt `script-closure-call-spelling` proves the pair. The binding-held spelling asymmetry is a named FG-195 residual | 4 interpreter probes; blocks a receipt named in the FG-179 essay |

| FG-201 | **P1** | **DONE** | A `withEnv` binding read through `${env.X}` inside `script { }` resolves to NULL — green build, wrong bytes | MEASURED 2026-08-17 by the FG-196 suite run: case `script-nested-wrappers-env` — sealed PROVEN on its last run — now DIVERGES: jenkins `+ printf a:one` then `+ printf t:prod`, fogell `+ printf a:null` then `+ printf t:null`; BOTH engines report SUCCESS and the workspace hashes differ on both files, which is the ADR 0001 worst class. Reproduced 4 of 4 (three in-suite attempts under the FG-119 retry, one isolation run), and reproduces identically with the FG-196 walker change stashed — measured pre-existing before anything on that branch landed. BISECTED, not attributed: `git bisect run` over 5d6d6cc..main, building and running this one case per step, names first bad commit `94b3a59` — "FG-179: env provenance is a cell's identity, not a global bit", 2026-08-13. That commit's own nine receipts stayed green; whether its branch re-ran THIS case is not established here. The diverged receipt is COMMITTED as the run produced it — the scorecard records 163 proven of 164 expected — rather than left reading PROVEN from a run the code no longer matches **DONE 2026-08-17, same day.** THE MECHANISM, from the fix rather than a guess: the refresh's "ours" check compared against a PER-INVOCATION local, so a wrapper nested inside another (`dir { withEnv { … } }`) found the OUTER wrapper's cell, failed the identity check, took the leave-the-script's-binding-alone arm, and its refresh silently never ran — `${env.A}` interpolated the outer environment's null. The commit had built `JenkinsEnvCells`, the identity set spanning every hosted-installed cell, and used it in the ASSIGNMENT arm but not the REFRESH arm; the fix is membership in that set, and the per-invocation local is deleted. A script's own `def env` still refuses the refresh: its cell is minted by `Env.withVar` and never enters the set. PROVEN: receipt `script-nested-wrappers-env` re-sealed PROVEN, the five neighbouring env-family receipts re-proven, full suite 164 of 164 with no diverged receipt. AND PINNED WHERE CI CAN SEE IT: a new interpreter unit test drives a real overlay stack through nested-then-sibling wrappers at the hosted seam and is MUTATION-PROVEN — it fails on the pre-fix interpreter and passes on the fix — because CI cannot run differentials, which is precisely how this regression survived four days | the case re-seals PROVEN with every FG-178/FG-179 receipt still green, and the suite carries no diverged receipt |
| FG-202 | **P1** | **DONE** | After a nested `withEnv` block EXITS, `${env.X}` retains the inner value where Jenkins restores the outer | MEASURED 2026-08-17 as a probe (the PRE-FIX measurement is preserved at [evidence/20260817T165857Z-fg-202-probe](../evidence/20260817T165857Z-fg-202-probe) — its DIVERGED receipt records what the engine did before the fix, and is the same stem as the now-PROVEN case on purpose: one shape, two dates, two verdicts). `script { dir('s') { withEnv(['A=one']) { sh 'true' }; echo "after:${env.A}" } }` gives jenkins `after:null`, fogell `after:one`, BOTH builds SUCCESS — a wrong value under a green build, reproduced 3 of 3 under the FG-119 retry. CONSTRUCTED BY THE PRE-PUSH VERIFIER while refuting the FG-201 fix, then measured. THE MECHANISM, from the code: the hosted refresh runs at wrapper-body ENTRY only; nothing re-refreshes the cell when the wrapper pops its overlay, so the retained inner snapshot survives into the enclosing body. Before the FG-201 fix this exact shape printed null BY ACCIDENT — the nested refresh never ran at all — so the fix made the inside read correct and exposed the exit read. The shape needs NESTING: an un-nested wrapper's minted binding does not survive its body, so the following read resolves dynamically and stays live **DONE 2026-08-17, same day, in TWO ROUNDS BECAUSE THE VERIFIER REFUSED THE FIRST.** The fix: when a block-taking step's Perform finishes, the call-site's hosted `env` cell is re-refreshed from the host, which by then reports the enclosing overlay again — membership in `JenkinsEnvCells` decides, so a script's own `env` stays untouched. THE FIRST SPELLING REFRESHED ON RETURN ONLY, and the verifier constructed the escape before any receipt could: a body fault swallowed by a script-level `catch` leaves Perform by exception, and execution continues with the stale cell. The refresh moved into a FINALLY, and the constructed shape was then MEASURED and matched: `try { withEnv(['A=one']) { def x = 1 / 0 } } catch (e) { }` then a read — both engines print `after:null`. PROVEN by two receipts, one per exit: `post-exit-env-read` (plain return) and `post-exit-env-read-fault` (fault into catch), so a green suite now witnesses BOTH paths where it could witness neither. The seam unit test pins the pair plus the sibling shape, mutation-proven three ways: return-only spelling fails at the fault read, pre-FG-201 fails at the nested read, the finally spelling passes. Full 166-case acceptance suite green on the final binary | the probe becomes case `post-exit-env-read` sealed PROVEN; every FG-178/FG-179/FG-201 receipt stays green |
| FG-203 | P3 | TODO | `def snap = env` hands out a SNAPSHOT of the map where Jenkins hands out a live object | RAISED BY THE PRE-PUSH VERIFIER on the FG-202 fix, from the code, UNMEASURED — filed at the reviewer's description per the FG-193 precedent, unclassifiable until a probe runs. The construction: an `EVar` read of `env` yields the cell's current `VMap` VALUE, so `withEnv(['A=one']) { snap = env }` then reading `snap.A` after the wrapper exits gives `one` here, where Jenkins' `EnvActionImpl` reference would read the RESTORED environment. Pre-existing FG-179 model boundary — "a value-semantics map standing in for a live host object" until FG-193 (2026-08-17) moved the mechanism without closing the construction: maps are refs now, but each wrapper refresh REPLACES the env cell's map ref rather than mutating it, so a saved alias goes stale at the next wrapper boundary — and a write through the stale alias now refuses loudly instead of mutating a private copy. Not introduced by the FG-202 exit refresh and not moved by it | a probe on both engines; if real, the fix direction is FG-179's environment model, not another refresh site |
| FG-190 | P2 | **PARTIAL** | The FG-187 newline guard mis-pairs a block comment whose own TEXT contains `/*` | RAISED BY THE PRE-PUSH VERIFIER ON PR #54 and measured in the strict `script { }` path as a false failure. A local paired FG-190/192 candidate now lexes comments forward with Groovy's non-nesting boundary, records whether consumed trivia contains a break, and makes that fact live only at the exact trivia-end stream index so later tokens and speculative backtracking cannot observe it stale. | FG-191 | **P1** | **DONE** | Ref cells can build a CYCLIC value graph, and `==` walks it structurally | RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #54. `def make() { def c; c = { c }; return c }` returns a closure whose captured `Env` holds the cell that holds the closure, so `Value -> Env -> ref -> Value` closes a loop. Structural equality on two such values recurses without bound. Before FG-179 locals were copied and the graph could not close. NOT MEASURED YET — it needs a probe that builds the cycle and compares, and the shape is easy to get wrong in a way that looks like a hang rather than a defect, so it is filed at the reviewer's description rather than at a severity I have not established. FIX DIRECTION: compare by cell IDENTITY rather than structurally wherever a `VClosure` is involved, which is also what Groovy does — closures are reference-equal or not equal **A SECOND SITE, PR #54**: `GString.fs`'s binding snapshot folds with an `old <> v` comparison, so two separately created self-referential closures reach structural equality there too. Same root, different caller — which is the argument for fixing it at the equality rather than at each site. **A THIRD CONSTRUCTOR, 2026-08-17**: FG-193 gave `VMap` a ref, so `def m = [:]; m.self = m` now BUILDS a cyclic map — and `toDisplay` walking it is WORSE than the equality shape this row was filed for: display is reachable from `echo "${m}"` with no depth or tick guard, so the recursion is a StackOverflow that kills the process rather than a fault, where Groovy prints `(this Map)` and succeeds. No corpus or case file builds a cycle; the fix wanted here covers maps too, and display needs the same guard as equality **MEASURED 2026-08-17, AND THE TRUTH WAS WORSE AND ELSEWHERE**: the reviewer's exact spelling never recursed (two literals differ in their ASTs, so equality answers false before any walk) — but the same-AST spelling (`make()` twice, compare the results), a cyclic-map comparison, and cyclic-map DISPLAY all KILLED THE PROCESS with a StackOverflow: exit 134, no fault, no receipt, walker dead — presenting as neither a hang nor a wrong answer, the two shapes the row warned probes get wrong. FIXED at the equality, as this row argued: `Value.tryEq` compares closures by IDENTITY (AST node + captured env record, by reference — pinned from three directions), walks maps through their refs with a seen-pair path, and reports a cycle instead of chasing it; `==`, `!=`, switch-case matching, `contains` and GString's changed-binding fold all route through it. A detected cycle raises the fault Groovy's own chase produces (the JVM dies of StackOverflowError and the build FAILS — measured, both engines fail compatibly), with one named corner: our Thrown is caught by `catch (Exception)` where the JVM's Error is not — `catch (Throwable)` agrees on both. Display renders `(this Map)` exactly as AbstractMap.toString does. SIX receipts PROVEN tier-1 — five in one oracle run plus `script-loop-closure-eq`, the false EQUALITY this branch's own probe caught before it shipped (the first identity spelling compared the captured env RECORD, which a loop body assigning through cells never changes, so two loop-minted closures compared true where Groovy says false; every evaluation mints a fresh record now, cells shared, capture intact) — `script-cyclic-map-display`, `script-same-ast-closure-eq`, `script-closure-identity-eq`, `script-cyclic-closure-eq`, `script-cyclic-map-eq` — plus six unit tests pinning the three former process-kills, both identity directions, and the loop near-miss. THREE RESIDUALS, NAMED BY THE VERIFIER AND RECORDED RATHER THAN SILENT: (1) `sort` still compares STRUCTURALLY — unrouted — so a cyclic value inside a sorted list remains a process kill, and the headline above is scoped to the five routed sites; FG-205 owns it, probe first, because Groovy's own sort-of-maps semantics (a catchable cannot-compare, reportedly) are unmeasured. (2) The compatibility claim for cyclic-map comparison carries an ITERATION-ORDER caveat: F# walks keys sorted, LinkedHashMap walks insertion order, so a cyclic pair that also differs in an ordinary key can fault here where Groovy answers false, or answer false here where Groovy dies — constructed, unmeasured. (3) DEPTH without a cycle is still unbounded in both `tryEq` and display — pre-existing, the old structural walk recursed identically — a ~10k-deep acyclic chain within the loop budget can still overflow; the fix class chosen (path tracking) does not bound depth | reviewer's constructions, 2 sites; unmeasured |

UNMEASURED on the oracle, deliberately: the fix's direction depends on what Jenkins does with `sort` over maps at all (reportedly a catchable cannot-compare for acyclic maps too, which would make Fogell's current silent success on THOSE a second divergence in the same arm), so the probe comes first, per the rule every ticket this cycle has obeyed | a probe of `sort` over maps, cyclic and acyclic, on both engines; then the arm routed or refused to match |
| FG-206 | **P2** | **DONE** | An ABSORBED failure's reason journals as the explanation of a later refusal it does not explain | FOUND BY THE FLEET SESSION'S max-effort review of merged PR #97, reproduced end-to-end on both machines before filing — the exact verification order FG-114's own retry blocker taught. The shape: a captured failure inside a script-level `try` is absorbed by `catch`, nothing clears the shared reason ref, and a LATER hosted child failing through a NON-capturing site (`dir("../outside")`'s traversal refusal in the measured repro) journals the absorbed diagnostic — `step-reason Gate 0 script returned exit code 3` durable while the run log's terminal cause is the dir refusal. The per-site overwrite protects only paths that END in a capturing site; roughly thirty non-capturing refusals were reachable as the terminal disposition **DONE 2026-08-18, same day, in TWO ROUNDS BECAUSE THE VERIFIER REFUSED THE FIRST.** The fix is the THIRD site of the FG-114 invariant — only the site that owns the final disposition may explain it: the shared ref is freshened at a hosted child's entry (`Perform`), joining the per-hooked-step and per-retry-attempt freshenings. THE FIRST SPELLING CLEARED UNCONDITIONALLY, and the verifier measured the regression before any lane could: after a refusal HALTS the branch, later calls still ENTER the host to be skipped (FG-182), and each entry wiped the reason the refusal had just written — erasing FG-114's durable reason for any refused call with a successor, a strictly commoner shape than the one being fixed, invisible to every existing pin because each used a refusal as the script's last call. The freshening now fires only when the child will DISPATCH (`halted` guard): an absorbed failure never sets Failed (the FG-176 observing sink absorbs it) so the catch shape still freshens, while a refusal does, so post-halt entries leave its reason standing. Honest ABSENCE replaces the wrong explanation — the terminal site does not capture, so nothing may journal. Proven in THREE directions in the restart lane: the catch-then-refusal shape journals NO reason beside its failed finish; the uncaught control still journals `script returned exit code 3` (an escaping fault dispatches no further child); and a refused call WITH a successor keeps its own reason on the durable record. SUCCESSOR WORK, the three uncaptured terminal sites the same review named — approver-fault, a genuine unstash failure, and the dir refusal all emit+fail without capturing, so post-FG-206 they journal honest ABSENCE where an operator debugging the exact cases FG-114 was built for would want the reason; capturing at those sites is the enhancement, and the fleet session's altitude suggestion (masking by construction at the OnStepReason choke point, rather than per capture site) belongs to whichever ticket does it |

| FG-193 | **P1** | **DONE** | A Groovy map is a REFERENCE object; `VMap` has no identity, so aliases do not see each other's mutations | RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #54 as two findings that are one root, which is why they are one ticket. **(a)** `def local = [:]; def env = local; env.FOO = …` — the FG-179 assignment arm replaces the `env` cell's `VMap` with a NEW map, so `local` still holds the old one. Groovy mutates the shared object and both names see it. **(b)** `def saved = env; def env = saved; env.FOO = …` — rebinding through an alias mints a fresh cell, which is not in the Jenkins-provenance set, so a write to what IS the Jenkins environment is treated as a local map mutation. Provenance by cell identity is right for VARIABLES and cannot answer a question about the identity of the VALUE. **THE SHAPE IS THE ONE FG-179 JUST SOLVED ONE LEVEL DOWN.** Locals needed reference semantics and got ref cells; maps need the same and have an immutable `Map` inside an F# union. Patching the assignment arm to mutate 'the shared object' is not possible while no shared object exists, so this is a VALUE-MODEL change — most likely `VMap of Map<string, Value> ref` — with the same blast radius ref cells had, and it belongs in its own PR. NOT MEASURED: both are reviewer constructions, and the second needs the walker to supply a real Jenkins env, so neither is probed here **MEASURED 2026-08-17, BOTH REAL, BOTH CLASS A, THEN FIXED THE SAME DAY** ([evidence/20260817T233425Z-fg-193-probes](../evidence/20260817T233425Z-fg-193-probes)): (a) jenkins `alias:x` against fogell `alias:null` — and the mechanism was WORSE than the row's prediction: the old assignment arm matched only `env`-named targets, so a write through any other name was silently DROPPED, not even replace-the-copy; (b) needed the shell to see it — `read:${env.FOO}` PROVED misleadingly because both engines echo `bar`, while `sh 'echo shell:$FOO'` showed the aliased write reaching Jenkins' shell and vanishing here. THE FIX IS THE ROW'S OWN SKETCH: `VMap of Map ref` — 20 sites, far below the ref-cell blast radius — with assignment routed by the RECEIVER'S identity: a map in `JenkinsEnvMaps` (the value-level twin of `JenkinsEnvCells`, seeded and refreshed together) routes to the host exactly as the direct spelling does, so (b) now fails LOUDLY by the deliberate FG-178 refusal instead of silently — measured post-fix, `fogell=<absent>` where it had echoed an empty value; any other map mutates THROUGH the shared ref, receipt `script-map-alias-mutation` PROVEN tier-1; a non-map receiver faults CATCHABLY in strict mode — THE FAULT'S CLASS WAS THE VERIFIER'S CATCH: the first spelling refused UNCATCHABLY where Jenkins throws and a script-level catch runs on, measured (`try { s.FOO = 'x' } catch (Exception e)` and the null-receiver spelling both SUCCEED there, and the uncatchable version diverged live: jenkins=success fogell=failure), fixed to the catchable UnknownProperty the old blanket target-eval raised, receipts `script-nonmap-write-caught` and `script-null-write-caught` PROVEN tier-1. The probe also hit a parser gap AND MISATTRIBUTED IT — the verifier refused the first sentence written about it, the parser source in hand, and isolation proved the parser right: the typed `catch (MissingPropertyException e)` PARSES AND RUNS; what fails is a SECOND catch clause (`reserved` at the second `catch`) and a DOTTED type name — the original probe carried two catches and blamed the type, the FG-139 confounded-probe mistake re-run. All three isolation probes and their outputs are in the evidence dir; the two real gaps are FG-015b-family, recorded, unfixed. LISTS REMAIN VALUE-SEMANTIC (the FG-193 root one type over): a list-element write through a dotted receiver now faults catchably where it silently no-opped, and giving lists identity is undone work this row does not claim. The statement-level index spelling stays FG-015b's parse gap. Three unit tests — alias visibility, the catchable fault in both directions, and the hosted seam asserting the aliased write reaches the host | 2 reviewer constructions, unmeasured |

| FG-194 | **P1** | **DONE** | `break` inside a `for-in` was caught PER ITERATION, so the loop ran the body for every remaining element | RAISED IN REVIEW ON PR #54. The handler had `with BreakSignal -> ()`, which resumes the next iteration; `SWhile` one arm below has always set its running flag to false, so this is a transcription slip rather than a design question. **PRE-EXISTING, NOT A REF-CELL REGRESSION**, and worth separating because three findings on this branch WERE regressions of that change: the line predates it. What cells changed is VISIBILITY — an assignment made before the break now survives, so a wrong value reaches a step instead of being discarded with the environment, which is how the reviewer saw it. FIXED with the same shape `SWhile` uses. Probed all three paths, because a break fix that broke `continue` would be a worse trade: break stops (`picked:[a]`), continue skips one (`seen:[b]`), and an unsignalled loop still runs every element (`all:[ab]`) | receipt `script-for-in-break`, tier 1: `picked` holds the FIRST element only, which separates BOTH wrong answers — resuming after the break and treating it as `continue` each leave all three — and `after.txt` proves the loop was left rather than the script abandoned |

| FG-195 | **P1** | **DONE** | CALLABLE RESOLUTION has no signature model, and three defects on one branch came from that | **THREE OCCURRENCES IN ONE BRANCH, EACH A FALSE SUCCESS, EACH PATCHED WITH A REFUSAL.** (a) a LOCAL shadowing a preamble helper: `def x = { 'LOCAL' }` then `x()` ran the helper, where Groovy resolves the local. (b) DUPLICATE helper names: `def pick()` beside `def pick(v)` folded into a map by name, last-wins, so `pick()` ran the one-arg body. (c) ARITY ignored: `List.truncate` on both sides let `def constant(v)` be called as `constant()`, binding nothing and running the body — Jenkins rejects it, having no zero-arg overload. ALL THREE ARE ONE SHAPE: a call resolving to something the language would not pick, because resolution is by NAME and Groovy's is by SIGNATURE. Each is refused today with a named reason, which is honest and is not the answer — a refusal is a promise that the construct is unsupported, and all three are ordinary Groovy that Jenkins runs. WHAT THE REDESIGN NEEDS: a callable is a NAME plus a SIGNATURE; resolution picks by arity among candidates; a local binding shadows a helper; and a closure VALUE is callable (FG-189 folds into this rather than standing alone). MEASURED, all three, before refusing **(d) NAMED ARGUMENTS AND BLOCKS**, found in the round after (c) and in the guard written FOR (c): the arity check counted positionals only, so `def zero() { … }` called as `zero(foo: 1)` passed it and ran. Groovy passes named arguments as a single Map — a rule the interpreter's own builtin path already documents one screen away — and a trailing closure as a final argument, so that is a one-argument call Jenkins rejects. **FOUR OCCURRENCES NOW, AND THE FOURTH WAS INSIDE A PATCH FOR THE THIRD**, which is the clearest possible argument that counting is not resolution **SPEC WRITTEN 2026-08-14**, while the four defects were fresh and grounded in the four code sites touched during FG-188 rather than inferred: [→ detail](tickets/FG-195.md) **DONE 2026-08-17.** `Funcs` carries CANDIDATES; resolution picks by arity through ONE argument-forming rule (a named group is one Map argument, FIRST; a trailing block is one FINAL argument); a local closure shadows a helper and is invocable in both spellings (`f(x)` and `f.call(x)` — FG-189 folded in as the spec said); no match and same-arity ambiguity refuse NAMING the attempted signature. All four measured shapes receipt-PROVEN together — `script-local-closure-shadows-helper`, `script-helper-overload-by-arity`, `script-helper-arity-mismatch`, `script-helper-named-args-rejected` — plus `script-closure-call-spelling` and the pre-existing helper case re-proven; 11 unit tests pin the shapes and the depth-leak fix the verifier's pass added. NAMED RESIDUALS, recorded on the ticket doc rather than silently: the parser erases `{ -> }` so an explicitly zero-parameter closure called WITH an argument runs where Groovy throws; a BINDING-held closure runs via `.call` and is denied via the bare spelling; a VALUE read of a helper name renders where Groovy raises; the step-name-shadowing boundary is stated in code and unmeasured | 4 probes, one per shape, each against plain Groovy semantics |

## Standing risks

1. **Acceptance ≠ compatibility.** 93.9% inherited acceptance against 0 proven
   receipts. FG-002 exists because of this and blocks the release lane.
2. **Interpreter sandbox.** ADR 0002 buys coverage and inherits Jenkins' script
   security problem. FG-013 and FG-072 are the mitigation and they are P0.
3. **Windows is claimed nowhere yet.** The hardware blocker is gone — a
   GitHub-hosted `windows-latest` runner is a real Windows host — but that
   changes only what is *testable*, not what is *proven*. A green Windows CI job
   licenses "Fogell runs on Windows" (FG-038a). Platform **parity** needs Jenkins
   producing receipts on that same runner (FG-038b). The charter must not promise
   parity until 038b has receipts, and a green 038a must never be reported as if
   it were 038b. Note also that Windows is demanded by **zero** of the 228 corpus
   files, so it earns no priority from the compatibility number.
4. **Untrusted corpus execution.** Parsing is safe; running is not. The
   allowlist-by-executed-surface rule is not optional.
5. **Non-durable numbers.** Forge's ~0 ms/step is the non-durable path. Any
   published latency must come from the durable path (FG-024).
